# Maestro Views and the D2 Query Language Implementation Plan

> **For agentic workers:** REQUIRED SUB-SKILL: Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development (recommended) or superpowers:executing-plans to implement this plan task-by-task. Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.

**Goal:** Give a game's agents a saved, parameterised, versioned **query plus layout** — a JSON query document that compiles to bounded, project-filtered SQL over the metamodel, a fixed renderer catalogue that consumes one uniform node/edge envelope, per-view manual coordinates that survive the content moving underneath them, and a staleness report that says which step of a query a renamed type broke.

**Architecture:** One new domain package `internal/views` and one extracted shared package `internal/graph`. A query is a versioned JSON document (`v: 1`) that is **decoded, bounded, type-checked and resolved to ids entirely in Go before a byte of SQL exists**; the compiler then emits a `SELECT`-only statement whose every caller-controlled value is a bind parameter, runs it in a read-only transaction under a statement timeout, and returns one envelope — nodes, edges, stats, truncation flags, staleness diagnostics — whatever the renderer. Four tables (`views`, `view_positions`, `view_refs`, `view_assets`), all scoped by `project_id`, every child-to-parent key composite. Multi-hop steps compile to `WITH RECURSIVE` with a visited-path guard, emitted by `internal/graph` so the analysis sub-project calls the same walk rather than writing a second one.

**Tech Stack:** Go 1.25.7, pgx/v5 (the compiler builds SQL at runtime and therefore runs **outside** sqlc — see "What in the shipped code makes this harder"), sqlc for the saved-view CRUD, Postgres 16 (`jsonb`, `WITH RECURSIVE`, `bytea`, composite foreign keys), the official MCP Go SDK (`github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk` v1.7.0), `internal/paging` for the saved-view listing cursor.

**Prerequisite:** `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-31-core.md`, `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-31-metamodel.md` and `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-09-02-markdown.md` are implemented and landed. This plan is written against **the code that shipped**, not against those plans' code blocks — both earlier plans had blocks that were wrong and had to be regenerated from the landed files. Every identifier, signature, SQL snippet and constant quoted below was read out of `internal/metamodel/`, `internal/markdown/`, `internal/paging/cursor.go`, `internal/db/queries/metamodel.sql`, `internal/db/migrations/0004_metamodel.sql` and `internal/web/` on 2026-09-02.

**Source spec:** `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-02-views-and-query-language-design.md`, roadmap item 4 of `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-31-core-and-metamodel-design.md`.

---

## Before the first task: how to not repeat the last forty

Read this section once. It is not preamble. Forty tasks of Core, metamodel
and markdown produced the same four defects over and over, and each one
below cost at least one review round somebody could have skipped.

### `TEST_DATABASE_URL` must be exported

`internal/testutil`'s `NewPool` **skips** the test when `TEST_DATABASE_URL`
is unset. Over 500 tests in this repository take a pool. With the variable
unset, `go test ./...` prints `ok` for every package and asserts almost
nothing. Before the first task, and in every shell that runs tests:

```bash
export TEST_DATABASE_URL='postgres://postgres:postgres@localhost:5432/postgres?sslmode=disable'
go test ./internal/db/ -run TestMigrationsApply -v
```

Expected: `--- PASS: TestMigrationsApply`, not `--- SKIP`. If it says SKIP,
nothing below this line is being verified, and a task that "passes" has
verified its own comments and nothing else.

### The three defects this project produced over and over

1. **Documentation claiming more than the code does.** Seventeen
   consecutive tasks shipped a doc comment, a tool description, a SQL
   header or a spec sentence that promised behaviour the code did not
   have — including a comment that was **inverted** (it said the opposite
   of what the function did), one that **understated its own coverage**
   (so a later reader re-did work that was already there), and a test
   whose replacement was **weaker than what it replaced**. The rule for
   this sub-project: **a comment that states a guarantee names the test
   that pins it; if no test pins it, the comment says "not tested" or the
   sentence comes out.** Before writing "always", "never", "cannot",
   "is refused" or "is bounded", find the assertion. Before deleting a
   test, diff what the replacement asserts against what the original
   asserted, in the commit message.
2. **Tests that pass for the wrong reason.** Every test in this plan must
   be seen red before it is trusted. The steps say so, one by one:
   *break the code under the test, run it, watch it fail with the message
   you expect, put the code back.* A test that was never red is a
   comment. This matters more here than anywhere so far, because a query
   engine's tests are mostly "the right rows came back", and an empty
   result satisfies an assertion about rows that should not be there.
   **Every negative query test in this plan asserts a non-empty positive
   control in the same test**, so an empty answer cannot pass it.
3. **A fix that closes one path and leaves the same hole one step along.**
   Every fix gets chased into (a) the other call sites in the same
   package, (b) the other surface — MCP has a REST mirror and vice versa
   — and (c) the code blocks of the tasks that have not run yet. When a
   task changes a shared helper, it says which later tasks' blocks move
   with it. **The UTF-8 bound has been missed four times, each one step
   along from the last fix.** A query document is caller text — every
   string in it, at every depth: a type key, a field key, an enum value,
   a param name, a renderer parameter, a set name — and this is exactly
   where that defect repeats if nobody plans for it. Task 2 bounds the
   *document*, not a list of its known string fields, and
   `TestEveryStringInAQueryIsBounded` walks the decoded tree to prove no
   string escaped.

### The one thing nine review rounds missed

Nine rounds of review over the metamodel, and none noticed that **a
relation's field values were write-only** for a whole sub-project:
`relations.upsert` accepted `fields`, the database stored them, and no
read path anywhere on either surface returned them. Every review verified
writing. None asked whether anything could read it back. It was found by
using the product end to end.

**So: every feature this plan adds has a step that reads it back through
the public surface** — through the MCP tool or the REST route a real
caller uses, not through the service and not through a SQL query in the
test. Tasks 6, 9, 11, 12, 13, 14 and 15 each carry an explicit read-back
step, and Task 16 reads the whole thing back at once. The three shapes most at risk here, named so they are not
discovered by a designer:

- **`renderer_params`** is a free jsonb blob written by `views.upsert`.
  It is the exact shape a relation's `fields` had. Task 11's read-back
  step reads it back through `Get`, and Task 15's through `views.get`.
- **`view_positions.pinned`** is written by `views.set_positions` and is
  the flag the whole `mixed` layout mode turns on. Task 13's read-back
  step reads it back through `GetPositions`, and Task 15's through
  `views.get`.
- **An asset's `width`, `height` and `mime`** are computed on upload and
  never sent again by the caller. Task 14's read-back step reads them
  back, and Task 15's does it again through `views.list_assets`.

### The invariants this sub-project inherits, restated in each task

- **Isolation is enforced in SQL, not Go.** Every `:one`/`:many` query in
  `internal/db/queries/views.sql` takes the resolved project id and
  filters on it: `WHERE project_id = $1 AND lower(key) = lower($2)`,
  never `WHERE lower(key) = lower($2)`; `WHERE project_id = $1 AND id =
  $2` for anything addressed by id. A view id in a caller's hand is not
  authority to read it. **In the compiled query this rule reaches into
  the recursive term**: `project_id = $1` appears on every table
  reference including inside the `UNION ALL` arm, because an anchor-only
  filter seeds correctly and then lets a walk leave the project through
  any edge whose far side lives elsewhere. Task 5 pins that with a test
  that deletes the filter and watches a cross-game row appear.
- **Child-to-parent foreign keys are composite**, carrying `project_id`,
  so the database refuses a row whose parent belongs to another game. The
  spec's §5.4 was caught proposing single-column keys and says so itself;
  so were all three 2026-09-02 specs. **The views tables are composite
  from the first migration** — Task 1, not a later fix. A composite key
  needs `UNIQUE (id, project_id)` on the parent, which is why `views` and
  `view_assets` each carry one on top of their primary key.
  `ON DELETE SET NULL` on a composite key **names its column** —
  `SET NULL (updated_by_token_id)` — because a bare one would try to null
  the `NOT NULL` `project_id` and would fail at token-revocation time
  rather than at migration time. Keys to `users` stay single-column:
  users are global.
- **The cursor lives in `internal/paging`.** `paging.Fingerprint`'s
  contract — **project id first, always** — is stated in prose and
  enforced by nothing, so the views listing passes it first *and* pins
  the composition in its own test. Task 11 carries two tests, not one:
  `TestACursorFromAnotherGameIsRefusedByTheViewListing` (behavioural) and
  `TestTheViewListingFingerprintIsProjectIdFirstAndCarriesItsDomain`
  (compositional). **The behavioural test can pass without the project
  id** when another key part — here the renderer filter — discriminates,
  which is exactly how the original defect survived its first test.
- **The wire vocabulary.** Inherited: `not_found`, `version_conflict`,
  `schema_violation`, `invalid_schema`, `invalid_input`,
  `endpoint_type_mismatch`, `in_use`, `retryable`, plus Core's
  `scope_violation`, `forbidden`, `unauthorized`, `bad_request`,
  `request_too_large`. This sub-project adds exactly four, each argued in
  "Open questions settled": `query_invalid`, `renderer_requirements`,
  `limit_exceeded`, `query_stale`. It does **not** add `query_timeout` —
  see the argument there. **Nothing a caller can fix may report
  `internal_error`**, and a query document is nothing but things a caller
  can fix.
- **Caller input is bounded before Postgres sees it**, reported as the
  caller's own argument with a field path: valid UTF-8, no control
  characters, lengths capped, page limits **clamped** to the cap rather
  than folded onto the default, cursors reported as `invalid_input` at
  path `cursor`. For a query document the "field path" is a **JSON
  pointer** (`/traverse/0/where/value`), which is the one shape this
  sub-project adds to `metamodel.FieldError.Path`.
- **An upsert is a compare-and-set in SQL** under a `FOR UPDATE` read
  taken inside the same transaction. A plain read followed by an
  unguarded upsert is a lost update. `updated_at` belongs to the
  `set_updated_at` trigger and to no query.
- **Events publish after commit**, never inside the transaction, and each
  states its `minRole` and `humanOnly` as a decision with an argument
  rather than copying the neighbouring call site. **Only a deferred
  foreign key can distinguish a publish that was moved inside the
  transaction** — a refusal test cannot, because a rolled-back write and
  a refused write look identical from outside. Task 11 installs a
  deferred constraint in its own throwaway database so the commit, and
  only the commit, fails, exactly as
  `TestNoEventIsPublishedWhenTheCommitFails` does in
  `internal/metamodel`.
- **Content routes register through `registerContentRoute`**, which
  applies the editor check to non-GET routes itself.
  `TestEveryContentRouteIsRegisteredAsContent` reads every registered
  pattern containing `{game}` that is not a project-admin route as
  content and fails if it was registered any other way — so a views route
  added with `mux.HandleFunc` fails a test in a package it never touched.
- **Every negative test asserts the specific message and the specific
  path**, not merely that an error occurred.

---

## Open questions settled to write this plan

The spec's §7 carries nine. Seven of them block a task; here is the
answer each task assumes, and the argument.

**O1 — where layout runs, and on what engine. Settled: nowhere in this
sub-project. The server computes no coordinates at all.** This is not a
deferral of the work, it is a decision about the seam: `views.run`
returns nodes, edges and whatever stored positions apply, and the
*picture* is made by the client. `layout_mode` and `layout_seed` are
stored and returned as **contract** — `auto` means "the client lays every
node out and ignores stored positions", `manual` means "the client uses
every stored position and places the rest once", `mixed` means "pinned
nodes are fixed and the client lays the rest out around them" — and the
seed exists so two designers opening one view get the same picture from a
force layout. Which engine draws it (ELK.js, d3-force, something
vendored and smaller) is a sub-project 5 question that needs a spike
against the no-build-step rule, and answering it here would put a
JavaScript dependency into a plan that ships no JavaScript. **What this
plan owes sub-project 5 and delivers: the envelope, the stored positions,
the seed, and `stats` honest enough to tell a slow layout from a slow
query.** Task 13's `layout_mode` is validated, stored, returned, and
described in the tool description as a client contract — and its doc
comment says "no server code reads this value", because a comment
claiming the server honours a mode it never sees is defect 1 in its
purest form.

**O2 — should `field_schema` gain `indexed: true`. Settled: no, and not
in this sub-project.** The spec's own §4.4 already argues the measurement
side: `entities_type_idx` filters first, entity counts per type in a real
game are hundreds to low thousands, and a scan of two thousand jsonb rows
inside a 5-second timeout is not close to a problem. The part the spec
does not say, and which decides it: **`indexed: true` makes a write path
execute DDL.** `types.upsert` would have to `CREATE INDEX` (and
`DROP INDEX` when the flag comes off, and rename when a field key
changes, and clean up when a type is deleted), inside the transaction
that also re-validates every entity of the type, taking a lock on the
whole `entities` table in a database shared by every game on the
instance. That is a new class of failure — index-name collisions across
games, a migration state that lives outside `internal/db/migrations` and
so is invisible to `TestMigrationsApply`, a `CREATE INDEX` that fails and
leaves a type declaring an index that does not exist — bought for a
problem no game has yet. **The escape hatch stays open and gets a
measurement instead:** Task 7 puts `duration_ms` in every result's
`stats` and Task 15's end-to-end step records the number for the worked
example, so when someone argues this again they argue it with data.

**O3 — who may save a view. Settled: running is `viewer`, saving is
`editor`, and there are no private or draft views in v1.** Running is a
GET; `registerContentRoute` applies `requireEditor` to non-GET routes
only, so a viewer's browser renders a view exactly as it renders a
document's reading view (`internal/markdown/events.go` records the same
reasoning for its own gating). Saving, removing, setting positions,
uploading an asset and pointing a view at one are all writes and all get
the editor check for free from the same helper. **Draft views are
refused for v1 with an argument, not deferred vaguely:** a draft is a
second visibility axis over a table that has none, and the thing an agent
actually needs while composing — try a query without leaving an artefact
behind — is `views.run` with an inline query and `views.validate`, both
of which this plan ships. A draft view would be a worse version of an
inline run.

**O4 — paging a `table` view. Settled: no cursor on `views.run` in v1,
and the reason is stated in the tool description rather than hidden.**
A cursor over `views.run` is only meaningful for a nodes-only result: a
page of a graph is not a graph, and half an edge set is a wrong picture
rather than a partial one. So it would be a second execution path with a
second envelope — which is exactly what the spec says is not obviously
right. What ships instead: `max_nodes` truncation, honest and flagged,
with a hard cap of 5000, which is above any table a designer scrolls in
one sitting. **The escape hatch, named so it is not invented twice:**
when this stops being enough it is a separate `views.run_table` tool with
its own cursor and its own row-shaped envelope, not a cursor bolted onto
this one. Task 10's `table` renderer description says so.

**O5 — aggregation (`summarise`). Settled: not here, and not as a stage
of this language.** Counts and grouped counts change the envelope from
nodes-and-edges into something with neither, and the uniform envelope is
the property that lets a saved view swap `graph` for `table` without
rewriting its query. "Quests per zone" is a question for the analysis
sub-project, whose results are already not pictures. Recorded here so the
views sub-project does not grow a second envelope in a later task.

**O7 — background image layers. Settled: one image per view, and the
schema says so.** `views.background_asset_id` is a single nullable
column. A layered map needs a join table, and adding one now for a
plausible ask means shipping and testing a many-to-many nothing uses.
Task 14's migration comment names the change a second layer would need,
so whoever does it does not rediscover it.

**O8 — the two seams the analysis spec assigns to this one. Both
settled, because views lands first.**

- **A view sourced from an analysis: the query language grows no
  `source` key in v1, and the extension point is reserved rather than
  described.** Reserved concretely: Task 2's decoder **refuses unknown
  top-level keys**, so adding `"source"` later is an additive change to a
  document that would previously have been refused — never a
  reinterpretation of one that silently succeeded. And Task 6's compiler
  takes its seed rows through a `SeedSource` interface with exactly one
  implementation (`fromSelectors`), so the analysis engine adds a second
  implementation rather than editing the compiler. The alternative —
  designing `source` now against an analysis engine that does not exist
  — designs it twice.
- **One reachability walk or two: one, and it lives in `internal/graph`,
  which is neither package.** The spec pair is reconcilable but nothing
  said who owned it, and "decide after both are written" is precisely how
  the drift the analysis spec warns about happens. The precedent is
  already in this repository: `internal/paging` was extracted for exactly
  this reason, and its package comment says why a copy is a copy of its
  bugs. So Task 5 creates `internal/graph`, which owns **the SQL
  primitive** — a project-filtered, depth-bounded, path-guarded recursive
  walk over `relations`, emitted as SQL text plus bind arguments — and
  owns nothing else. The **policy** on top is not shared and is not
  pretended to be: this sub-project's policy is "which entities a
  traversal step reached", the analysis engine's is "reachability under
  `all`/`any` gating over relation traits". Two policies, one walk. Task
  5's package comment states that division, and states that
  `internal/analysis` does not exist yet, so it does not claim a second
  caller it cannot point at.

**O9 — staleness designed twice. Settled: `view_refs` only; this
sub-project adds no `design_version` counter.** The two mechanisms answer
different questions — "is this query still valid" against "has anything
changed since this result" — and **this plan caches no results**, so the
second question has no asker here. When the analysis engine adds
`projects.design_version` for its stored route verdicts, views neither
needs nor reads it. Recorded so a later "views cache" does not invent a
third mechanism.

**O6 is already closed** by the spec's own reconciliation note (2026-09-02):
there is no reference field type, an edge field naming another entity is a
soft reference, and §3.3's `text` treatment of `requires_ability` is
permanent. Nothing in this plan validates or follows one, and Task 3's
operator table treats it as the `text` it is.

**One thing the spec proposes that this plan refuses: `query_timeout` as
a wire code.** The spec's §5.5 lists it. It does not ship, and the
argument is `internal/metamodel/service.go`'s own: SQLSTATE `57014`
(`query_canceled`) is already in `retryableSQLStates`, and `IsRetryable`
already maps it to the `retryable` wire code whose whole meaning is
"change nothing and resend". A statement timeout on a view is either
contention (resend is right) or a query that is too expensive (resend is
wrong) — and the existing code already faced exactly that split and
resolved it by keeping the code and **completing the advice beside it**.
So a timed-out view answers `retryable` with a message that names the
elapsed budget and the three bounds to lower (`max_depth`, `max_nodes`,
the `where` that is not narrowing). A ninth code that means the same
thing as an existing one, with the same recovery, is how a vocabulary
rots. Task 7 states this in the tool description and pins it with
`TestATimedOutQueryIsRetryableAndSaysWhichBoundToLower`.

---

## What in the shipped code makes this sub-project harder than the spec assumes

Six things. Each has a task that deals with it, named here so it is not a
surprise mid-plan.

1. **The compiler cannot use sqlc, and it is the first code in this
   repository that builds SQL at runtime.** Every statement in
   `internal/db/queries/` is static and checked by `make sqlc-check`
   (`sqlc diff`). A query document produces a different statement per
   call: a different number of CTEs, a different number of predicates, a
   different set of bind parameters. So `internal/views/compile.go` calls
   `pool.Query(ctx, sql, args...)` directly and **is not covered by
   `sqlc diff` at all** — the safety `sqlc` was providing (no injection,
   no typo'd column) has to be re-established by construction and by
   test. Task 6 does it three ways: a `builder` type that is the only
   thing that can append to the statement and that **cannot append a
   caller string** (its `bind(any) string` returns `$n` and appends to
   the arg slice — there is no method that takes a caller value as
   text); a golden test asserting the exact SQL text for the worked
   examples; and `TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText`, which
   compiles a query whose every string is a distinctive sentinel and
   asserts none of them appears in the emitted SQL. The saved-view CRUD
   in Task 11 *is* ordinary sqlc and stays in `internal/db/queries/views.sql`.
2. **The traversal that shipped is right for one hop and the wrong shape
   to recurse with.** `ListEntitiesRelatedTo` (`internal/db/queries/metamodel.sql:518`)
   selects the far end of an edge in the **join condition**, with two
   arms:
   ```sql
   JOIN relations r
     ON (sqlc.arg('direction')::text = 'outgoing'
         AND r.source_id = sqlc.arg('anchor_id')::uuid AND r.target_id = e.id)
     OR (sqlc.arg('direction')::text = 'incoming'
         AND r.target_id = sqlc.arg('anchor_id')::uuid AND r.source_id = e.id)
   ```
   Its own comment says the arms are exclusive, and they are — **because
   `direction` is a scalar constant**, so one arm is dead on every row.
   That property is exactly what a recursive walk loses: `direction: any`
   makes both arms live at once, and an edge from a node to itself, or a
   pair of nodes joined in both directions by two rows, then matches
   twice and the walk doubles at every level. Task 5 does not reuse this
   shape. It emits, per step, **one arm per direction**, each computing
   the far endpoint as a scalar expression, unioned — and the `path`
   guard plus `UNION` (not `UNION ALL` at the outer collection point) is
   what makes a node appear once. The plan says which and why in Task 5's
   step 1.
3. **`relations` has no `key` and no `version`.** An edge is addressable
   only as `(relation_type_id, source_id, target_id)`, and
   `UpsertRelation` is the one upsert in the metamodel with **no version
   guard at all**. Consequences this plan lives with rather than fixes: a
   view cannot pin or annotate a *specific* edge across a re-seed that
   deleted and recreated it, so `view_positions` keys on `entity_id`
   only, and there is no `view_edge_positions`; and the envelope's
   `edges[].id` is a real `relations.id` but is **not stable across a
   re-seed**, which Task 6's `Edge` doc comment says in as many words.
4. **`entities.fields` is indexed by `gin (fields jsonb_path_ops)`,
   which serves containment and nothing else** — no ranges, no `>`/`<`,
   not even key existence. Every predicate in this language that is not
   equality is a scan of the type's entities. This is acceptable (O2) and
   is **stated in the compiled query's own comment and in the tool
   description**, not discovered by a designer. Task 7's `stats` is what
   makes it measurable.
5. **`metamodel`'s validation vocabulary is `FieldError{Path, Message}`
   with no JSON tags, aggregated by `*ValidationError{Code, Fields}`,
   and its `Code` is matched by `errors.Is` against three sentinels
   only.** An unrecognised non-empty `Code` matches **no** sentinel and
   surfaces as `internal_error` — which is a trap for four new codes.
   Task 2 therefore does not invent a fifth aggregator: it declares four
   sentinels in `internal/views/errors.go` and a `*QueryError` whose
   `Is` maps its own code onto them, and Task 15 adds four arms to
   `internal/web`'s `mcpErrorFor` and `writeDomainError` **in the same
   commit as the sentinels**, with `TestEveryViewsSentinelHasAWireCode`
   iterating the sentinel list so a fifth one added later cannot be
   forgotten.
6. **`internal/metamodel`'s field-key rule and its row-key rule are two
   different rules, and the query language references both.** A *field*
   key is `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$`, at most 64 bytes (`schema.go`'s
   `keyPattern`). A *row* key — an entity key, an entity-type key, a
   relation-type key — is `^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9_-]*$`, at most 64
   bytes, matched case-insensitively, and is exported for reuse as
   `metamodel.RowKeyProblems(path, key)`. A query document names both,
   often in adjacent positions (`{"type": "quest", "where": {"field":
   "min_level"}}`), and applying the wrong one refuses a legal query or
   accepts an illegal one. Task 2 uses `metamodel.RowKeyProblems` for
   type keys and entity keys, and its own `fieldKeyProblems` — which is
   **not** a third copy, because `@`-sigil built-ins have to be admitted
   before the pattern runs — for field keys.

---

## File structure

| Path | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| `internal/db/migrations/0008_views.sql` | `views`, `view_positions`, `view_refs`, `view_assets`; composite keys, triggers, indexes; the reverse-traversal index on `relations` |
| `internal/db/queries/views.sql` | the saved-view CRUD, positions and assets — every statement filtering on the resolved project id |
| `internal/views/errors.go` | the four new sentinels, `*QueryError` with its JSON pointer, and the shared refusal constructors |
| `internal/views/query.go` | the query document's types, its decoder, its structural bounds and its pointer machinery |
| `internal/views/predicate.go` | the predicate tree, the operator table per `metamodel.FieldType`, and attribute references |
| `internal/views/resolve.go` | keys → ids against one project, params → literals, and the `view_refs` extraction |
| `internal/views/compile.go` | the `builder`, the seed selectors, the depth-1 join steps, predicate → SQL |
| `internal/views/execute.go` | the read-only transaction, the bounds, truncation detection, `stats`, the envelope |
| `internal/views/project.go` | projection: label, `color_by` and friends, the one-hop `related` attribute, `ambiguous` |
| `internal/views/renderers.go` | the renderer catalogue, its parameters and its requirement checks |
| `internal/views/views.go` | `Upsert`, `Get`, `List`, `Remove` for saved views, and `view_refs` maintenance |
| `internal/views/stale.go` | id-versus-key resolution, the diagnostic codes, `on_stale` |
| `internal/views/positions.go` | `SetPositions`, `ClearPositions`, `GetPositions`, the layout mode contract |
| `internal/views/assets.go` | asset upload bounds, image sniffing, storage and `SetBackground` |
| `internal/views/service.go` | `Service`, `Actor`, `withTx`, `publish`, the shared error mappers |
| `internal/views/events.go` | the `view.*` event kinds with their gating, argued |
| `internal/graph/walk.go` | the bounded, project-filtered, path-guarded recursive walk, emitted as SQL + args |
| `internal/web/mcp_views.go` | the `views.*` MCP tools |
| `internal/web/api_views.go` | the REST mirror, the asset upload and the asset serving route |

---

### Task 1: The views schema

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/db/migrations/0008_views.sql`
- Create: `internal/db/views_schema_test.go`

The migration number follows what landed: `0001_identity.sql` …
`0007_documents.sql`. Check with `ls internal/db/migrations` before
writing and use the next free number if another branch landed one first;
nothing else in this task changes.

Four invariants this task exists to put in the database rather than in Go:

- **Every child-to-parent key is composite**, carrying `project_id`, so
  the database refuses a position, a ref or a background whose parent
  belongs to another game. The spec's §5.4 shows single-column keys and
  says itself they "are not yet right".
- **A composite key needs `UNIQUE (id, project_id)` on the parent**,
  which is why `views` and `view_assets` each carry one on top of their
  primary key, exactly as `entity_types`, `entities` and `documents` do.
- **`ON DELETE SET NULL` on a composite key names its column.** A bare
  `SET NULL` would try to null `project_id`, which is `NOT NULL`, and the
  statement would fail at type-deletion time — the exact moment
  `view_refs` exists to survive — rather than at migration time.
- **`view_refs.entity_type_id` and `.relation_type_id` are the one place
  in this repository where `ON DELETE SET NULL` is load-bearing rather
  than incidental.** When a type is deleted the ref row survives with a
  null id and its key text intact, so "which views did that break, and
  where in each query" is one indexed query rather than a scan over every
  stored jsonb document.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the migration**

`internal/db/migrations/0008_views.sql`:

```sql
-- Saved views: a query plus a layout, and the state a layout accumulates.
--
-- Four tables. views is the saved record; view_positions is where a human
-- dragged each node of one view; view_refs is the dependency index that
-- makes "which views did deleting this type break" one query; view_assets
-- holds background images as bytea.
--
-- Every row is scoped by project_id, and every key from one of these
-- tables to a project-scoped parent is composite, carrying project_id
-- alongside the parent id, so the database -- not Go -- refuses a
-- cross-game row. A composite key needs a UNIQUE (id, project_id) on the
-- parent to reference, which is why views and view_assets each carry one
-- on top of their primary key, exactly as 0004_metamodel.sql's types and
-- entities and 0007_documents.sql's documents do. Keys to users stay
-- single-column: users are global, not project-scoped.
--
-- What is pinned by a test, and where: views_schema_test.go asserts each
-- cross-game refusal with its SQLSTATE, that revoking a token nulls only
-- the token column, that deleting an entity type nulls only
-- view_refs.entity_type_id and leaves ref_key and pointer intact, that
-- deleting an entity drops its positions and leaves the view, that
-- deleting a view takes its positions and refs, that deleting an asset
-- nulls the background of every view pointing at it, the case-insensitive
-- key, the updated_at triggers and the non-finite coordinate refusals.
-- Comments below that describe Go behaviour (which renderer names are
-- legal, what layout_mode means to a client) are marked as such and are
-- pinned in internal/views, not here.
-- +goose Up

-- Background images live in Postgres rather than on disk or in object
-- storage: a world map is one file of a few megabytes, and adding a
-- storage backend to a single-binary deployment for that is
-- disproportionate. It also means pg_dump backups already cover them,
-- which a filesystem directory would not.
--
-- The mime CHECK is a closed list on purpose and SVG is not in it: an SVG
-- served inline is a script-execution vector, and a raster map is what a
-- designer has anyway. The 8 MB per-asset bound is applied in Go
-- (internal/views/assets.go) rather than here, because a bytea length
-- CHECK refuses the row only after Postgres has already received and
-- decoded every byte, which is the cost the bound exists to avoid.
--
-- Assets are per-project and reusable across views, not owned by one
-- view: one world map backs a room graph and a fast-travel map alike.
CREATE TABLE view_assets (
    id         uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    project_id uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES projects (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    filename   text NOT NULL,
    mime       text NOT NULL CHECK (mime IN ('image/png', 'image/jpeg', 'image/webp')),
    width      integer NOT NULL CHECK (width > 0),
    height     integer NOT NULL CHECK (height > 0),
    bytes      bytea NOT NULL,
    created_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    created_by_user_id  uuid REFERENCES users (id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
    created_by_token_id uuid,
    FOREIGN KEY (created_by_token_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES api_tokens (id, project_id) ON DELETE SET NULL (created_by_token_id),
    -- The target of the composite key from views.background_asset_id.
    UNIQUE (id, project_id)
);
-- The listing's own sort order, project-leading, so a page can be sought
-- to rather than read whole and sorted. It also gives the projects delete
-- cascade an index to work from.
CREATE INDEX view_assets_project_idx ON view_assets (project_id, created_at, id);

CREATE TABLE views (
    id          uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    project_id  uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES projects (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    key         text NOT NULL,
    name        text NOT NULL,
    description text NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
    -- The query document as the agent wrote it, v and all. Stored as
    -- authored: a rename never rewrites it (the views plan, Task 12), so
    -- what is read back is what was written.
    query           jsonb NOT NULL,
    -- No CHECK on renderer, deliberately, and the asymmetry with
    -- layout_mode below is the decision rather than an oversight. The
    -- renderer catalogue is Go's (internal/views/renderers.go) and is
    -- expected to grow; a CHECK would put the catalogue in two places and
    -- make adding a renderer a migration. layout_mode's three values are
    -- a closed contract that will not grow, and the database is the right
    -- place for a closed contract.
    renderer        text NOT NULL,
    renderer_params jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'::jsonb,
    -- What layout_mode means is a contract with the *client*: no server
    -- code reads this value beyond validating and returning it. auto: the
    -- client lays every node out and ignores stored positions. manual:
    -- every node with a stored position uses it and the rest are placed
    -- once. mixed: pinned nodes are fixed and the rest are laid out
    -- around them.
    layout_mode text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'mixed'
                CHECK (layout_mode IN ('auto', 'manual', 'mixed')),
    -- Seeds the client's force layout so two designers opening one view
    -- see the same picture. An unseeded force layout draws a different
    -- diagram every load, which destroys the one thing a saved view is
    -- for: being recognisable.
    layout_seed integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
    background_asset_id uuid,
    background_scale    double precision NOT NULL DEFAULT 1 CHECK (background_scale > 0),
    background_offset   jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{"x":0,"y":0}'::jsonb,
    version    integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
    created_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    updated_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    updated_by_user_id  uuid REFERENCES users (id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
    updated_by_token_id uuid,
    FOREIGN KEY (updated_by_token_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES api_tokens (id, project_id) ON DELETE SET NULL (updated_by_token_id),
    -- Composite, so a view cannot point at another game's image. The SET
    -- NULL names its column: project_id is NOT NULL and must survive the
    -- asset's deletion untouched. One image per view, deliberately (the
    -- views plan, open question O7); a second overlay layer needs a join
    -- table (view_id, asset_id, z, scale, offset) and this column would
    -- then be dropped in that migration, not widened.
    FOREIGN KEY (background_asset_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES view_assets (id, project_id) ON DELETE SET NULL (background_asset_id),
    -- The target of the composite keys from view_positions and view_refs.
    UNIQUE (id, project_id)
);
-- Keys are the stable handle an agent re-seeds against, matched
-- case-insensitively so a second run with different casing collides with
-- the existing row instead of creating a twin. Same rule as
-- entity_types_key_key and documents' path key.
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX views_key_key ON views (project_id, lower(key));
-- The listing's keyset, in its own sort order.
CREATE INDEX views_project_idx ON views (project_id, name, id);
CREATE TRIGGER views_set_updated_at
    BEFORE UPDATE ON views
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION set_updated_at();

-- Where a human put each node of one view.
--
-- Per view, not per entity: the same zone sits at its real map
-- coordinates in a World map view and wherever the algorithm put it in a
-- Mage route view.
--
-- Positions are a cache of a human's arrangement and never a claim that
-- anything exists. Deleting an entity cascades its rows away and nothing
-- else happens; an entity that appears has no row, is laid out
-- automatically and joins the picture unpinned. Re-running a query never
-- rewrites positions, which is what lets a query be edited without losing
-- an afternoon of map work.
--
-- There is no view_edge_positions and no per-edge annotation, because
-- relations carry no key and no version (0004_metamodel.sql): an edge is
-- addressable only as (relation_type_id, source_id, target_id), so a
-- re-seed that deleted and recreated it would strand any row keyed on its
-- id. Nodes have keys; edges do not.
CREATE TABLE view_positions (
    view_id    uuid NOT NULL,
    entity_id  uuid NOT NULL,
    project_id uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES projects (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    -- The CHECKs reject NaN and both infinities. In Postgres NaN compares
    -- greater than every other double, so `x < 'Infinity'` is false for
    -- it and the conjunction refuses all three values with one clause.
    -- internal/views/positions.go refuses them first, with a field path;
    -- this is the backstop for a write path that does not go through it.
    x double precision NOT NULL
      CHECK (x > '-Infinity'::double precision AND x < 'Infinity'::double precision),
    y double precision NOT NULL
      CHECK (y > '-Infinity'::double precision AND y < 'Infinity'::double precision),
    pinned     boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT true,
    updated_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    PRIMARY KEY (view_id, entity_id),
    FOREIGN KEY (view_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES views (id, project_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    FOREIGN KEY (entity_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES entities (id, project_id) ON DELETE CASCADE
);
-- The reverse lookup and the entities delete cascade's index. The primary
-- key already serves every read that starts from a view.
CREATE INDEX view_positions_entity_idx ON view_positions (project_id, entity_id);
CREATE TRIGGER view_positions_set_updated_at
    BEFORE UPDATE ON view_positions
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION set_updated_at();

-- The dependency index: one row per type reference in a view's query,
-- written on every upsert.
--
-- ON DELETE SET NULL rather than CASCADE is the whole point of this
-- table. When a type is deleted the row survives with a null id and its
-- key text intact, so "which views did that break, and where in each
-- query" is one indexed query rather than a scan that parses every stored
-- jsonb document. Resolution at execution time is by id first (a rename
-- does not change an id, so a renamed type still resolves), then by key
-- (a type deleted and re-created under the same key resolves, which is
-- the common shape of a designer fixing a mistake), then dead.
--
-- pointer is the JSON pointer into the query where the reference sits
-- ("/traverse/0/via"), which is what lets a staleness report name the
-- step that broke instead of the view that broke.
CREATE TABLE view_refs (
    view_id          uuid NOT NULL,
    project_id       uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES projects (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    kind             text NOT NULL CHECK (kind IN ('entity_type', 'relation_type')),
    ref_key          text NOT NULL,
    entity_type_id   uuid,
    relation_type_id uuid,
    pointer          text NOT NULL,
    FOREIGN KEY (view_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES views (id, project_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    FOREIGN KEY (entity_type_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES entity_types (id, project_id) ON DELETE SET NULL (entity_type_id),
    FOREIGN KEY (relation_type_id, project_id)
        REFERENCES relation_types (id, project_id) ON DELETE SET NULL (relation_type_id),
    -- kind is not decoration: it says which of the two id columns this row
    -- uses, and the CHECK stops a row claiming one kind and carrying the
    -- other's id. A null id is legal on both arms -- that is what a
    -- deleted type looks like.
    CHECK ((kind = 'entity_type' AND relation_type_id IS NULL)
        OR (kind = 'relation_type' AND entity_type_id IS NULL))
);
-- One ref per pointer per view: a pointer addresses exactly one position
-- in one document, so a second row for it would be a rewrite that did not
-- delete the first. The upsert deletes this view's refs and reinserts
-- them in one transaction, and this index is what makes that a refusal
-- rather than a silent duplicate if it ever stops doing so.
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX view_refs_key ON view_refs (view_id, pointer);
-- "Which views did deleting this type break": the two lookups the SET
-- NULL above exists to serve, each project-leading.
CREATE INDEX view_refs_entity_type_idx ON view_refs (project_id, entity_type_id);
CREATE INDEX view_refs_relation_type_idx ON view_refs (project_id, relation_type_id);

-- Reverse traversal by relation type, for the recursive walk in
-- internal/graph. relations_edge_key (relation_type_id, source_id,
-- target_id) already serves a forward walk; nothing served a backward
-- one, so an `in` walk over a game with one dominant relation type
-- filtered after the fact. Reverse walks are not an edge case: the
-- worked example "quests available to a Mage" walks `available_to`
-- inwards.
--
-- The metamodel plan's Task 1, correction 6, deliberately did not add
-- this index -- "an index nothing reads is pure write cost ... it belongs
-- to the task that introduces the query it serves" -- and named this
-- sub-project as the reason it was considered. This is that task.
--
-- 2026-09-02-analysis-engine-design.md §11 asks for a *different* index,
-- relations (project_id, relation_type_id), for a different access shape;
-- neither replaces the other. Whoever adds that one checks first: with
-- both, relations carries six indexes on the table expected to hold the
-- most rows, and every index is write cost on every seeded edge.
CREATE INDEX relations_type_target_idx ON relations (relation_type_id, target_id);

-- +goose Down
-- Children first: view_refs and view_positions both key into views, and
-- views keys into view_assets. Dropping a table takes its constraints,
-- indexes and triggers with it; set_updated_at() belongs to 0001, whose
-- Down drops it.
DROP INDEX relations_type_target_idx;
DROP TABLE view_refs;
DROP TABLE view_positions;
DROP TABLE views;
DROP TABLE view_assets;
```

```bash
export TEST_DATABASE_URL='postgres://postgres:postgres@localhost:5432/postgres?sslmode=disable'
go test ./internal/db/ -run TestMigrationsApply -v
```

Expected: `--- PASS: TestMigrationsApply`. If it says `--- SKIP`, stop and
export `TEST_DATABASE_URL`. If goose refuses the `SET NULL (column)`
syntax, the server is older than Postgres 15; this repository targets 16.

- [ ] **Step 3: Write the schema test**

`internal/db/views_schema_test.go`. It follows
`internal/db/documents_schema_test.go`'s shape: a throwaway database per
test from `testutil.NewPool`, raw SQL, SQLSTATE assertions.

```go
package db_test

import (
	"context"
	"errors"
	"testing"

	"github.com/google/uuid"
	"github.com/jackc/pgx/v5/pgconn"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/testutil"
)

// seedTwoGames makes two projects, each with one entity type, one entity,
// one relation type and one view, and returns the ids of both games'
// rows. Every cross-game test below writes a row of game A pointing at a
// parent of game B and asserts the database refuses it.
type gameFixture struct {
	projectID      uuid.UUID
	entityTypeID   uuid.UUID
	entityID       uuid.UUID
	relationTypeID uuid.UUID
	viewID         uuid.UUID
	assetID        uuid.UUID
}

func seedGame(t *testing.T, ctx context.Context, pool *pgxpool.Pool, slug string) gameFixture {
	t.Helper()
	var g gameFixture
	err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO projects (slug, name) VALUES ($1, $1) RETURNING id`, slug).Scan(&g.projectID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert project: %v", err)
	}
	err = pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO entity_types (project_id, key, label, label_plural)
		 VALUES ($1, 'quest', 'Quest', 'Quests') RETURNING id`, g.projectID).Scan(&g.entityTypeID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert entity type: %v", err)
	}
	err = pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO entities (project_id, entity_type_id, key, name)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, 'hogger', 'Wanted: Hogger') RETURNING id`,
		g.projectID, g.entityTypeID).Scan(&g.entityID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert entity: %v", err)
	}
	err = pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO relation_types (project_id, key, label)
		 VALUES ($1, 'requires', 'Requires') RETURNING id`, g.projectID).Scan(&g.relationTypeID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert relation type: %v", err)
	}
	err = pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO view_assets (project_id, filename, mime, width, height, bytes)
		 VALUES ($1, 'map.png', 'image/png', 4, 4, '\x00'::bytea) RETURNING id`,
		g.projectID).Scan(&g.assetID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert asset: %v", err)
	}
	err = pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO views (project_id, key, name, query, renderer)
		 VALUES ($1, 'quest-map', 'Quest map', '{"v":1}'::jsonb, 'graph') RETURNING id`,
		g.projectID).Scan(&g.viewID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert view: %v", err)
	}
	return g
}

// sqlState returns the SQLSTATE of err, or "" if it is not a Postgres
// error. Asserting the state rather than the message is what keeps these
// tests from passing on an unrelated failure.
func sqlState(err error) string {
	var pgErr *pgconn.PgError
	if errors.As(err, &pgErr) {
		return pgErr.Code
	}
	return ""
}

func TestAViewPositionCannotCrossGames(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	a := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-a")
	b := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-b")

	// Positive control first: game A's own entity is accepted, so an
	// empty answer below cannot be mistaken for a refusal.
	_, err := pool.Exec(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO view_positions (view_id, entity_id, project_id, x, y)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, $3, 1, 2)`, a.viewID, a.entityID, a.projectID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("a position within one game must be accepted: %v", err)
	}

	_, err = pool.Exec(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO view_positions (view_id, entity_id, project_id, x, y)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, $3, 1, 2)`, a.viewID, b.entityID, a.projectID)
	if got := sqlState(err); got != "23503" {
		t.Fatalf("a position naming another game's entity must be refused with 23503, got %q (err %v)", got, err)
	}
}

func TestAViewCannotUseAnotherGamesBackground(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	a := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-a")
	b := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-b")

	_, err := pool.Exec(ctx,
		`UPDATE views SET background_asset_id = $1 WHERE id = $2`, a.assetID, a.viewID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("a background from the same game must be accepted: %v", err)
	}

	_, err = pool.Exec(ctx,
		`UPDATE views SET background_asset_id = $1 WHERE id = $2`, b.assetID, a.viewID)
	if got := sqlState(err); got != "23503" {
		t.Fatalf("a background from another game must be refused with 23503, got %q (err %v)", got, err)
	}
}

// TestDeletingAnEntityTypeNullsTheRefAndKeepsItsKey is the one this whole
// table exists for: a deleted type must leave a readable dead reference,
// not disappear.
func TestDeletingAnEntityTypeNullsTheRefAndKeepsItsKey(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	a := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-a")

	_, err := pool.Exec(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO view_refs (view_id, project_id, kind, ref_key, entity_type_id, pointer)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, 'entity_type', 'quest', $3, '/from/0/type')`,
		a.viewID, a.projectID, a.entityTypeID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert ref: %v", err)
	}
	// The entity has to go first: entities key into entity_types with
	// ON DELETE RESTRICT, which is 0004_metamodel.sql's own decision.
	if _, err := pool.Exec(ctx, `DELETE FROM entities WHERE id = $1`, a.entityID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("delete entity: %v", err)
	}
	if _, err := pool.Exec(ctx, `DELETE FROM entity_types WHERE id = $1`, a.entityTypeID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("deleting a type that a view references must be allowed: %v", err)
	}

	var (
		typeID    *uuid.UUID
		refKey    string
		pointer   string
		projectID uuid.UUID
	)
	err = pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`SELECT entity_type_id, ref_key, pointer, project_id FROM view_refs WHERE view_id = $1`,
		a.viewID).Scan(&typeID, &refKey, &pointer, &projectID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("the ref row must survive its type: %v", err)
	}
	if typeID != nil {
		t.Fatalf("entity_type_id must be null after the type is deleted, got %v", *typeID)
	}
	if refKey != "quest" || pointer != "/from/0/type" {
		t.Fatalf("the key and the pointer must survive: got %q at %q", refKey, pointer)
	}
	if projectID != a.projectID {
		t.Fatalf("project_id must be untouched by the SET NULL, got %v want %v", projectID, a.projectID)
	}
}

func TestNonFiniteCoordinatesAreRefused(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	a := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-a")

	for _, value := range []string{"NaN", "Infinity", "-Infinity"} {
		_, err := pool.Exec(ctx,
			`INSERT INTO view_positions (view_id, entity_id, project_id, x, y)
			 VALUES ($1, $2, $3, $4::double precision, 0)
			 ON CONFLICT (view_id, entity_id) DO UPDATE SET x = excluded.x`,
			a.viewID, a.entityID, a.projectID, value)
		if got := sqlState(err); got != "23514" {
			t.Fatalf("x = %s must be refused by the CHECK with 23514, got %q (err %v)", value, got, err)
		}
	}
}

func TestARefCannotClaimOneKindAndCarryTheOthersID(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	a := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-a")

	_, err := pool.Exec(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO view_refs (view_id, project_id, kind, ref_key, relation_type_id, pointer)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, 'relation_type', 'requires', $3, '/traverse/0/via')`,
		a.viewID, a.projectID, a.relationTypeID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("a well-formed relation_type ref must be accepted: %v", err)
	}

	_, err = pool.Exec(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO view_refs (view_id, project_id, kind, ref_key, entity_type_id, pointer)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, 'relation_type', 'requires', $3, '/traverse/1/via')`,
		a.viewID, a.projectID, a.entityTypeID)
	if got := sqlState(err); got != "23514" {
		t.Fatalf("kind relation_type carrying an entity_type_id must be refused with 23514, got %q (err %v)", got, err)
	}
}

func TestRevokingATokenNullsOnlyTheTokenColumnOfAView(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	a := seedGame(t, ctx, pool, "game-a")

	var userID uuid.UUID
	if err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash) VALUES ('v@example.com', 'x') RETURNING id`).
		Scan(&userID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert user: %v", err)
	}
	var tokenID uuid.UUID
	if err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`INSERT INTO api_tokens (project_id, created_by_user_id, name, token_hash)
		 VALUES ($1, $2, 'seed', 'h') RETURNING id`, a.projectID, userID).Scan(&tokenID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("insert token: %v", err)
	}
	if _, err := pool.Exec(ctx,
		`UPDATE views SET updated_by_token_id = $1, updated_by_user_id = $2 WHERE id = $3`,
		tokenID, userID, a.viewID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("stamp the audit columns: %v", err)
	}
	if _, err := pool.Exec(ctx, `DELETE FROM api_tokens WHERE id = $1`, tokenID); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("revoking a token must not fail: %v", err)
	}

	var (
		gotToken   *uuid.UUID
		gotUser    *uuid.UUID
		gotProject uuid.UUID
	)
	if err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		`SELECT updated_by_token_id, updated_by_user_id, project_id FROM views WHERE id = $1`,
		a.viewID).Scan(&gotToken, &gotUser, &gotProject); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("read the view back: %v", err)
	}
	if gotToken != nil {
		t.Fatalf("updated_by_token_id must be null, got %v", *gotToken)
	}
	if gotUser == nil || *gotUser != userID {
		t.Fatalf("updated_by_user_id must be untouched, got %v", gotUser)
	}
	if gotProject != a.projectID {
		t.Fatalf("project_id must be untouched by the SET NULL, got %v", gotProject)
	}
}
```

**Corrections from a review pass against commit `6241c24`** (twenty-one
adversarial isolation cases including both statement orderings of a
two-update move, nothing deferrable, and the index measurement
reproduced to the digit on its two most falsifiable numbers all held;
the schema itself was right. Five findings, all documentation or
coverage, closed here.)

1. **The comment warning about the index count miscounted the indexes
   it exists to warn about.** The paragraph above
   `relations_type_target_idx` said "with both, `relations` carries six
   indexes"; verified live, this migration alone already brings
   `relations` to six (0004_metamodel.sql's four plus this one), and
   adding the analysis engine's `(project_id, relation_type_id)` makes
   **seven**. The comment now states both counts — six from this
   migration, seven with the analysis engine's added — instead of
   naming only the number it was trying to warn against and getting it
   wrong by one.

2. **A note on unindexed `SET NULL` paths read as exhaustive when it
   named one of five.** The comment above `views.background_asset_id`
   said only that no index leads with that column, true, but left four
   more equally unindexed `SET NULL` paths in this same migration
   unmentioned: `views.updated_by_token_id`, `views.updated_by_user_id`,
   `view_assets.created_by_token_id`, `view_assets.created_by_user_id`
   — each a sequential scan on token revocation or user deletion, same
   as the background case. This matches `0004` and `0007`, which index
   no audit column either, so it is repo convention rather than a new
   defect; the comment now says "no audit or background key here has a
   leading index" and names all five, and also states the useful
   asymmetry: every **CASCADE** path in this migration does have a
   usable index.

3. **Two things nothing pinned.** `relations_type_target_idx` — the
   most-argued line in this migration — was defended only by a comment;
   a future `DROP INDEX` would regress silently, and no migration index
   anywhere in `internal/db` is asserted by a test, so this needed a
   deliberate decision rather than a default. Given how much argument
   and measurement this one specific index carries, it is now pinned:
   `TestRelationsTypeTargetIndexExists` (`internal/db/views_schema_test.go`)
   asserts its presence via `pg_indexes`, and the migration comment
   records that it is the one exception to the repo's usual
   unpinned-index convention. `view_positions.pinned NOT NULL DEFAULT
   true` was likewise stated only in this file's header prose, though
   Task 13's layout contract reads it; it is now pinned by
   `TestAViewPositionDefaultsToPinned` and by a comment on the column
   itself.

4. **The weakest number in the comment.** The write-cost claim read
   "634 ms median with the index against 617 ms without", inside the
   noise of the workload it was measured over — a number this project
   has now corrected four times before. Re-measured on this project's
   own test Postgres 16: a single `COPY` of 20,000 relations into a
   freshly seeded 20,000-entity project, **five runs each way**: 432.9
   ms median with the index (422.3–456.3 ms) against 412.2 ms median
   without (405.4–415.5 ms). Unlike the original figures, these ranges
   do not overlap, and the medians differ by about 5% — a small but
   real insert cost, which the comment now states plainly rather than
   calling it noise. The conclusion is unchanged: the read win measured
   above is worth this insert cost. The measurement script was a
   throwaway `_test.go` in `internal/db`, run manually and deleted; it is
   not part of the committed suite, the same way the original figures'
   own measurement script was not.

5. **Recorded for Tasks 11 and 12, not fixed here — nothing in this
   migration needs to change for either fact.**
   - `view_refs` has no primary key. It is addressed throughout by
     `(view_id, pointer)`, enforced by the unique index
     `view_refs_key`, not by a `PRIMARY KEY` clause. Task 11's queries
     and Task 12's staleness reads should key off that pair, not assume
     a surrogate id exists to join or return.
   - **A dead reference is indistinguishable from one that never
     resolved.** Both are a `kind`, a `ref_key` and a null id column —
     `view_refs` records no separate "this used to resolve and no
     longer does" bit. Task 12's staleness diagnostics either live with
     that (report both as "does not resolve") or add what distinguishes
     them deliberately; this migration does not decide it for them.
   - The good news Task 12 depends on: a ref row **survives** the
     deletion of what it points at, with its `kind`, `ref_key`,
     `pointer` and `project_id` intact and only its own id arm (
     `entity_type_id` or `relation_type_id`) nulled —
     `TestDeletingAnEntityTypeNullsTheRefAndKeepsItsKey` and
     `TestDeletingARelationTypeNullsOnlyItsOwnRefColumn` pin exactly
     this. And the post-deletion lookup ("which views did deleting this
     type break") is served by an index, `view_refs_entity_type_idx` or
     `view_refs_relation_type_idx`, rather than a scan — both are
     `(project_id, <type>_id)`, project-leading, matching the SET
     NULL's own query shape.

---

### Task 2: The query document, its refusals, and the bound that has been missed four times

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/errors.go`
- Create: `internal/views/query.go`
- Create: `internal/views/query_test.go`
- Create: `internal/views/text_test.go`

No database in this task. Everything here is a pure function over a
`[]byte`, which is what makes it cheap to test exhaustively — and this is
the task where the query language stops being able to hurt anything
downstream.

**The invariant this task carries, restated:** *caller input is bounded
before Postgres sees it, and reported as the caller's own argument with a
field path.* Here the field path is a **JSON pointer**. And the specific
failure this task exists to prevent: **the UTF-8 bound has been missed
four times in this project, each fix one step along from the last.** A
query document is caller text at every depth — a type key, a set name, a
field key, an operator, an enum value inside `value`, a param name, a
`label_from` — so this task does **not** enumerate the string fields it
knows about. It **walks the decoded document by reflection** and bounds
every string it finds, including strings inside `any` values and inside
maps. Enumeration is how the last four were missed.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test for the sentinels and the wire codes**

`internal/views/errors_test.go`:

```go
package views

import (
	"errors"
	"testing"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
)

// TestEachSentinelMatchesOnlyItsOwnCode pins that a QueryError answers
// errors.Is for its own sentinel and for no other. The metamodel's
// ValidationError has exactly this shape and exactly this trap: a code
// that is set but unrecognised matches no sentinel at all and surfaces as
// internal_error, which is the one thing a caller-fixable failure must
// never be.
func TestEachSentinelMatchesOnlyItsOwnCode(t *testing.T) {
	cases := map[string]error{
		CodeQueryInvalid:         ErrQueryInvalid,
		CodeRendererRequirements: ErrRendererRequirements,
		CodeLimitExceeded:        ErrLimitExceeded,
		CodeQueryStale:           ErrQueryStale,
	}
	for code, own := range cases {
		err := &QueryError{Code: code}
		if !errors.Is(err, own) {
			t.Fatalf("a QueryError with code %q must match its own sentinel", code)
		}
		for otherCode, other := range cases {
			if otherCode == code {
				continue
			}
			if errors.Is(err, other) {
				t.Fatalf("a QueryError with code %q must not match %q's sentinel", code, otherCode)
			}
		}
		if errors.Is(err, metamodel.ErrInvalidInput) {
			t.Fatalf("a QueryError with code %q must not match invalid_input", code)
		}
	}
}

// TestAnUnknownCodeMatchesNothingRatherThanDefaulting is the trap named
// above, asserted directly. Defaulting an unrecognised code onto the
// most-taught sentinel would tell a caller to fix the wrong thing.
func TestAnUnknownCodeMatchesNothingRatherThanDefaulting(t *testing.T) {
	err := &QueryError{Code: "query_slightly_off"}
	for _, sentinel := range []error{ErrQueryInvalid, ErrRendererRequirements,
		ErrLimitExceeded, ErrQueryStale, metamodel.ErrInvalidInput, metamodel.ErrNotFound} {
		if errors.Is(err, sentinel) {
			t.Fatalf("an unrecognised code must match no sentinel, matched %v", sentinel)
		}
	}
}

// TestAQueryErrorNamesItsPointerAndItsProblem pins the message shape a
// caller reads when it does not branch on the code.
func TestAQueryErrorNamesItsPointerAndItsProblem(t *testing.T) {
	err := &QueryError{Code: CodeQueryInvalid, Fields: []metamodel.FieldError{
		{Path: "/traverse/0/via", Message: `no relation type "requires" in this game`},
	}}
	want := `query_invalid: /traverse/0/via: no relation type "requires" in this game`
	if got := err.Error(); got != want {
		t.Fatalf("Error() = %q, want %q", got, want)
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `internal/views/errors.go`**

```go
// Package views is Maestro's saved-view domain: a JSON query document
// that compiles to bounded SQL over the metamodel, a renderer chosen from
// a fixed catalogue, and the coordinates a human dragged.
//
// It depends on internal/metamodel deliberately and acyclically: a query
// names entity types, relation types and their declared fields, so the
// dependency is real, and nothing in the metamodel mentions views. Taking
// it means this package reuses that one's FieldError, ValidationError,
// sentinels, key grammar and text bounds rather than growing a second,
// drifting copy of each — the same reasoning internal/markdown's package
// comment records.
package views

import (
	"errors"
	"fmt"
	"strings"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
)

// The four wire codes this sub-project adds, and their sentinels.
//
// Four, and not five: the spec's §5.5 also proposes `query_timeout`, and
// it deliberately does not ship. SQLSTATE 57014 (query_canceled) is
// already in internal/metamodel's retryableSQLStates and already maps to
// the `retryable` code, whose meaning — change nothing and resend — is
// the right first advice for a statement timeout, and whose message this
// sub-project extends with which bound to lower when resending stops
// helping (execute.go). A code that names the same recovery as an
// existing code is how a vocabulary rots.
//
// Each of the four is a code because each names a *different recovery*:
//
//   - query_invalid — the document is wrong. Fix the pointer this error
//     names and send it again. Every structural and type failure.
//   - renderer_requirements — the document is fine and the renderer
//     cannot consume it. Pick another renderer, or change the query so it
//     produces what this one needs.
//   - limit_exceeded — a declared limit is above its hard cap. Lower it;
//     the cap is in the error.
//   - query_stale — the document names something this game no longer
//     has. Repair the query, or re-run with on_stale: "best_effort".
const (
	CodeQueryInvalid         = "query_invalid"
	CodeRendererRequirements = "renderer_requirements"
	CodeLimitExceeded        = "limit_exceeded"
	CodeQueryStale           = "query_stale"
)

var (
	ErrQueryInvalid         = errors.New(CodeQueryInvalid)
	ErrRendererRequirements = errors.New(CodeRendererRequirements)
	ErrLimitExceeded        = errors.New(CodeLimitExceeded)
	ErrQueryStale           = errors.New(CodeQueryStale)
)

// Sentinels lists every sentinel this package answers with, so
// internal/web's TestEveryViewsSentinelHasAWireCode can iterate it rather
// than repeating a list that a fifth sentinel would silently fall out of.
// The order is the declaration order and carries no meaning.
func Sentinels() []error {
	return []error{ErrQueryInvalid, ErrRendererRequirements, ErrLimitExceeded, ErrQueryStale}
}

// The sentinels this package reuses rather than redeclaring. They are
// aliases of the metamodel's, not new values, so internal/web's existing
// mcpErrorFor and writeDomainError arms catch them with no change.
var (
	ErrNotFound        = metamodel.ErrNotFound
	ErrVersionConflict = metamodel.ErrVersionConflict
	ErrInvalidInput    = metamodel.ErrInvalidInput
	ErrInUse           = metamodel.ErrInUse
)

// QueryError is a refusal of a query document, carrying one or more
// problems, each addressed by a **JSON pointer** into the document rather
// than by a Go field path.
//
// A pointer is the shape this sub-project adds to
// metamodel.FieldError.Path. It is the right address here for the reason
// the spec's §2.1 gives: an agent that gets `/traverse/0/where/value`
// knows exactly which of five traversal steps to re-write, and no amount
// of prose gets it there as reliably. internal/web's fieldDetails
// publishes it as details.fields[].path with no change — a pointer is a
// string like any other path.
//
// Is maps Code onto exactly one sentinel and defaults nothing: an
// unrecognised code matches nothing, exactly as metamodel.ValidationError
// behaves, and TestAnUnknownCodeMatchesNothingRatherThanDefaulting pins
// it.
type QueryError struct {
	Code   string
	Fields []metamodel.FieldError
}

func (e *QueryError) Error() string {
	parts := make([]string, 0, len(e.Fields))
	for _, f := range e.Fields {
		parts = append(parts, f.Error())
	}
	if len(parts) == 0 {
		return e.Code
	}
	return fmt.Sprintf("%s: %s", e.Code, strings.Join(parts, "; "))
}

func (e *QueryError) Is(target error) bool {
	switch e.Code {
	case CodeQueryInvalid:
		return target == ErrQueryInvalid
	case CodeRendererRequirements:
		return target == ErrRendererRequirements
	case CodeLimitExceeded:
		return target == ErrLimitExceeded
	case CodeQueryStale:
		return target == ErrQueryStale
	default:
		return false
	}
}

// invalidQuery is the one constructor every structural and type refusal
// in this package goes through, so a future refusal cannot reach a caller
// as a bare error and therefore as internal_error — the failure mode
// metamodel.searchQueryProblem's own comment records.
func invalidQuery(pointer, message string) error {
	return &QueryError{Code: CodeQueryInvalid, Fields: []metamodel.FieldError{
		{Path: pointer, Message: message},
	}}
}

// invalidQueryProblems is invalidQuery for a pass that found several
// problems at once, so an agent fixes all of them in one round trip
// instead of discovering them one call apart. Validation is a whole-document
// pass for exactly this reason.
func invalidQueryProblems(problems []metamodel.FieldError) error {
	return &QueryError{Code: CodeQueryInvalid, Fields: problems}
}

// pointer builds an RFC 6901 JSON pointer from path parts. Ints are
// array indices; strings are member names and are escaped, because a
// member name is only ever one of this package's own constants today but
// a `fields` map key is a game's own and may hold a slash.
func pointer(parts ...any) string {
	var b strings.Builder
	for _, p := range parts {
		b.WriteByte('/')
		switch v := p.(type) {
		case int:
			fmt.Fprintf(&b, "%d", v)
		case string:
			b.WriteString(strings.ReplaceAll(strings.ReplaceAll(v, "~", "~0"), "/", "~1"))
		default:
			panic(fmt.Sprintf("views: a pointer part must be an int or a string, got %T", p))
		}
	}
	return b.String()
}
```

- [ ] **Step 6: Write the failing test for the document and its bounds**

`internal/views/query_test.go`:

```go
package views

import (
	"errors"
	"strings"
	"testing"
)

// parseFails is the assertion every negative case below uses: the parse
// is refused, with query_invalid, at exactly this pointer, and the
// message says this. Asserting the pointer as well as the code is what
// makes these tests useful to the agent that reads the error.
func parseFails(t *testing.T, doc, wantPointer, wantMessageContains string) {
	t.Helper()
	_, err := ParseQuery([]byte(doc))
	if err == nil {
		t.Fatalf("this document must be refused: %s", doc)
	}
	if !errors.Is(err, ErrQueryInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("must be query_invalid, got %v", err)
	}
	var qe *QueryError
	if !errors.As(err, &qe) {
		t.Fatalf("must be a *QueryError, got %T", err)
	}
	for _, f := range qe.Fields {
		if f.Path == wantPointer && strings.Contains(f.Message, wantMessageContains) {
			return
		}
	}
	t.Fatalf("no problem at %q containing %q; got %v", wantPointer, wantMessageContains, qe.Fields)
}

// TestTheSimplestUsefulQueryParses is the positive control every negative
// test below leans on. Without it, a ParseQuery that refused everything
// would pass this whole file.
func TestTheSimplestUsefulQueryParses(t *testing.T) {
	q, err := ParseQuery([]byte(`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}`))
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("must parse: %v", err)
	}
	if len(q.From) != 1 || q.From[0].Type != "quest" {
		t.Fatalf("from did not survive the parse: %+v", q.From)
	}
	// `as` defaults to the type key, and the default is applied at parse
	// time rather than at compile time so that every later stage sees one
	// spelling of the set name.
	if q.From[0].As != "quest" {
		t.Fatalf("as must default to the type key, got %q", q.From[0].As)
	}
}

func TestAQueryMustDeclareItsVersion(t *testing.T) {
	parseFails(t, `{"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}`, "/v", "must be 1")
	parseFails(t, `{"v":2,"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}`, "/v", "must be 1")
}

// TestAnUnknownKeyIsRefusedRatherThanIgnored is load-bearing beyond
// tidiness: it is what reserves the extension point for the analysis
// seam (this plan's open question O8). A document with a "source" key is
// refused today, so adding "source" later is additive rather than a
// reinterpretation of a document that used to succeed silently.
func TestAnUnknownKeyIsRefusedRatherThanIgnored(t *testing.T) {
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest"}],"source":{"analysis":"unreachable"}}`,
		"", `unknown key "source"`)
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","tyep":"typo"}]}`, "", `unknown key "tyep"`)
}

func TestAQueryMustSeedFromSomething(t *testing.T) {
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[]}`, "/from",
		"must name at least one entity type to start from")
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1}`, "/from",
		"must name at least one entity type to start from")
}

func TestATypeKeyObeysTheRowKeyGrammar(t *testing.T) {
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"has space"}]}`, "/from/0/type",
		"must be letters, digits, underscores or hyphens")
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":""}]}`, "/from/0/type", "is required")
}

// TestASetNameIsDeclaredOnceAndReferencedByName pins the two halves of
// the naming rule. There is deliberately no implicit "previous step":
// the spec's §2.2 argues that an implicit chain makes a two-branch query
// impossible to read.
func TestASetNameIsDeclaredOnceAndReferencedByName(t *testing.T) {
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"},{"type":"zone","as":"q"}]}`,
		"/from/1/as", `the set name "q" is already used`)
	parseFails(t,
		`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],`+
			`"traverse":[{"from":"nope","via":"requires","as":"r"}]}`,
		"/traverse/0/from", `no set named "nope" is declared before this step`)
	parseFails(t,
		`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],`+
			`"traverse":[{"from":"r","via":"requires","as":"r"}]}`,
		"/traverse/0/from", `no set named "r" is declared before this step`)
}

func TestADirectionIsOneOfThree(t *testing.T) {
	parseFails(t,
		`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],`+
			`"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"requires","direction":"sideways","as":"r"}]}`,
		"/traverse/0/direction", `must be "out", "in" or "any"`)
}

// TestDepthTakesBothSpellings pins that a bare integer and a {min,max}
// object mean the same thing, and that the object's halves are checked
// against each other.
func TestDepthTakesBothSpellings(t *testing.T) {
	q, err := ParseQuery([]byte(`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],` +
		`"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"requires","depth":3,"as":"r"}]}`))
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("a scalar depth must parse: %v", err)
	}
	if q.Traverse[0].Depth.Min != 1 || q.Traverse[0].Depth.Max != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("depth 3 must mean {min:1,max:3}, got %+v", q.Traverse[0].Depth)
	}
	q, err = ParseQuery([]byte(`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],` +
		`"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"requires","depth":{"min":2,"max":4},"as":"r"}]}`))
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("an object depth must parse: %v", err)
	}
	if q.Traverse[0].Depth.Min != 2 || q.Traverse[0].Depth.Max != 4 {
		t.Fatalf("got %+v", q.Traverse[0].Depth)
	}
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],`+
		`"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"requires","depth":{"min":5,"max":2},"as":"r"}]}`,
		"/traverse/0/depth", "min 5 is above max 2, so no depth is legal")
	parseFails(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],`+
		`"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"requires","depth":0,"as":"r"}]}`,
		"/traverse/0/depth", "must be at least 1")
}

// TestTheStepCapIsRefusedAtParseTime is the CTE bound from the spec's
// §4.3 table: eight steps per query, refused rather than truncated,
// because an agent that asked for nine should learn it cannot have them.
func TestTheStepCapIsRefusedAtParseTime(t *testing.T) {
	var b strings.Builder
	b.WriteString(`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"s0"}],"traverse":[`)
	for i := 0; i < MaxSteps+1; i++ {
		if i > 0 {
			b.WriteString(",")
		}
		b.WriteString(`{"from":"s0","via":"requires","as":"s` + string(rune('a'+i)) + `"}`)
	}
	b.WriteString(`]}`)
	parseFails(t, b.String(), "/traverse", "at most 8 traversal steps")
}

func TestAnOversizeDocumentIsRefusedBeforeItIsParsed(t *testing.T) {
	doc := `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"` + strings.Repeat("a", MaxQueryBytes) + `"}]}`
	parseFails(t, doc, "", "is too large")
}
```
