- [ ] **Step 5: See each of the three load-bearing lines red**

1. Delete `AND r.project_id = $1` from the join in the arm loop.
   Expected: `TestTheProjectFilterIsInBothTermsOfTheRecursion` fails with
   `the recursive term does not filter on the project`.
2. Delete `AND NOT %[2]s = ANY(w.path)`. Expected:
   `TestAWalkTerminatesOnACycle` **hangs**, and then fails on the test
   binary's timeout. Run it with `-timeout 20s` so the red arrives
   quickly, and record in the commit message that the failure mode is a
   hang rather than a wrong answer — which is the whole argument for the
   guard being a correctness requirement.
3. Change `directionsOf(Any)` to return `[]Direction{Out}` and note that
   `TestDirectionAnyDoesNotDoubleASymmetricEdge` **still passes** — one
   arm cannot double. Then change the emitter to the shipped one-hop
   shape (one arm, a two-armed `OR` on `(near = w.id)`) and watch it fail

- [ ] **Step 6: Commit**

```bash
git add internal/graph/walk.go internal/graph/walk_test.go
git commit -m "feat: one bounded, project-filtered, cycle-guarded graph walk in internal/graph"
```

**Corrections from implementing Task 5** (landed as `a1bda17`, `74b8f1c`,
`1bdabd3` and the direction guard on top of them; every mutation named
below was applied to the shipped code, watched fail the test that claims
it, and restored, with the baseline confirmed green — and confirmed
*running*, `-v`, since this package's tests skip silently without
`TEST_DATABASE_URL`). Eight findings. Three of them change Task 8's code
block.

1. **The emitter this task specifies does not run.** "One arm per
   direction, `any` is the `UNION ALL` of the two arms" is illegal SQL:
   Postgres refuses a recursive term whose self-reference appears more
   than once, and with three `UNION ALL` branches it reports SQLSTATE
   42P19, *recursive reference to query "w" must not appear within its
   non-recursive term* — the first two branches become the non-recursive
   term. Reproduced against this project's own Postgres before anything
   was written. What ships is **one** arm whose near end matches either
   column and whose far end is the scalar `CASE` of whichever matched:
   `(r.source_id = w.id OR r.target_id = w.id)` with
   `CASE WHEN r.source_id = w.id THEN r.target_id ELSE r.source_id END`.
   One arm cannot traverse one edge twice from one node, which is the
   property the two-arm shape was reaching for.

2. **The doubling is real, was reproduced, and no test in this package
   can see it.** Written the legal way that gets the two-arm rows — a
   two-armed edge relation feeding one self-reference — one self-loop at
   max depth 3 with the path guard removed returns **1, 2, 4 and 8 rows**
   at depths 0 to 3, against **1, 1, 1, 1** for the one-arm `CASE`. That
   is the plan's "doubles at every level", measured. But *with* the guard
   the two shapes return identical rows, because the second copy of a
   self-loop is excluded by the same path test as the first, and a
   self-loop is the only edge that can match from both ends at once. So
   the arm count is pinned by nothing, and `TestASelfLoopIsNotTraversed`
   says so in its own comment rather than implying it pins the shape. The
   measurement lives in `WalkCTE`'s doc comment, which is where the next
   reader tempted by the two-arm form will be.

3. **The plan's `TestDirectionAnyDoesNotDoubleASymmetricEdge` asserted
   the wrong number, and does not ship.** A pair joined by an edge in each direction is **two edges**,
   not one hop counted twice; the plan's `want 1` is a walk that
   deduplicates by node, and a walk that deduplicates by node hands a
   renderer one of the two edges and no way to learn about the other. One
   row is one edge traversal, stated on `WalkCTE` and asserted by
   `TestDirectionAnyTraversesEachEdgeOnceFromEachNode`: two rows, one
   distinct node, two distinct `via_relation` values. Collapsing rows to
   nodes is the caller's job and loses nothing; the opposite is not true.

4. **The visited-path guard does not own termination, and the plan's
   step 5.2 predicted a hang that does not happen.** The depth bound
   terminates the walk on its own, so deleting the guard leaves the
   plan's `TestAWalkTerminatesOnACycle` — termination plus the node set —
   *green*, which is this project's second standing defect in its purest
   form: the test would have been a comment. What the guard changes is
   how many times a cycle is walked, and that is what is asserted:
   `TestAWalkOverACycleReturnsEachNodeOnce` pins **3 rows**, against 11
   without the guard for the same three-node cycle at max depth 10, and
   against 2047 for that cycle under `direction: any` — the branching
   factor raised to the depth bound. Only the row count is red without
   the guard. The test is renamed accordingly, and `WalkCTE`'s comment no
   longer claims the walk "does not terminate" without it.

5. **The project filter has two positions in the recursive term, not
   one, and the behavioural test the plan called impossible is
   possible.** Task 1's invariant says Task 5 "pins that with a test that
   deletes the filter and watches a cross-game row appear"; Task 5's own
   body says the behavioural version "cannot fail while the composite
   foreign keys hold". Both are half right. The filter sits on the
   relation (`r.project_id = $1`, which stops a walk *through* another
   game's edge) **and** on the entity at the far end of it
   (`far.project_id = $1`, which stops a walk *into* another game's
   entity) — two holes one step apart, and a fix to either leaves the
   other open. `TestAWalkCannotLeaveItsProjectThroughARogueEdge` forges
   one row for each, in its own throwaway database, after dropping
   `relations`' three composite foreign keys there; both mutations are
   red with a message naming which of the two filters was lost, and the
   legitimate edge is asserted in the same test so an empty answer cannot
   pass. `TestTheProjectFilterIsInBothTermsOfTheRecursion` keeps the text
   assertion and additionally **counts** the occurrences in the recursive
   term, so removing one of the two is red there too.

6. **`MinDepth` was an input the emitter ignored.** The plan's `Walk`
   declares it and its comment hands it to the caller — the same shape as
   `ResolvedSet.EntityIDs`, which Task 4's correction 3 removed for being
   declared and unfillable. Here the field is genuinely useful, so it is
   *honoured* instead: the package always emits a second, non-recursive
   CTE, `<name>_out`, which applies `depth >= $min` and the row cap, and
   exports **`ReadFrom(w)`** as the name a caller reads from.
   `TestMinDepthDropsTheNearHopsAfterWalkingThem` pins both halves — the
   near hops are gone, the far ones are still there, which they could not
   be if the bound had pruned the recursion. **`CappedName` does not
   ship**; `ReadFrom` replaces it.

7. **`MaxRows`'s comment claimed a bound it does not have.** `LIMIT` is
   not allowed in a recursive term, so the cap is a `LIMIT` on the
   wrapper: it bounds the rows a caller reads, not the work Postgres
   does. The field comment now says exactly that, says that nothing here
   measures whether the demand-driven recursion stops early under it, and
   says that which rows survive the cap is unspecified — there is no
   `ORDER BY` — so a caller at the cap has a truncated answer and must
   report it as one. `TestMaxRowsCapsWhatComesBack` pins the cap with an
   uncapped control in the same test.

8. **An unknown `Direction` was walked as `out`.** The plan's switch
   defaults, so a zero-value `Direction` — the one a caller reaches by
   forgetting the field — silently answered an outgoing walk. It panics
   now, beside the CTE-name panic and for the same reason: the wrong
   answer with no error is the class this repository keeps producing.
   `TestAnUnknownDirectionPanics` covers the zero value and carries the
   three known spellings as its control. **`TestACTENameThatIsNotAnIdentifierPanics`
   moved with it**: written as the plan has it, with no direction, it
   would panic on the direction and stay green with the name guard gone.

**Four tests beyond the block**, each closing something the block stated
and nothing held: `TestDirectionInWalksTheOtherWay` (`In` and `Out` share
an emitter, and a swapped pair of columns answers every chain test
correctly and every inward question backwards),
`TestAWalkWithNoRelationTypesReachesOnlyItsSeed` (an empty type list
follows no edge, which is the opposite of what dropping the `ANY` clause
would do), `TestASeedsOwnBindsAreRenumbered` (the unit half of the
renumbering Task 8 depends on, asserting the shifted text and the
argument count) and `TestAnUnknownDirectionPanics` above.

**Recorded for Task 8**, verified rather than assumed:

- Its block reads `graph.CappedName`; the name is `graph.ReadFrom`, and
  the CTE it names already applies `MinDepth`, so **the outer min-depth
  filter that block describes must not be written** — and the step 3
  instruction to "break `MinDepth`'s outer filter" is now the mutation
  `minDepth := bind(0)` in `internal/graph`, red on
  `TestMinDepthDropsTheNearHopsAfterWalkingThem`.
- Its `TestACycleInContentIsDrawnRatherThanHung` must not rest on a hang:
  the depth bound already stops the walk, so the views-level cycle test
  has to assert the node and edge counts, as the graph-level one does.
- `Walk.EdgePredicate`/`EdgeArgs` are **not** in this task's API. They go
  in with the `edge_where` that needs them.
- The direction spellings match on both sides —
  `internal/views`'s `DirectionOut`/`In`/`Any` are `"out"`, `"in"`,
  `"any"`, which is what `graph.Direction` accepts — so
  `graph.Direction(step.Step.Direction)` is a safe conversion for a
  document that has been through `ParseQuery`, and panics for one that
  has not.
- The statement timeout named in this sub-project's bounds is **Task
  7's**, on the transaction in `internal/views`; nothing about it belongs
  to `internal/graph`, which emits statement text and runs nothing.

**Second round of review corrections on Task 5** (landed as `0e5642e`; a
review of `a1bda17`..`c18db26`, the first of nineteen tasks where no
quantitative figure in the shipped comments was over-claimed — every
number in `WalkCTE`'s comment reproduced exactly, and the findings are
about what the code *does*). Six findings. Every mutation named below was
applied to the shipped code, run with `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported and
`-v` confirming zero `SKIP`, and restored.

9. **The walk could never hand a renderer the edge that closes a cycle,
   and now it does. This is the sentence the later tasks need to read.**
   `NOT (far) = ANY(w.path)` sat in the recursive term's `WHERE`, so it
   suppressed the **row**, not just the recursion: a three-node cycle came
   back with three of its edges' worth of nodes and only *two* of its
   three edges, a self-loop came back with nothing at all, and a symmetric
   pair walked `out` lost its return edge. That contradicted three things
   at once — this walk's own argument for one-row-per-edge-traversal ("a
   renderer cannot draw an edge it was never handed"), the core spec's
   premise that a cycle is legal content deliberately allowed so it can be
   surfaced as a design error, and the analysis engine's whole reason for
   being the second caller. `TestASelfLoopIsNotTraversed` asserted the
   absence as correct; it does not ship.

   What ships is the reviewer's shape: the CTE carries a sixth column,
   `closed`, computed in the recursive term as "the far node is already
   on the path"; the row is **emitted regardless**, and the recursive term
   carries `AND NOT w.closed`, so the closing hop is returned exactly once
   and is never expanded from. Termination is unchanged and now has two
   independent reasons: every path the recursion carries forward is
   simple, and the depth bound still prunes.

   **What a walk returns, walked `out` from `a`, stated so eleven tasks
   can read it:**
   a self-loop `a -> a` returns one row — `a` at depth 1, `closed`, over
   the loop's own relation; a two-cycle `a -> b -> a` returns two —
   `b` at depth 1 open, then `a` at depth 2 `closed` over the return edge,
   the edge nobody could be handed before; a three-cycle
   `a -> b -> c -> a` returns four at min depth 0 — three distinct nodes,
   three distinct relations, the fourth row being `a` again at depth 3,
   `closed`, over `c -> a`. `TestASelfLoopIsReturnedOnceAndNotExpanded`,
   `TestATwoCycleReturnsItsReturnEdge` and
   `TestAWalkOverACycleReturnsEachNodeOnceAndTheClosingEdgeWithIt` pin all
   three, the last asserting the **edge** set beside the node set, which is
   the assertion the old shape would have failed.

   **This supersedes correction 4's "3 rows" and its test name.** The
   three-node cycle is 4 rows now, against 11 with no guard at all; both
   counts were re-measured on this project's Postgres, and the
   suppress-the-row mutation is red on four tests.

   **One line came in with it, and it is load-bearing only under `any`:**
   `r.id IS DISTINCT FROM w.via_relation`, which stops a walk
   re-traversing the relation it just arrived by. The old path guard hid
   the need for it, because a backtrack always lands on the previous node
   and was therefore suppressed. Now that a closing hop is *returned*,
   without this line every single edge in the graph would report itself as
   a two-cycle, and an analysis engine hunting prerequisite cycles would
   find one everywhere. Under `out` and `in` it can never fire. Removing
   it is red on the self-loop and `any` tests.

10. **The arm-count mutant is writable now, and it is written.**
    Correction 2 recorded that with the closing row suppressed, the
    one-arm and two-arm emitters return identical rows and **no test in
    this package could tell them apart**. Returning the row is what makes
    them observable: a self-loop is exactly the edge a two-armed shape
    matches from both ends, and it is now a row rather than nothing, so
    the second copy is a second row. The mutant — a two-armed edge
    relation (`source AS near, target AS far` `UNION ALL`
    `target AS near, source AS far`) feeding one self-reference, legal
    SQL, `out` and `in` untouched — returns **3 rows over 2 relations**
    where the shipped emitter returns 2 over 2, and
    `TestASelfLoopIsReturnedOnceAndNotExpanded` is the only test that goes
    red on it. That test now carries the assertion correction 2 said it
    could not.

11. **The accumulating path was untested; a cycle that excludes the seed
    is the input that tests it.** Mutating `w.path || (far)` to `w.path`,
    so the path never grows past the seed, **survived all thirteen
    tests**: every cycle in every fixture passed through the seed, so a
    guard that only remembers the seed answered them all identically.
    This is the standing "test that passes for the wrong reason" defect
    one step past the correction that already caught it once in this task.
    `TestACycleThatExcludesTheSeedIsGuardedByTheWholePath` seeds
    `a -> b -> c -> d -> b` and walks from `a`: 5 rows shipped, 11 for the
    mutant, which is now the only test red under it.

12. **The anchor is the third project-filter position, and only the text
    test saw it.** Deleting `AND anchor.project_id = $1` killed nothing
    behavioural, because every behavioural fixture's own seed already
    filtered. That matters precisely because `SeedSQL` is documented as
    the caller's own SQL and this package explicitly does not control
    whether it filters — so the anchor join is the only thing between a
    project-blind seed and another game's entity.
    `TestTheAnchorFiltersOnTheProjectEvenWhenTheSeedDoesNot` seeds with
    `WHERE key = $1` across two games and asserts this game's two entities
    and neither of theirs, with the positive control in the same
    assertion. It passed on shipped code — the filter worked, it was
    simply unpinned. `TestTheProjectFilterIsInBothTermsOfTheRecursion`
    additionally counts the anchor's occurrence, so both halves are red
    under the deletion.

13. **Truncation is detectable now, and a truncated answer is the near
    hops.** `LIMIT MaxRows` made exactly-at-cap and truncated-at-cap the
    same answer while `MaxRows`'s own comment told a caller that hitting
    the cap meant a truncated answer it must report. The wrapper emits
    **`LIMIT MaxRows + 1`**, the same `cap + 1` mechanism Task 7's
    truncation flags are built on — and it belongs here because the
    truncation happens here. The wrapper also carries **`ORDER BY depth`**
    before the `LIMIT`, so a truncated walk is a connected prefix of
    nearest hops rather than a scatter of nodes whose own edges were
    dropped. This supersedes correction 7's "which rows survive the cap is
    unspecified".
    `TestMaxRowsReturnsOneRowPastTheCapSoTruncationIsDetectable` pins
    cap+1 with both an at-cap and an uncapped control;
    `TestATruncatedWalkIsOrderedByDepth` pins the prefix behaviourally
    **and the ordering as text**, and says which half pins what: Postgres
    evaluates a recursive CTE breadth-first, so the behavioural half is
    green with no `ORDER BY` at all and is only the positive control; the
    text assertion is what makes the ordering a contract rather than an
    executor detail, and it is the half that is red.

14. **Two smaller ones.** Negative and inverted bounds were accepted
    silently — a negative row cap meant *no* cap, a negative depth meant
    seed-only, a minimum above the maximum meant nothing at all. Three
    empty-or-wrong answers with no error, which is the class this package
    already panics on twice; `WalkCTE` panics on all four shapes and
    `TestANegativeOrInvertedBoundPanics` pins them with a control for the
    legal degenerate bounds (`min 0 max 0`, no cap). And **relation types
    are the one thing the walk trusts another table for**: there is no
    join to `relation_types`, so there is no position to filter, and
    safety rests entirely on
    `relations_relation_type_id_project_id_fkey` — verified against
    `0004_metamodel.sql`, and the reason
    `TestAWalkCannotLeaveItsProjectThroughARogueEdge` has to drop that
    constraint before it can forge a row. That is one sentence on the
    `RelationTypeIDs` field, not a join.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running (`-v`,
zero `SKIP`) before each: suppress the closing row again (4 tests);
`w.path || far` → `w.path` (1); no guard at all (5); anchor loses its
project filter (2); `LIMIT cap` instead of `cap + 1` (2); wrapper loses
`ORDER BY depth` (1); the two-armed emitter (1); no non-backtracking
guard (2); bounds accepted silently (1); relation loses its project
filter (2); far entity loses its project filter (2); `MinDepth` ignored
(6). Twelve mutations, twelve red, none survived.

**What this changes for the tasks that have not run yet:**

- **The CTE has a sixth column, `closed`.** Task 8 reads
  `ReadFrom(w)`; a `SELECT *` picks it up, and a step's projection has to
  decide what it means. The recommendation, not yet a decision: a closing
  row is a real edge and belongs in `edges`, and its node is already in
  `nodes` by construction, so the natural handling is to emit the edge and
  not re-emit the node.
- **`MaxRows` returns up to `MaxRows + 1` rows.** Task 8 sets
  `MaxRows: r.Limits.MaxNodes * 4`; that walk now returns up to
  `4*MaxNodes + 1`, and the extra row is the signal — Task 7's truncation
  flags should read it rather than re-deriving truncation downstream. A
  walk silently truncated at the graph level arrives *under* the node
  limit and would otherwise be reported to the designer as complete.
- **Task 8's `TestACycleInContentIsDrawnRatherThanHung` must assert
  `n` edges for an `n`-cycle, not `n-1`.** The earlier note in this
  section told it to assert node *and* edge counts; written against the
  old walk it would have asserted `n-1` and frozen the defect into the
  contract. An `n`-cycle now yields `n` nodes and `n` edges.
- **A step with `direction: "any"` returns closing rows for genuine
  cycles only**, not for every edge, because of the non-backtracking
  guard. A views-level test that counts rows under `any` should expect
  the two-cycle shape described in finding 9, not the pre-fix counts.

---

### Task 6: The compiler and the first result — a depth-1 query, run and read back

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/compile.go`
- Create: `internal/views/execute.go`
- Create: `internal/views/compile_test.go`
- Create: `internal/views/execute_test.go`

This is where the language becomes SQL. It is also the first code in this
repository that builds SQL at runtime, so the safety `sqlc diff` was
providing has to be re-established by construction.

**Three mechanisms, and each has a test:**

1. **A `builder` is the only thing that can append to a statement, and it
   has no method that takes a caller value as text.** `bind(v any) string`
   appends to the argument slice and returns `$n`; `write(literal string)`
   takes a Go string literal the compiler itself wrote. There is no
   `writef` with a `%s` a value could reach.
2. **A golden test** on the emitted SQL for the spec's worked examples, so
   a change to the emitter is a diff a reviewer reads rather than a
   behaviour they infer.
3. **`TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText`**, which compiles a
   query whose every string is a distinctive sentinel and asserts that
   none of them appears anywhere in the emitted SQL.

**The invariant this task carries:** *isolation is enforced in SQL, not
Go* — every `FROM entities` and every `JOIN relations` in the emitted
statement carries `project_id = $1`, and `TestEveryTableReferenceIsProjectFiltered`
parses the emitted SQL for table references and fails on one without a
filter, so a new clause added in a later task cannot forget.

**And the read-back rule:** this task's step 2 runs the compiled query
through `Service.Run` and asserts the envelope contains the entity that
was seeded — not that a row count matched, but that a named quest with its
key, its name, its type and its set came back.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing compiler tests**

`internal/views/compile_test.go`:

```go
package views

import (
	"regexp"
	"strings"
	"testing"
)

func compileOf(t *testing.T, g *game, doc string) (string, []any) {
	t.Helper()
	r, err := g.views.Resolve(t.Context(), g.projectID, mustParse(t, doc))
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("resolve: %v", err)
	}
	sql, args, err := Compile(r, g.projectID)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("compile: %v", err)
	}
	return sql, args
}

// TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText is the injection question,
// answered by construction rather than by care. Every string the caller
// controls is a distinctive sentinel; none may appear in the SQL.
func TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	// The sentinels have to be legal for the document to resolve, so they
	// are the seeded keys themselves — which is the strongest form of the
	// test: even a *valid* key must travel as a bind parameter, because
	// the compiler cannot tell a valid key from a crafted one.
	sql, args := compileOf(t, g, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","keys":["hogger"],
		"where":{"field":"rank","op":"eq","value":"rare"}}]}`)
	for _, sentinel := range []string{"hogger", "rare", "quest", "min_level"} {
		if strings.Contains(sql, sentinel) {
			t.Errorf("the caller value %q reached the statement text:\n%s", sentinel, sql)
		}
	}
	if len(args) < 3 {
		t.Fatalf("the values must have gone somewhere: %d bind arguments", len(args))
	}
}

// TestEveryTableReferenceIsProjectFiltered walks the emitted SQL rather
// than one query's behaviour, so a clause added by a later task cannot
// quietly drop the filter.
func TestEveryTableReferenceIsProjectFiltered(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	sql, _ := compileOf(t, g, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],
		"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"available_to","direction":"out","to_type":"class","as":"c"}],
		"edges":[{"from_step":"c"}]}`)
	refs := regexp.MustCompile(`(?i)\b(FROM|JOIN)\s+(entities|relations)\b`).FindAllString(sql, -1)
	if len(refs) == 0 {
		t.Fatalf("no table references found; the assertion below would be vacuous:\n%s", sql)
	}
	for _, block := range strings.Split(sql, "SELECT") {
		if !strings.Contains(block, "entities") && !strings.Contains(block, "relations") {
			continue
		}
		if !strings.Contains(block, "project_id = $1") {
			t.Errorf("a select over entities or relations does not filter on the project:\n%s", block)
		}
	}
}

func TestATypedPredicateGuardsItsCastByJsonbType(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	sql, _ := compileOf(t, g,
		`{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","where":{"field":"min_level","op":"between","value":[20,30]}}]}`)
	if !strings.Contains(sql, "jsonb_typeof") {
		t.Fatalf("a number predicate must guard its cast with jsonb_typeof:\n%s", sql)
	}
	if !strings.Contains(sql, "::numeric") {
		t.Fatalf("a number predicate must compare numerically:\n%s", sql)
	}
}

func TestInvalidRowsAreExcludedUnlessAskedFor(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	sql, _ := compileOf(t, g, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}`)
	if !strings.Contains(sql, "invalid = false") {
		t.Fatalf("invalid rows must be excluded by default:\n%s", sql)
	}
	sql, _ = compileOf(t, g, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest"}],"include_invalid":true}`)
	if strings.Contains(sql, "invalid = false") {
		t.Fatalf("include_invalid must lift the filter:\n%s", sql)
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Write the failing execution test, which is the read-back**

`internal/views/execute_test.go`:

```go
package views

import (
	"testing"
)

// TestASeedSelectorComesBackAsNodes is the read-back this whole
// sub-project's first feature owes. It asserts a named quest with its
// key, name, type and set — not a row count, which an unrelated bug can
// also satisfy.
func TestASeedSelectorComesBackAsNodes(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	res, err := g.views.Run(t.Context(), g.projectID, RunRequest{
		Query: mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"quests"}]}`),
	})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run: %v", err)
	}
	byKey := map[string]Node{}
	for _, n := range res.Nodes {
		byKey[n.Key] = n
	}
	hogger, ok := byKey["hogger"]
	if !ok {
		t.Fatalf("the seeded quest must come back; got %v", keysOf(res.Nodes))
	}
	if hogger.Name != "Wanted: Hogger" || hogger.Type != "quest" || hogger.Set != "quests" {
		t.Fatalf("the node did not carry its identity: %+v", hogger)
	}
	if len(res.Nodes) != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("three quests are seeded, got %d: %v", len(res.Nodes), keysOf(res.Nodes))
	}
	if res.Stats.Nodes != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("stats must count what came back, got %+v", res.Stats)
	}
}

func TestAPredicateNarrowsTheResultAndTheControlProvesIt(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	all, err := g.views.Run(t.Context(), g.projectID, RunRequest{
		Query: mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}`)})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run: %v", err)
	}
	if len(all.Nodes) != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("positive control: three quests, got %d", len(all.Nodes))
	}
	band, err := g.views.Run(t.Context(), g.projectID, RunRequest{
		Query: mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","where":{"all":[
			{"field":"min_level","op":"gte","value":20},
			{"field":"min_level","op":"lte","value":30}]}}]}`)})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run: %v", err)
	}
	if got := keysOf(band.Nodes); len(got) != 2 {
		t.Fatalf("the 20-30 band holds hogger and defias, got %v", got)
	}
}

func TestAOneHopTraversalReturnsItsEdges(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	res, err := g.views.Run(t.Context(), g.projectID, RunRequest{
		Query: mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"class","as":"cls",
			"where":{"field":"@key","op":"eq","value":"mage"}}],
			"traverse":[{"from":"cls","via":"available_to","direction":"in",
			             "to_type":"quest","as":"reachable"}],
			"edges":[{"from_step":"reachable"}]}`)})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run: %v", err)
	}
	if len(res.Edges) != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("three quests are available to the mage, got %d edges", len(res.Edges))
	}
	for _, e := range res.Edges {
		if e.Type != "available_to" || e.Source == e.Target {
			t.Fatalf("an edge must carry its type and two endpoints: %+v", e)
		}
	}
}

// TestARunFromAnotherGameSeesNothing is the isolation test at the
// execution boundary, with its positive control in the same test.
func TestARunFromAnotherGameSeesNothing(t *testing.T) {
	g, other := newGame(t)
	q := `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}`
	mine, err := g.views.Run(t.Context(), g.projectID, RunRequest{Query: mustParse(t, q)})
	if err != nil || len(mine.Nodes) != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("positive control: %v, %d nodes", err, len(mine.Nodes))
	}
	theirs, err := other.views.Run(t.Context(), other.projectID, RunRequest{Query: mustParse(t, q)})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run in the other game: %v", err)
	}
	for _, n := range theirs.Nodes {
		for _, m := range mine.Nodes {
			if n.ID == m.ID {
				t.Fatalf("a node of one game came back in the other: %s", n.Key)
			}
		}
	}
}

func keysOf(nodes []Node) []string {
	out := make([]string, 0, len(nodes))
	for _, n := range nodes {
		out = append(out, n.Key)
	}
	return out
}
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Write the builder and the compiler**

`internal/views/compile.go` — the shape, with the parts that matter in
full:

```go
package views

import (
	"fmt"
	"strings"

	"github.com/google/uuid"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/graph"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
)

// builder is the only thing in this package that appends to a statement.
//
// **It has no method that takes a caller's value as text**, and that is
// the whole design: bind() returns the placeholder and puts the value in
// the argument slice, write() takes a Go string literal this file wrote.
// There is deliberately no writef with a %s a value could reach — a
// single fmt.Sprintf with a caller value in it is the injection this
// repository's only runtime-built SQL could have, and the way to not have
// it is to make it unspellable rather than to remember not to write it.
// TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText is the assertion.
type builder struct {
	sql  strings.Builder
	args []any
}

func (b *builder) write(literal string) { b.sql.WriteString(literal) }

func (b *builder) bind(v any) string {
	b.args = append(b.args, v)
	return fmt.Sprintf("$%d", len(b.args))
}

// SeedSource is where a query's seed rows come from. There is exactly one
// implementation, fromSelectors, and the interface exists so that the
// analysis-sourced seed the analysis spec's §10 describes is a second
// implementation rather than an edit to the compiler. See this plan's
// open question O8: v1 has no `source` key, ParseQuery refuses unknown
// top-level keys so adding one later is additive, and this is the seam it
// will attach to.
type SeedSource interface {
	// seedSQL returns a SELECT of one uuid column, using b for its binds.
	seedSQL(b *builder, projectID uuid.UUID, set ResolvedSet) string
}

// Compile turns a resolved query into one SELECT and its arguments.
//
// $1 is always the project id, in every CTE and every arm, and
// TestEveryTableReferenceIsProjectFiltered asserts it as text.
func Compile(r *Resolved, projectID uuid.UUID) (string, []any, error) {
	b := &builder{}
	b.args = append(b.args, projectID) // $1, referenced by every clause
	var ctes []string

	for i, set := range r.Sets {
		ctes = append(ctes, compileSelector(b, projectID, r, set, fmt.Sprintf("s%d", i)))
	}
	for i, step := range r.Steps {
		cte, err := compileStep(b, projectID, r, step, fmt.Sprintf("t%d", i))
		if err != nil {
			return "", nil, err
		}
		ctes = append(ctes, cte)
	}
	nodes := compileNodeUnion(b, r)
	edges := compileEdgeUnion(b, r)

	b.write("WITH RECURSIVE ")
	b.write(strings.Join(ctes, ",\n"))
	b.write(",\n")
	b.write(nodes)
	b.write(",\n")
	b.write(edges)
	b.write("\nSELECT 'node' AS kind, n.* FROM node_rows n\n")
	b.write("UNION ALL\nSELECT 'edge' AS kind, e.* FROM edge_rows e")
	return b.sql.String(), b.args, nil
}
```

Write `compileSelector` so that it emits, for a set named `s0` over
entity type `$k` with keys `$m` and a predicate:

```sql
s0 (id, set_name) AS (
    SELECT e.id, $n
    FROM entities e
    WHERE e.project_id = $1
      AND e.entity_type_id = $k
      AND e.invalid = false
      AND lower(e.key) = ANY($m)
      AND (<predicate>)
)
```

with `AND e.invalid = false` omitted when `r.Query.IncludeInvalid` is set,
and the `lower(e.key) = ANY(...)` clause omitted when the selector names
no keys. The set name travels as a **bind parameter**, not as SQL text —
which is what makes `TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText` pass on
a legal key.

Write `compilePredicate(b, alias string, p *ResolvedPredicate) string`
returning a parenthesised boolean expression, with these arms:

- `All` → `(a AND b AND …)`, `Any` → `(a OR b OR …)`, `Not` → `NOT (a)`,
  an empty tree → `true`.
- A **built-in** leaf compiles against a column: `@name` → `e.name`,
  `@key` → `e.key`, `@invalid` → `e.invalid`, `@created_at` →
  `e.created_at`, `@type` → a comparison against the resolved type id
  rather than against a key string, because the id is what the row holds.
- A **declared field** leaf compiles against `e.fields`, with the key
  bound: `e.fields -> $k`. **The type guard comes before the cast**,
  always, because the metamodel flags a row invalid rather than rejecting
  it when a schema evolves, so a column declared `number` can genuinely
  hold `"veinte"` in a row written before the schema changed:

```go
// numberLeaf emits the guarded comparison a number predicate needs.
//
// The guard is not defensive. internal/metamodel marks an entity invalid
// when a schema change makes its values not fit, and leaves the values in
// place (MarkEntitiesOfTypeInvalid), so a field declared number today may
// hold a string written yesterday. Without jsonb_typeof the cast raises
// SQLSTATE 22P02 and the whole view fails on one stale row.
func numberLeaf(b *builder, alias, key string, op Operator, values []any) string {
	path := fmt.Sprintf("%s.fields -> %s", alias, b.bind(key))
	value := fmt.Sprintf("(%s.fields ->> %s)::numeric", alias, b.bind(key))
	guard := fmt.Sprintf("jsonb_typeof(%s) = 'number'", path)
	switch op {
	case OpBetween:
		return fmt.Sprintf("(%s AND %s BETWEEN %s AND %s)",
			guard, value, b.bind(values[0]), b.bind(values[1]))
	case OpIn:
		return fmt.Sprintf("(%s AND %s = ANY(%s))", guard, value, b.bind(values))
	case OpExists:
		return fmt.Sprintf("(%s.fields ? %s)", alias, b.bind(key))
	default:
		return fmt.Sprintf("(%s AND %s %s %s)", guard, value, sqlComparison(op), b.bind(values[0]))
	}
}
```

with `sqlComparison` mapping `eq neq lt lte gt gte` onto `= <> < <= > >=`
and **panicking on anything else**, because an operator that reached here
without a comparison is a compiler bug and not a caller's mistake — the
one place in this package where a panic is right, and its message says so.

Text leaves use `->>` with `ILIKE` for `contains`/`starts_with`/`matches`
and **escape the caller's value's `%` and `_` before binding it**, since a
designer's quest name may hold either:

```go
// likeOperand escapes the two LIKE metacharacters in a caller's value,
// so `starts_with: "50%"` matches a name starting "50%" rather than a
// name starting "50". The escape character is backslash and the pattern
// says so with ESCAPE '\'.
func likeOperand(s string) string {
	r := strings.NewReplacer(`\`, `\\`, `%`, `\%`, `_`, `\_`)
	return r.Replace(s)
}
```

`matches` is the literal-anchored glob the operator table promises: `*`
becomes `%` and `?` becomes `_` **after** the escape, and nothing else is
a metacharacter.

`list<text>` leaves use `@>` for `contains` (which the shipped
`entities_fields_idx` GIN index actually serves),
`jsonb_array_length` for the length family, and
`?|` / `?&` for `contains_any` / `contains_all`.

- [ ] **Step 5: Write `execute.go` with the envelope and `Run`**

```go
// Node is one row of a result. It carries identity and the attributes the
// projection asked for, and never a full fields payload unless
// include_fields was set: a thousand-node result with every jsonb field
// inlined is a five-figure token bill for a picture the agent is not
// going to look at.
type Node struct {
	ID     uuid.UUID      `json:"id"`
	Key    string         `json:"key"`
	Type   string         `json:"type"`
	Name   string         `json:"name"`
	Set    string         `json:"set"`
	Role   string         `json:"role,omitempty"`
	Attrs  map[string]any `json:"attrs,omitempty"`
	Fields map[string]any `json:"fields,omitempty"`
	// Ambiguous is set when a one-hop related attribute found more than
	// one entity and the first by name was used. Silently picking one and
	// saying nothing would produce a map that is wrong in a way nobody
	// can see.
	Ambiguous bool `json:"ambiguous,omitempty"`
}

// Edge is one relation in a result.
//
// **ID is a relations.id and is not stable across a re-seed.** relations
// carries no key and no version (0004_metamodel.sql), so an edge is
// addressable only as (relation_type_id, source_id, target_id) and a
// re-seed that deleted and recreated it produces a new id for the same
// edge. Nothing in this sub-project stores an edge id; a client that does
// is storing something that will change under it.
type Edge struct {
	ID     uuid.UUID      `json:"id"`
	Type   string         `json:"type"`
	Source uuid.UUID      `json:"source"`
	Target uuid.UUID      `json:"target"`
	Label  string         `json:"label,omitempty"`
	Fields map[string]any `json:"fields,omitempty"`
}

// Stats is always populated. The first question about a slow view is
// whether it is the query or the size of the answer, and without both
// numbers it cannot be answered. DurationMS is also the measurement this
// plan's open question O2 (indexed jsonb fields) is to be re-argued with.
type Stats struct {
	Nodes           int   `json:"nodes"`
	Edges           int   `json:"edges"`
	MaxDepthReached int   `json:"max_depth_reached"`
	DurationMS      int64 `json:"duration_ms"`
}

// Truncated says which caps were hit. Truncation is not an error: a
// designer asking about a huge graph should see a thousand nodes and a
// warning, not a stack trace.
type Truncated struct {
	Nodes bool `json:"nodes"`
	Edges bool `json:"edges"`
	Depth bool `json:"depth"`
}

// Result is the one envelope every renderer consumes. One shape for every
// renderer is the point: swapping `graph` for `table` on a saved view
// must never require rewriting the query.
type Result struct {
	Nodes     []Node       `json:"nodes"`
	Edges     []Edge       `json:"edges"`
	Stats     Stats        `json:"stats"`
	Truncated Truncated    `json:"truncated"`
	Stale     []Diagnostic `json:"stale"`
}

// RunRequest is one execution: a parsed query, the parameter values that
// override its declared defaults, and what to do about staleness.
type RunRequest struct {
	Query     *Query
	Params    map[string]any
	OnStale   string
	IncludeFields bool
}

// Run compiles and executes one query.
func (s *Service) Run(ctx context.Context, projectID uuid.UUID, req RunRequest) (Result, error)
```

`Run`'s body: load the catalogue, `ResolveAgainst`, bind `req.Params` over
the declared defaults (type-checked with `coerceParam`, refused as
`query_invalid` at `/params/<i>` on a mismatch), `Compile`, then execute
inside the read-only transaction Task 7 adds — for now a plain
`s.pool.Query`, replaced in the next task, with a `// Task 7 replaces
this with the bounded transaction` comment so the gap is a note rather
than a silence.

**Corrections from implementing Task 6** (landed as `f633b9e`, `787e1ef`,
`9b29be5` and `d32cd13`; every mutation named below was applied to the
shipped code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with
the baseline confirmed green — and confirmed *running*, `-v`, zero
`SKIP`). Nine findings. Four of them change a code block a later task
depends on.

1. **"A builder with no method that takes a caller value as text" is not
   expressible with a `string` parameter, and the plan's block is not
   what ships.** `func (b *builder) write(literal string)` accepts
   `set.Name` and compiles: the promise was a naming convention, and
   `TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText` can only ever prove it
   for the queries it happens to compile. What ships is a defined type,
   `frag`, over `string`. An untyped constant — `"SELECT "` — converts to
   it implicitly; a `string` *variable* does not, so `b.write(set.Name)`
   **does not compile**, and spelling a caller's value into a statement
   requires writing `frag(...)` in the source. `write(frag)`,
   `bind(any) frag`, `sprintf(frag, ...frag)` and `joinFrags` are the
   whole surface, and `sprintf`'s format is an untyped constant at every
   call site for the same reason.
   `TestTheOnlyStringToFragmentConversionsAreTheOnesNamedHere` parses
   `compile.go`'s own syntax tree and refuses a `frag(...)` conversion
   outside the four helpers that build a placeholder, a name or a format
   from things a caller cannot reach — so the construction is a test
   rather than a habit, and it holds for queries nobody has written.
   Both tests are red under the plan's own step 8.1 mutation.

2. **Step 8.2's behavioural half does not fail, and the plan's note about
   why is wrong.** Deleting `AND e.project_id = $1` from `compileSelector`
   leaves `TestARunFromAnotherGameSeesNothing` **green** — not because the
   fixture seeds one game, which it does not, but because the selector
   also filters `e.entity_type_id = $k` against an id resolved in *this*
   game, and another game's rows carry another game's type ids. The two
   filters are independent guards and the second hides the loss of the
   first. So the text test is the only one that catches it, exactly as
   Task 1's invariant said and for a different reason than the plan gives
   — and the golden files catch it too, which is the second thing they
   buy. `TestEveryTableReferenceIsProjectFiltered` and all three golden
   files are red under that mutation.

3. **Three shapes the plan's block would have compiled into SQL Postgres
   refuses, all found by writing the emitter rather than by review.**
   - **A relation has no `key`, no `name` and no `invalid` column**
     (`0004_metamodel.sql`), and `edge_where` admitted every built-in,
     so `{"field":"@key"}` on a step's `edge_where` compiled to `r.key`
     and failed the whole view with a SQL error instead of a pointer.
     Fixed where it belongs — in resolution, so `views.validate` catches
     it without compiling: `fieldScope` gains an `edge` flag and admits
     only `@type` and `@created_at` there.
     `TestAnEdgePredicateAdmitsOnlyTheBuiltinsARelationHas` pins the
     refusal with both admitted built-ins as its controls. The compiler
     carries the same refusal a second time, because `Compile` is
     exported and takes a `*Resolved` a Go caller may have built by hand.
   - **`edges[].via` was resolved by nobody.** `resolveInto` walks
     `from`, `traverse` and `project` and never `edges`, so the
     `via`/`between` spelling reached the compiler as raw keys. Resolving
     them privately in `compile.go` would have been a second place that
     turns a key into an id — and a reference missing from `Refs`, so
     deleting a relation type a view draws edges with would report the
     view as fine. `Resolved` gains **`Edges []ResolvedEdge`**, filled by
     the same `relationType` closure every other reference goes through,
     walked between `traverse` and `project` so problems stay in document
     order. `TestAnEdgeEntrysRelationTypeIsResolvedAndListed` pins the
     refusal and the listed dependency.
   - **An `edges: [{"from_step": …}]` naming a *selector*** has no
     relation to draw, and `checkQuery` only asserts the set exists. It is
     refused rather than answered with an empty edge set.

4. **`@type`: the decision Task 4 left here, made.** It compiles against
   the id the row holds — `entity_type_id`, or `relation_type_id` in an
   `edge_where` — for `eq`, `neq` and `in`, and **a key this game does not
   declare is refused**, not drawn as nothing. The alternative Task 4
   recorded (compare the key as text, misspelling is an empty result)
   loses to §3's own argument: an empty picture that is really a typo is
   the most expensive failure mode this language has, and this one is
   nameable. The pattern operators cannot be answered by an id, so they
   compile against the type's key through a project-filtered scalar
   subquery. **Two consequences for later tasks**: the refusal is raised
   by the *compiler*, not by resolution, so Task 15's `views.validate`
   has to compile as well as resolve to surface it; and `@type` still
   contributes no `TypeRef`, which stays Task 11 and 12's decision.

5. **A depth greater than 1 is refused, with its pointer, until Task 8.**
   Emitting it as a one-hop join would answer a depth-3 question with a
   depth-1 answer and no error.
   `TestAMultiHopStepIsRefusedUntilTheWalkArrives` pins it and **Task 8
   deletes it** in the same commit as `compileStep`'s recursive arm.

6. **`Result.Stale`, `RunRequest.OnStale` and `Node.Attrs` do not ship
   as the block writes them.** `Stale []Diagnostic` names a type Task 12
   declares, and a field declared against a type that does not exist does
   not compile; `OnStale` would be a knob nothing reads. Both are Task
   12's to add. `Node.Attrs`, `Node.Ambiguous` and `Edge.Label` do ship,
   because Task 9 fills them and the envelope's shape is a contract Task
   15's tool description is generated against — each says on the struct
   that nothing fills it yet. `Truncated` ships all-false with a comment
   saying **all-false means "not measured"** until Task 7 emits the
   `cap + 1`, rather than "not truncated".

7. **`Stats.MaxDepthReached` is computed from the query's shape, not from
   a depth column**, and is exact only because every step this build
   compiles is one hop: a set's depth is its source set's depth plus the
   step's own, and a set that contributed no node contributes no depth.
   **Task 8 must replace that arithmetic** with `graph.WalkCTE`'s own
   `depth` column, which is the only thing that knows how deep a walk
   actually got.

8. **The result ordering had to be pinned as text, because its
   behavioural half is green without it.** A node named by two `nodes`
   entries is deduplicated in Go, keeping the first entry that claimed
   it, which is only meaningful if the rows arrive in the entries'
   declaration order — so each arm carries a bound `rank` and the final
   statement is `ORDER BY 1, 11, 2`. Deleting the `ORDER BY` left
   `TestANodeInTwoSetsComesBackOnceUnderTheFirstSetThatClaimedIt` passing
   three runs out of three, because a `UNION` of two arms happens to come
   back in arm order on this Postgres. The test now asserts the clause as
   text and says which half pins what — the same split
   `TestATruncatedWalkIsOrderedByDepth` makes in `internal/graph`, and
   the same defect that correction found there.

9. **What the compiled statement actually costs, corrected.** This plan
   and the spec both say "every predicate that is not equality is a
   scan"; equality is a scan too. `entities_fields_idx` is
   `gin (fields jsonb_path_ops)`, which serves `@>` on the whole `fields`
   column and nothing else — so a guarded `(fields ->> $k)::numeric = $v`
   is as much a scan as `>` is. The one leaf an index can answer is
   `contains` on a `list<text>` field, emitted as
   `fields @> jsonb_build_object($k, jsonb_build_array($v))` for exactly
   that reason. `Compile`'s own comment states this rather than the
   plan's version, and **Task 15's tool description must say the same**.

**Three things decided here that the plan did not name:**

- **A scalar comparison is a guarded cast, never containment**, including
  `eq`. Containment would be indexable, but it gives `neq` different null
  semantics from `lt` — `NOT (fields @> …)` matches a row that lacks the
  field, a guarded cast does not — and one uniform rule ("a row that does
  not carry the field matches no comparison") is worth more than one
  indexed operator.
- **A row key is folded on both sides, a name is not.** `@key eq
  "Hogger"` and the `keys` shortcut both compare `lower()` against a
  lowered bind, because `entities_key_key` is `UNIQUE (project_id,
  entity_type_id, lower(key))` and folding one side only refuses a
  spelling the write path accepted. `@name` is free text and is compared
  as written. `TestAKeyComparisonFoldsCaseOnBothSides` covers both
  clauses.
- **A parameter with no value is refused at the leaf that reads it**, not
  compiled as `NULL`. `bindParams` overlays a run's values on the
  document's defaults, reports every problem in one pass, addresses a bad
  value at the declaration it belongs to (`/params/N`) and an undeclared
  name at `/params`.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each: the set name spelled into the SQL (2 tests); the selector's project
filter deleted (4); the `jsonb_typeof` guard deleted (2, the second with
SQLSTATE 22P02 — the failure the guard exists for, seen); the node
dedupe removed (1); `likeOperand` made a no-op (1); the key fold removed
(1); an unknown `@type` key answered as nothing (1); a multi-hop step
emitted as one hop (1); an unbound parameter compiled as nothing (1); an
`edges` entry naming a selector drawn as nothing (1); an `edges` entry
resolving its own key (1); `include_fields` ignored (1); a run's
parameters ignored (1); the `ORDER BY` deleted (1, after the text
assertion was added — it survived three runs before). Fourteen
mutations, fourteen red.

**Recorded for Task 7**, verified rather than assumed: `Service` carries
a `*pgxpool.Pool` again — Task 4's correction 16 removed it as unread,
and the compiler is what reads it — so `runInTx` has the handle its block
expects. `Run` currently calls `s.pool.Query` directly with a comment
naming Task 7 as its replacement, and the statement carries **no `LIMIT`
at all**, so Task 7 adds the `cap + 1` in both collection points
(`node_rows` and `edge_rows`), not in the final union.


**Corrections from Task 6's review round** (landed as `e2cf1d6`,
`1cfded7`, `9a2e2e0`, `9a77093`, `509501f`, `94b1fcd` and `7ad9a03`;
every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code, watched fail
the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline confirmed green
and confirmed *running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported). The
injection guarantee itself held: every caller-controlled string the
language admits was exercised and came back as one of 36 bind arguments,
with nothing in the query text. What did not hold is the **guards meant
to keep that true for code nobody has written yet** — and Tasks 7, 8 and
9 all add SQL.

1. **The AST guard read one file and four routes walked past it.** The
   first version parsed `compile.go` alone and descended only into
   function declarations, so all of these compiled and left the suite
   green: a `frag(...)` conversion **in any other file of the package**
   (`frag` is unexported but package-*scoped*); **no conversion at all**,
   because `builder.sql` was a bare `strings.Builder` and
   `b.sql.WriteString(v)` needs none; a **parenthesised** conversion,
   `(frag)(v)`, whose call function is an `*ast.ParenExpr` rather than an
   `*ast.Ident` — applied inside `compile.go` itself and the test passed;
   and a **local type alias**, `type textFrag = frag`, whose conversions
   never mention `frag`. A package-level variable initialiser, or a
   function literal assigned to one, was never scanned either. What ships:
   the test parses **every non-test file in the package directory** (and
   fails if it finds fewer than two, so it can never silently go back to
   watching one), unwraps parentheses, refuses any type declaration whose
   underlying type is `frag`, walks each *declaration* rather than each
   function body, and — the second route closed by construction rather
   than by assertion — the builder now holds an unexported `sqlText`
   wrapper whose only appender takes a `frag`, with a check that nothing
   but that wrapper's own two methods reaches the `.raw` buffer
   underneath. All five routes were re-applied and all five are red, each
   naming what it caught: the other file's function, the raw-buffer
   writer, `selector`, the alias declaration, and "a package-level
   declaration in compile.go".

2. **The project-filter invariant passed with a filter deleted, and it is
   the only guard there is.** The test split the statement on `SELECT`
   and asked whether `project_id = $1` appeared *somewhere* in each
   block, so deleting `AND et.project_id = $1` from `nodeUnion`'s
   entity-type join left it green — another table in the same block still
   carried one. Its vacuity check matched two of the four project-scoped
   tables, leaving `entity_types` and `relation_types` outside the test
   entirely. It now matches every `FROM`/`JOIN` of the four, captures the
   alias, and requires **that alias's own** `project_id = $1` in the same
   block, with a per-table vacuity check that fails if the query stops
   exercising one of them. Red under four mutations: the entity-type join
   filter (the one the old test missed), a relation-type join filter, the
   selector's own filter, and the step's far-entity filter.

   **What makes this the load-bearing test, recorded because it changes
   what the test is for**: in the current build *every* project filter is
   redundant. The selector filters on an `entity_type_id` resolved in this
   game, the step on a `relation_type_id` and a `to_type` resolved the
   same way, the between arm on its own `relation_type_id`, and both
   join-backs join to rows those filters already isolated. So
   `TestARunFromAnotherGameSeesNothing` cannot fail on a lost project
   filter under any shape this compiler emits today. The filters are
   defence in depth against the shapes Tasks 7, 8 and 9 add, and the text
   test is the only thing defending them — which is why a hole in it
   mattered more than it looked.

3. **The `matches` operator returned wrong answers and had no test of any
   kind.** `likePattern` escaped the operand, mapped the glob characters,
   and then ran a *second* replacer that turned every `\%` back into a
   bare `%` — including the ones the escape pass had just written for the
   caller's own characters. `matches "50%"` compiled to the pattern `50%`
   (a prefix match on "50") and `matches "a_b"` to `a_b` (any character
   between the a and the b), contradicting both the operator table's
   literal-anchored glob and this plan's own rule that the glob characters
   map *after* the escape. Against a fixture holding `50% Off` and
   `50 Silver`, `matches "50%*"` returned both. It is now **one pass over
   the runes** (`escapeLike`, with a `glob` flag), which cannot undo its
   own work because it never revisits a character it wrote, and
   `likeOperand` is that pass with the flag off. Two new tests: the
   pattern table itself (`50%` → `50\%`, `a_b` → `a\_b`, `50%*` →
   `50\%%`, `Hogg?r` → `Hogg_r`, `back\slash` → `back\\slash`), and the
   behavioural half with both fixture rows and two controls. Both are red
   against the shipped emitter restored.

4. **Four surviving mutations, now killed, and one of them says something
   about how it has to be tested.**
   - **The typed-array cast for the `in` family** (`::numeric[]`,
     `::text[]`, `::boolean[]`, `::timestamptz[]`) had nothing pinning it.
     `TestAnInListComparesAsItsOwnType` asserts the cast as text *and*
     reads the rows back, for a number list and a folded key list.
     Recorded because it is the interesting half: with every cast removed
     the **behavioural** assertions still pass — pgx sends a typed array
     regardless, so Postgres never has to guess — and only the text
     assertion goes red. The cast is a statement-shape invariant, and a
     statement-shape invariant is pinned as text or not at all.
   - **A non-default direction on an `edges[].between` entry** was used by
     no test. `TestABetweenEdgeReadsItsDirection` walks `takes_place_in`
     between two *different* sets (quests and zones), where `out` draws
     two edges, `in` draws none and `any` draws two — a same-set pair
     cannot tell the arms apart. The shipped semantics were correct, so
     this is coverage rather than a fix.
   - **The max-depth arithmetic** — the value Task 8 is told to replace —
     had no test. `TestMaxDepthReachedCountsTheHopsThatContributedANode`
     pins all four cases: a selector is 0, a step is 1, a step from a step
     is 2, and a set that drew no node contributes no depth.
   - **The edge dedupe** was unpinned while the node dedupe was.
     `TestAnEdgeDrawnTwiceComesBackOnce` draws the same three relations
     from a `from_step` entry and a `between` entry; the `UNION` cannot
     collapse them because each arm carries its own bound rank, so the
     three that come back are Go's doing.

   The two mutations that were **red only through the golden files** — a
   step's invalid-row filter and its destination-type filter — were
   confirmed golden-only and then given an answer from the database.
   `TestAStepDrawsOnlyItsDestinationTypeAndOnlyValidRows` seeds an
   `available_to` from a *zone* (nothing constrains a relation type's
   endpoints) and flags a quest invalid, then asserts the walk draws
   neither, with a control for each.

5. **The three low findings, two fixed and one recorded in the test that
   needed it.**
   - **The golden test now carries its own warning**, in the doc comment
     and in the failure message: `-update` makes any change to the emitter
     agree with itself, including a dropped filter, so the diff is read
     before the flag is reached. The three invariants that were red *only*
     there each have a test of their own now, but the next clause a task
     adds arrives in these files first and unaccompanied.
   - **An empty result serialised as `{"nodes": null, "edges": null}`**,
     because the slices were nil. `Run` starts them empty, and
     `TestAnEmptyResultSerialisesAsEmptyListsNotNull` marshals the
     envelope rather than trusting the declaration — Task 15's REST mirror
     hands this shape to clients that would otherwise have to read both
     spellings of "no nodes".
   - **A step refused an empty direction while an edge entry read it as
     "out"** in a switch arm of its own. Fixed towards the step:
     `applyDefaults` fills `edges[].direction` where every other default
     is filled, and the compiler's `""` arm is gone, so an empty direction
     is refused at `/edges/N/direction` exactly as it is on a step —
     reachable now only from a `*Resolved` a Go caller built by hand.
     `TestAnEdgeEntrysEmptyDirectionIsFilledOnceAndRefusedAfter` pins the
     default, the refusal and the control that still compiles. No golden
     file moved: a filled `out` emits the forward arm the empty string
     used to.

**Recorded for Task 15** (also written into that task's block): `@type`
refuses a misspelled key for `eq`, `neq` and `in`, and draws nothing
silently for `contains`, `starts_with` and `matches`, which take the
scalar-subquery path over the type's key. Defensible for a pattern, but
it is the same silent-empty failure the equality arms were just changed
to refuse, so the tool description has to state the split rather than
leave an agent to infer one rule from the other half.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each: a `frag` conversion in another file of the package (1); a raw
buffer write with no conversion (1); a parenthesised conversion inside
`compile.go` (1); a local alias over `frag` (1); a conversion in a
package-level variable's initialiser (1); the entity-type join's project
filter (1); a relation-type join's project filter (1); the selector's
project filter (1); the step's far-entity project filter (1); the
chained-replacer `likePattern` restored (2); every typed-array cast
removed (1, text only — see 4); the between arm's `in` answered as
forward (1); the max-depth arithmetic returning zero (1); the edge dedupe
removed (1); the step's invalid filter (1, now behavioural); the step's

Eighteen mutations, eighteen red.
