### Task 7: Bounds — the read-only transaction, truncation, stats and the timeout

**Files:**
- Modify: `internal/views/execute.go`
- Create: `internal/views/bounds_test.go`

An agent *will* write a query that walks a 400-mission graph unbounded.
The design has to survive that rather than hope.

| Bound | Default | Hard cap | Behaviour past it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `max_depth` | 4 | 12 | `limit_exceeded` at save (Task 3); truncated at run with `truncated.depth` |
| `max_nodes` | 1000 | 5000 | truncated, `truncated.nodes` |
| `max_edges` | 4000 | 20000 | truncated, `truncated.edges` |
| statement timeout | 5 s | 15 s | `retryable`, naming which bound to lower |
| CTE steps | — | 8 | refused at parse (Task 2) |

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

`internal/views/bounds_test.go`, with these named tests:

- `TestEveryQueryRunsInAReadOnlyTransaction` — runs a query and, from
  inside the same pool, asserts `default_transaction_read_only` was on by
  compiling a query whose emitted SQL the test wraps with a trailing
  `; INSERT` — no: instead it calls the unexported `runInTx` with a
  statement of its own (`INSERT INTO views …`) and asserts SQLSTATE
  `25006` (`read_only_sql_transaction`). Asserting the refusal is what
  makes "the compiler only ever emits SELECT" a guarantee rather than a
  property of the current code.
- `TestATruncatedResultIsFlaggedNotErrored` — seeds 12 quests, runs with
  `limits.max_nodes: 10`, asserts 10 nodes, `Truncated.Nodes == true` and
  a nil error. The `LIMIT cap + 1` mechanism means truncation is
  **detected**, not inferred, and the test asserts exactly 10 rather than
  11 so a leaked sentinel row would fail.
- `TestAnUntruncatedResultSaysSo` — the control: 3 quests, `max_nodes:
  10`, `Truncated.Nodes == false`. Without it, a `Truncated` that was
  always true would pass the test above.
- `TestStatsCountWhatCameBack` — asserts `Stats.Nodes`, `Stats.Edges` and
  a non-zero `DurationMS`.
- `TestATimedOutQueryIsRetryableAndSaysWhichBoundToLower` — sets the
  statement timeout to `1ms` through the unexported knob, runs a query,
  asserts `metamodel.IsRetryable(err)` and that the message contains
  `max_depth`, `max_nodes` and the elapsed budget. This is the
  settlement of the spec's `query_timeout` proposal: the code is
  `retryable`, the *advice* is completed, and the tool description says
  so (Task 15).

- [ ] **Step 2: Implement the bounded execution**

```go
// runInTx executes one compiled statement under the two settings that
// make the bounds real rather than intended.
//
// **default_transaction_read_only = on** is what makes "the compiler only
// ever emits SELECT" a guarantee instead of a property of the current
// code. The compiler is careful; a transaction that refuses a write is
// careful forever. TestEveryQueryRunsInAReadOnlyTransaction asserts the
// refusal with SQLSTATE 25006.
//
// **statement_timeout** bounds the work. Past it Postgres raises 57014,
// which internal/metamodel.IsRetryable already admits and internal/web
// already maps to the `retryable` wire code — so a timed-out view does
// not get a ninth code that means the same thing. What it does get is
// the advice completed: the error names the elapsed budget and the three
// bounds to lower, because "send the same call again" is right for
// contention and wrong for a query that is simply too expensive, and the
// caller cannot tell those apart from the code alone.
func (s *Service) runInTx(ctx context.Context, timeout time.Duration, sql string,
	args []any, scan func(pgx.Rows) error) error {
	tx, err := s.pool.BeginTx(ctx, pgx.TxOptions{AccessMode: pgx.ReadOnly})
	if err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("begin read-only: %w", err)
	}
	defer func() { _ = tx.Rollback(ctx) }()
	if _, err := tx.Exec(ctx, "SET LOCAL default_transaction_read_only = on"); err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("set read-only: %w", err)
	}
	if _, err := tx.Exec(ctx,
		fmt.Sprintf("SET LOCAL statement_timeout = %d", timeout.Milliseconds())); err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("set statement_timeout: %w", err)
	}
	rows, err := tx.Query(ctx, sql, args...)
	if err != nil {
		return err
	}
	defer rows.Close()
	return scan(rows)
}
```

`SET LOCAL statement_timeout` takes the milliseconds through
`fmt.Sprintf`, and that is the **one** place in this package a value is
formatted into SQL: the value is a `time.Duration` this package computed
from its own two constants and no caller can reach it. The comment above
the line says exactly that, and `TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText`
does not cover this statement because it is not the compiled one — which
is stated in the comment rather than left as a gap a reviewer has to find.

`Run` applies `LIMIT max_nodes + 1` and `LIMIT max_edges + 1` in the two
union arms, then truncates in Go and sets the flags. The extra row is how
truncation is **detected**: without it, a result of exactly `max_nodes`
rows is indistinguishable from a graph that happens to have that many.

- [ ] **Step 3: Run, watch pass, then see each red**

```bash
go test ./internal/views/ -run 'TestEveryQueryRunsInAReadOnly|TestATruncated|TestAnUntruncated|TestStatsCount|TestATimedOut' -v
```

Then: remove `pgx.ReadOnly` **and** the `SET LOCAL` (both, since either
alone still refuses) and watch the read-only test go red; change
`LIMIT max+1` to `LIMIT max` and watch `TestATruncatedResultIsFlaggedNotErrored`
fail with `Truncated.Nodes == false`; set the default timeout to a minute
and watch the timeout test fail.

- [ ] **Step 4: Commit**

```bash
git add internal/views/execute.go internal/views/bounds_test.go
git commit -m "feat: bound a view run with a read-only transaction, a timeout and detected truncation"
```

**Corrections from implementing Task 7** (landed as `179175d` and
`59603f1`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code,
watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline
confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL`
exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository). Six findings. Two of them
change the block this task gives, and one changes what a later task has
to do.

1. **`SET LOCAL default_transaction_read_only = on` does not make the
   transaction it runs in read-only, so the plan's block said the safety
   twice and provided it once.** `default_transaction_read_only` is the
   default for transactions started *afterwards*; the current one is
   governed by `transaction_read_only`. Measured rather than reasoned
   about: in a read-write transaction with only that setting, an `INSERT`
   **succeeds**. The plan's own step 3 states that either half alone
   still refuses, which was therefore false of the code it wrote — the
   test would have gone red on removing `pgx.ReadOnly` alone, and a
   reviewer reading the block would have concluded there were two guards
   where there was one. What ships sets `transaction_read_only`, and both
   halves were then checked alone: each refuses the `INSERT` with 25006,
   so the plan's instruction to remove both is now true of the code.

2. **Nothing is formatted into statement text, so the plan's "one
   deliberate exception" does not exist.** `set_config(name, value,
   is_local)` takes its value as a **bind parameter**, so the budget
   travels the way every other value in this package travels and
   `SET LOCAL … = %d` never has to be written. Both settings go in one
   statement and one round trip:
   `SELECT set_config('transaction_read_only', 'on', true),
   set_config('statement_timeout', $1, true)`. The exception the plan
   asked to be documented in a comment is documented as *not needed*
   instead.

   This mattered more than a tidiness: the frag guard
   (`TestTheOnlyStringToFragmentConversionsAreTheOnesNamedHere`) watches
   conversions into the builder's fragment type, and `execute.go`'s own
   SQL never becomes a fragment — so a `fmt.Sprintf` there is invisible to
   it, and the guard would have stayed green over exactly the line the
   plan called an exception. That is this repository's standing defect
   again: a hole closed at one call site, open one step along. The new
   `TestNoStatementTextIsAssembledOutsideTheCompiler` parses every
   non-test file and requires the statement argument of every `Exec`,
   `Query` and `QueryRow` to be either a literal this package wrote or the
   identifier holding the compiler's output — a `+`, a `fmt.Sprintf` or
   any other call fails, with a vacuity check that fails if the package
   stops executing SQL. **Re-applying the plan's own block makes it red**,
   naming `execute.go`'s Exec.

3. **`rows.Err()` after a scan that never iterated returns nil, so the
   read-only test passed the write.** pgx settles a failure into `Err()`
   when the result is finished with, not when `Query` returns: the
   refused `INSERT` came back with a nil error from `Query`, a scan
   closure that iterated nothing, and `Err() == nil`. `runInTx` now calls
   `rows.Close()` before `rows.Err()` (the defer stays for the early
   returns; Close is idempotent). The same hole was one step along in
   `Run`: its loop happens to drain the rows, so it does surface a
   mid-stream failure — a statement_timeout that fires during the fetch —
   but only because it iterates, and nothing said so. Removing the
   `Close()` makes `TestEveryQueryRunsInAReadOnlyTransaction` red with
   "a write inside a view's transaction must be refused".

4. **Truncation counts rows, not surviving nodes.** The obvious reading of
   "detected, not inferred" is `len(result.Nodes) > cap`, and it is wrong
   here: `Run` deduplicates a node claimed by two `nodes` entries, so two
   rows can collapse into one node and a result that fetched its
   `cap + 1` rows can leave `cap` nodes behind — reported as untruncated
   while the graph had more. The count is taken per row, before the
   dedupe, at each collection point. `LIMIT cap` in place of `cap + 1`
   makes both `TestATruncatedResultIsFlaggedNotErrored` and
   `TestAnEdgeResultIsTruncatedToo` red with the flag false, and leaves
   `TestAnUntruncatedResultSaysSo` green, which is what that control is
   for.

   The `cap + 1` tail also carries **`ORDER BY 10, 1`** — the same
   ordering the outer statement applies, on the collection point's own
   columns. Without it the rows a `LIMIT` keeps are whichever Postgres
   produced first, so a truncated result would drop a different arbitrary
   part of the graph on each run and the trim in Go would be trimming a
   set the document's declaration order did not choose. The three golden
   files move by exactly those two lines per collection point and the
   renumbering after them; the diff was read before `-update` was reached.

5. **The plan's third mutation does not reach the test it names, and the
   fix is a pinned constant.** "Set the default timeout to a minute and
   watch the timeout test fail" describes a knob the timeout test does not
   use: the test sets the per-`Service` budget to `1ms`, so raising
   `DefaultStatementTimeout` leaves it green — applied and confirmed
   green. Two answers, both taken. The mutation that *is* equivalent is
   ignoring the knob so every run gets the default, which is red with
   "a query given a millisecond must run out of it". And the table's two
   numbers are now asserted directly in
   `TestTheStatementBudgetIsClampedToItsHardCap`, so the plan's literal
   mutation is red there instead of nowhere — a default quietly raised to
   a minute is a minute of database time per picture, and it would
   otherwise fail nothing.

6. **The leak test asked the wrong connection, and then found that
   is_local was not what protected it.** `TestTheBoundsDoNotLeakOntoTheNextCaller`
   first read `SHOW statement_timeout` off the pool once: the test pool
   holds four connections and the run borrowed whichever was free, so
   three times in four it inspected a connection the run had never
   touched and passed on whatever it answered. It now acquires **every**
   connection the pool can hand out, holds them at once, and checks both
   settings on each. Then the mutation: flipping `is_local` to `false`
   left it **green**, because a plain `SET` is transactional too and
   `runInTx` always rolls back. So neither half is the guard on its own,
   the test holds the pair, and it was proved red against losing both
   (`is_local` off *and* the rollback turned into a commit) with
   "connection 0 kept a statement timeout of 1ms past the transaction
   that set it". Both facts are written into the test rather than left as
   a comfortable-looking assertion.

7. **`Truncated.Depth` still ships false and is still "not measured".**
   Every step this build compiles is one hop, so no run can exceed
   `max_depth` and nothing here could set it honestly. Task 8's recursive
   walk is what can. Said on the struct rather than left as a silence, and
   the field stays declared because the envelope's shape is a contract
   Task 15's tool description is generated against.

8. **Second round: nothing asserted the budget that actually reached
   Postgres.** Pinning the two constants closed the constant-editing
   route and left the route one step along open: changing
   `budget := s.statementBudget()` in `Run` to `s.statementTimeout` left
   the **whole package green**. On the default path that knob is `0`,
   `runInTx` binds `"0ms"`, and `0` is Postgres's spelling of *no
   timeout* — every production view would have run unbounded while the
   bounds table advertised 5s and 15s. All three tests that touched the
   timeout set the knob, so the default path was asserted by nobody.
   Closed twice, at the two different levels the defect has. In
   production, `runInTx` no longer fires the two settings blind: it reads
   back what `set_config` returned and **refuses a transaction that came
   back with `statement_timeout = 0`**, because an unbounded statement is
   not a view. In the tests, a `Service.observeBounds` hook — unexported,
   written only by this package's bounds tests — reports the values the
   database is holding, and
   `TestTheBudgetPostgresHoldsIsTheOneThisPackageComputed` asserts them
   through `Run`'s own path: unset knob → `5s`, `time.Hour` → `15s`, which
   is the only assertion proving the clamp travels through `Run` rather
   than being a pure function nobody calls. The one-identifier mutation is
   now red in eleven tests, the new one included, with "the transaction
   came back unbounded (statement_timeout=\"0\") for a budget of 0s".

9. **`max_nodes` was documented as a node cap and enforced as a row cap.**
   Correction 4 above chose to count rows, and the direction was right —
   no truncated result was ever reported complete — but the cost went
   unrecorded and was reachable from four sides. The same entity drawn by
   two `nodes` entries differs in `set_name` and `rank`, so `UNION` does
   not collapse it: three quests declared as two overlapping sets came
   back as the identical three nodes, flagged truncated at a cap of three
   *and* at four, and clear at six; six quests over the same two sets were
   flagged at a cap of ten. Being told a whole picture is partial is a
   designer chasing a bound that was never reached, and the trim in Go
   could then hand back fewer nodes than the cap allowed. The repair makes
   rows and nodes the same thing *before* the `LIMIT`: `capOf` now wraps
   the arms in `SELECT DISTINCT ON (id) … ORDER BY id, rank` — the same
   "first entry that claimed it" rule the Go deduplication already applied
   — with `LIMIT cap + 1` and the outer `ORDER BY rank, id` outside it. A
   row is a node, and `rows > cap` is exact in both directions.
   `TestTheNodeCapCountsNodesNotRows` holds both: the three-quest shape at
   caps of 3, 4 and 6 is three nodes and no flag, and the control — six
   quests at a cap of five — is five nodes and a flag. Measured before:
   `cap=3 nodes=3 truncated=true`, `cap=4 nodes=3 truncated=true`; after:
   `truncated=false` at every cap, control still flagged. The three golden
   files move by the wrapper and its renumbering, read before `-update`.
   The reviewer's fourth shape — a non-deterministic surviving node count
   over 23 quests — was **not** reproduced here: the closest construction
   returned 10 nodes on six consecutive runs, which the `ORDER BY rank,
   id` inside the collection point already explains. The dedupe makes the
   count exact whether or not that shape exists.

10. **The statement guard allowed a statement by identifier *name*, and
    watched three pgx methods out of six.** `compiled` matches any
    identifier spelled `statement`, so `statement = statement + " -- " +
    fromACaller` one line above the `Query` left both guards green — the
    fourth instance of the shape Task 6's review named, the guard closing
    the call site and leaving the assignment above it open. The walk now
    takes `*ast.AssignStmt` too: an assignment naming an allowed
    identifier must have a single right side that is a call to
    `compileWith`, with its own vacuity check, since an arm that matches
    nothing lets everything through. Red with "assigns a statement
    identifier from a *ast.BinaryExpr". And the executor set was
    `{Exec, Query, QueryRow}` while `pgx.Batch.Queue`, `Conn.Prepare` and
    `CopyFrom` also take statement text: `batch.Queue("SELECT " +
    fromACaller)` executed with every guard silent, and the vacuity check
    could not see it — `found < 2` fails when a *watched* call disappears,
    not when an unwatched one is added. The set is now a map from method
    name to the index of its statement argument (`Queue` at 0, `Prepare`
    at 2, the rest at 1), and it says in the comment that it is a list of
    the pgx entry points this package uses, to be revisited whenever a new
    one is. Both mutations red: "assembles the statement it passes to
    Queue" and "… to Prepare".

11. **A sub-millisecond budget silently became *no* budget.**
    `timeout.Milliseconds()` truncates, so a 500µs budget bound `"0ms"` —
    unlimited — and the clamp capped the top with no floor. The same
    defect class as 8, one line: `statementBudget` now floors at `1ms`,
    with the reason named, and the floor is asserted twice — as a pure
    function, and as the value the database comes back holding (`1ms`,
    measured through `Run`). The clamp's negative arm is pinned in the
    same test: `budget <= 0` did two jobs and only the unset one was
    asserted, so `-1s → 5s` is now written down.

12. **Node truncation leaves dangling edges, and that is recorded rather
    than repaired.** The edge arms join the set CTEs, not the capped node
    CTE, so trimming nodes does not trim the edges pointing at them:
    measured, a `max_nodes` of 2 over the fixture returns 2 nodes and 3
    edges, 2 of them dangling. Dropping them is not the obvious repair it
    looks like — an `edges: [{between: …}]` entry legitimately draws
    relations between sets the document chose *not* to draw as nodes, so
    "endpoint missing" is a normal, untruncated answer too, and a renderer
    has to tolerate it regardless of any flag. Counting such edges into
    `Truncated.Edges` would then be counting a normal answer as a
    truncation, which is exactly the over-report just removed in 9. So the
    sentence is on `Truncated` instead: an edge's endpoints are not
    guaranteed to be in `Nodes`, and a renderer that wants a closed graph
    filters on `Nodes` itself. Task 9's renderer work is where that filter
    belongs if one is wanted.

**Mutations proved red in the second round**, baseline confirmed running
first (`TEST_DATABASE_URL` set; a run without it skips 728 tests and still
prints `ok`): `Run` passing the raw knob instead of `statementBudget()`
(red in eleven tests); a `statement = statement + …` above the `Query`
(red in the assignment arm); `batch.Queue("SELECT " + …)` and
`tx.Prepare(ctx, "p", "SELECT " + …)` (red in the executor arm). The
row-cap defect was reproduced and re-measured rather than mutated, before
and after, and is quoted in 9.

**One decision this task made that the plan did not name.** The timeout's
advice is carried by an exported `*TimeoutError` that unwraps to the
`pgconn.PgError`, rather than by an `fmt.Errorf` sentence. Both keep
`metamodel.IsRetryable` true; only the type can be matched by
`internal/web`, and it has to be, because `mcpErrorFor`'s retryable arm
**deliberately discards the error's own message** and substitutes a
generic contention sentence. Written as an `fmt.Errorf`, the budget and
the bounds to lower would have been a sentence asserted by a unit test and
read by nobody — the "value nothing reads" this plan keeps refusing. The
arm that carries it is written into Task 15's block; it is not built here,
because no views tool is registered yet and a mechanism in `internal/web`
that nothing reaches would be the same defect one layer down.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each: `pgx.ReadOnly` and the read-only setting both removed (1); the
`cap + 1` reduced to `cap` (2, plus the three golden files); the budget
knob ignored (1); `runFailure` handing the database's error straight back
(1, the envelope loses its advice); the plan's own `SET LOCAL … %d`
restored (1, the new statement guard); `rows.Close()` dropped before
`rows.Err()` (1); `DefaultStatementTimeout` raised to a minute (1, after
the constants were pinned — green before); `is_local` off with the
rollback turned into a commit (1). Nine mutations, nine red, plus the
three confirmed **green** and recorded as such: each read-only half alone
still refuses; the plan's timeout mutation before the constants were
pinned; and `is_local` off on its own, which the rollback covers.
