### Task 8: Multi-hop traversal, wired through `internal/graph`

**Files:**
- Modify: `internal/views/compile.go`
- Create: `internal/views/traverse_test.go`

Task 6's `compileStep` handles `depth.Max == 1` with a plain join. This
task routes every step with `Max > 1` through `graph.WalkCTE`, and it is
where a cycle in a game's content stops being a hang.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

- `TestAMultiHopStepReachesTransitively` — seeds a prerequisite chain of
  four quests, walks `requires` with `depth: {min:1,max:3}`, asserts
  exactly the three ancestors and **not** the fourth. The negative half
  is what makes the depth bound assert something.
- `TestACycleInContentIsDrawnRatherThanHung` — makes `a requires b`,
  `b requires c`, `c requires a`, runs with `max_depth: 12`, asserts the
  run returns three nodes and three edges and does not time out. The core
  spec deliberately allows this content; the analysis engine exists to
  report it; the view must be able to *draw* it.
- `TestMinDepthDropsTheNearHops` — `depth: {min:2,max:3}` returns the
  grandparents and not the parents, with a control run at `min:1` in the
  same test showing the parents are reachable.
- `TestDirectionAnyWalksBothWaysWithoutDoubling` — the views-level
  counterpart of the `internal/graph` test, over real content.
- `TestTruncatedDepthIsFlagged` — a chain longer than `max_depth` sets
  `Truncated.Depth`.
- `TestAWalkStaysInsideOneGame` — the isolation test, with its positive
  control, because a recursive term is where this is easiest to lose.

- [ ] **Step 2: Implement `compileStep`'s recursive arm**

```go
// compileStep emits one traversal step. A step with max depth 1 is a
// plain join; anything deeper goes through internal/graph, which owns the
// recursion, its project filter, its path guard and its depth bound.
//
// The seed is the *set this step reads from*, which is already a CTE, so
// SeedSQL is a select over that CTE and carries no caller text at all.
// graph.WalkCTE renumbers its binds, which is why the seed is written
// with its own numbering here.
func compileStep(b *builder, projectID uuid.UUID, r *Resolved, step ResolvedStep, name string) (string, error) {
	if step.Step.Depth.Max <= 1 {
		return compileHop(b, projectID, r, step, name), nil
	}
	walk := graph.Walk{
		Name:            name + "_w",
		ProjectID:       projectID,
		SeedSQL:         "SELECT id FROM " + cteOf(r, step.FromSet),
		RelationTypeIDs: step.RelationTypeIDs,
		Direction:       graph.Direction(step.Step.Direction),
		MinDepth:        step.Step.Depth.Min,
		MaxDepth:        step.Step.Depth.Max,
		// The intermediate cap is four times the node cap, so a
		// pathological branching factor cannot build a giant intermediate
		// before the outer limit applies. Four rather than one because a
		// walk legitimately visits a node at several depths before the
		// outer DISTINCT collapses them.
		MaxRows: r.Limits.MaxNodes * 4,
	}
	sql, args := graph.WalkCTE(walk)
	b.adopt(args) // renumbered by WalkCTE against the builder's current count
	...
}
```

`builder.adopt` is new in this task: `WalkCTE` numbers from `$1`, so the
builder hands it its current argument count and appends the returned
arguments. Write it, and pin the renumbering with
`TestAWalkCTEsBindsAreRenumberedIntoTheOuterStatement`, which compiles a
query with a predicate *before* and *after* a recursive step and asserts
both still match — a renumbering bug shows up as the wrong quest coming
back, which is exactly the wrong-answer-without-an-error class this
project keeps producing.

The step's own `to_type`, `where` and `edge_where` filter the walk's
output rather than its recursion: `to_type` and `where` apply to the
reached entity and are applied outside the CTE; `edge_where` applies to
the relation each hop walked, which is inside, so it is passed to
`graph.Walk` as an extra join predicate — **and that is a change to
`internal/graph`'s API this task makes**: `Walk.EdgePredicate string` plus
`EdgeArgs []any`, documented there as "the caller's own SQL over the alias
`r`, containing no caller text".

- [ ] **Step 3: Run, see red, commit**

Break the path guard again from the views side and watch
`TestACycleInContentIsDrawnRatherThanHung` hang; break `MinDepth`'s outer
filter and watch `TestMinDepthDropsTheNearHops` fail with the parents
present.

```bash
git add internal/views/compile.go internal/views/traverse_test.go internal/graph/walk.go
git commit -m "feat: multi-hop traversal through the shared bounded walk"
```

**Corrections from implementing Task 8** (landed as `0052c66`, `aef9fd2`,
`c843393`, `f2be790` and `0fa5c3c`; every mutation named below was applied
to the shipped code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored,
with the baseline confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`,
`TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository). Nine
findings. Two of them change a guard three tasks now rest on, and one
changes what a later task has to describe.

1. **The project-filter guard passed with the walk's relation filter
   deleted, because an alias can be the *suffix* of another one.** The
   test asked whether `<alias>.project_id = $1` appears in the block as a
   substring, and `far.project_id = $1` **contains** the text
   `r.project_id = $1` — so the entity join beside it reported the
   relation alias `r` as filtered. That is precisely the pair
   `internal/graph`'s recursive term emits, and the filter it hid is the
   **first project filter in this package that is not redundant**: with
   `r.project_id = $1` gone the walk follows another game's edge between
   two of this game's quests and returns them, which is the shape Task 6's
   review recorded as impossible under any statement that build emitted.
   The guard now matches on a word boundary (`filteredOn`), and the
   mutation is red in it as well as in the behavioural test. Nine
   findings' worth of guard rebuilding, and the hole was one character of
   regexp.

2. **The plan's prescribed mutation for the cycle test does not fail, and
   does not hang.** "Break the path guard again from the views side and
   watch `TestACycleInContentIsDrawnRatherThanHung` hang" — removing
   `NOT w.closed` leaves the test **green** and the run at the same
   duration, because the depth bound terminates the walk on its own
   (`internal/graph`'s own doc comment says exactly this, and the plan's
   step 3 contradicts it), and the node and edge sets are deduplicated
   before anybody counts them. `Stats.MaxDepthReached` survives it too:
   `capOf` deduplicates with `ORDER BY id, rank, depth`, so a node kept at
   two distances keeps the shorter. The assertion that *is* red is
   `Truncated.Depth`: without the guard the cycle is re-entered once per
   level to the bound and past it, so a whole picture is reported partial.
   The test carries that assertion and the explanation now. This is the
   third prescribed mutation in this plan to be wrong about the path it
   reaches.

3. **`Truncated.Depth` is measured with the same cap + 1 idea as the
   other two flags, and the alternative was refused.** Each multi-hop step
   is walked one hop *past* what it asked for, the extra hop is dropped
   before the picture is built, and its existence is the flag. The obvious
   cheaper reading — "the deepest node came back at max_depth" — is an
   over-report on the commonest shape there is, a chain that ends exactly
   at the bound, which is the same defect correction 9 of Task 7 removed
   from the node cap. Both halves are asserted, and the inferred version
   (`depth >= max` in the probe) is red on the control.
   Two limits are stated on `Truncated` rather than left silent: the flag
   is about the *walk*, not the picture, so a hop the bound refused whose
   far entity `to_type` would have dropped still sets it; and a **one-hop
   step is not probed at all**, because a step asking for exactly one hop
   is a neighbour query and probing it would cost a second scan of
   `relations` on the commonest query in the language.

4. **The flag travels as its own row, not as a column on every row.** A
   `'depth_truncated'` arm of the final `UNION ALL` emits zero rows or
   one, and a query with no multi-hop step emits **no arm**, which is why
   a flat query cannot report a depth it never measured. The alternative
   considered was a constant boolean column cross-joined onto every node
   and edge, which pays per row for one bit and is emitted even when
   nothing recurses. The probe reads the *recursion* CTE and not
   `ReadFrom`'s wrapper: the wrapper's row cap is `ORDER BY depth`, so the
   deepest rows are the first it drops, and a row-capped walk would
   otherwise lose the evidence — such a run reports a node truncation
   instead, which is the larger fact, and that is written on the struct.

5. **`Stats.MaxDepthReached` is now a column on the row, and the depth is
   absolute.** Task 6's correction 7 asked for this and Task 7 deferred
   it. `rowColumns` gains `depth` — **last**, so `rank` stays the eleventh
   column and the final `ORDER BY 1, 11, 2` does not move — a selector's
   rows carry 0, a one-hop step carries `near.depth + 1`, and a walk
   carries its seed row's depth plus the walk's own. The seed's depth is
   found through `path[1]`, the id the walk anchored on, which is the only
   way a step reading from another step can count from the *seed
   selector* rather than from its own start:
   `TestAWalkFromAWalkCountsItsDepthFromTheSeed` is red with the addition
   removed, reporting 2 for a node four hops out. The old arithmetic —
   source set's depth plus the step's *declared* max — is deleted, and
   `TestMaxDepthReachedIsWhatTheWalkReachedNotWhatItAskedFor` is what it
   would fail: a walk that asked for four hops and found one reported
   four.

6. **`internal/graph` grew `EdgePredicate`/`EdgeArgs` as the plan said,
   and the placement is asserted with the node that separates the two
   placements.** A condition on the relation prunes the recursion; applied
   to the walk's *output* — where `to_type` and `where` legitimately live
   — every node behind an excluded edge would still be reached and
   returned. `TestAnEdgePredicateIsAppliedInsideTheRecursion` seeds
   `a -> b -> c`, excludes the first edge and asserts **c**, not merely
   that the answer narrowed. The predicate is parenthesised, with its own
   test, because it is ANDed into the join that carries the relation-type
   filter and a caller's `a OR b` would otherwise make that filter
   optional.

7. **The splice keeps `$1` meaning the project id, which is a property
   two packages' guards depend on and not a convenience.** `WalkCTE`
   numbers from `$1` with the project id there; this compiler's `$1` is
   the same value; so `builder.adopt` maps `$1` onto `$1` and shifts the
   rest to the end of its own argument list (`graph.Renumber`, exported
   for this one caller shape and given a test that it leaves `$1` alone).
   Renumbering `$1` too would have left every `project_id = $1` in the
   walk's text reading some other argument — and both packages' isolation
   guards look for that filter *by name*, so they would have gone quiet at
   the same time. The identity is checked in `adopt` rather than trusted:
   a builder whose `$1` is not the walk's project id is an error, not a
   picture from the wrong game. The off-by-one is red in three tests.

8. **`edge_where` is compiled against its own argument list.** The
   builder's `bind` numbers from the outer statement, and `WalkCTE`
   renumbers what it is given — so a predicate compiled the usual way
   would be renumbered a second time and compare the right column against
   the wrong value. `compiler.subPredicate` swaps in a fresh builder for
   the duration, which is the same trick the seed uses and is why both
   arrive at `graph` numbered from `$1`.

9. **One guard-adjacent thing that is *not* asserted, recorded rather
   than dressed up.** The from-set is grouped by id before a walk's depth
   is added back to it, taking each seed's shortest depth; removing the
   grouping leaves the whole suite **green**, because `capOf` deduplicates
   by id with `ORDER BY id, rank, depth` and keeps the shallowest row
   anyway. It stays because it stops this CTE holding one row per
   *spelling* of its seed, which is work rather than an answer — and the
   same argument produced a change that *is* observable in the emitted
   text: the walk's seed is `SELECT DISTINCT id`, so a walk anchors once
   per entity its seed set names rather than once per edge traversal that
   named it.

**Three things decided here that the plan did not name:**

- **A multi-hop step's own filters are split, and the split is the design
  of `walk()`.** `edge_where` prunes the recursion; `to_type`, `where` and
  the invalid-row exclusion filter its output. A walk that pruned on
  `to_type` could not pass *through* a zone to reach a quest, which is a
  picture the language promises;
  `TestAWalkDrawsOnlyItsDestinationTypeAndOnlyValidRows` seeds exactly
  that zone in the middle, with both controls.
- **The refusal Task 6 shipped is replaced by an assertion rather than
  deleted.** `TestAMultiHopStepEmitsARecursionRatherThanASecondJoin`
  asserts the recursion, its path guard and the wrapper as text, with a
  control that a one-hop step is still a plain join: a compiler that
  quietly emitted its own second join for a depth-3 step would still
  return rows, and only the shape of the statement says which question was
  asked.
- **The worked example is §3.2's own `depth: {min:1,max:4}` again.** The
  third golden file now holds a recursion, an edge predicate inside it and
  the renumbering that splices both into the statement around them —
  Task 6's comment said that depth was Task 8's, and this is Task 8.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each: the walk's relation project filter (2 — the behavioural isolation
test, and the text guard only after finding 1 was fixed); the walk's
far-entity project filter and this compiler's own, deleted together (1 —
each alone is covered by the other, which is defence in depth working as
intended and is why the pair had to be mutated together); the anchor's
project filter (3, across both packages); the closing row suppressed
(1 — two edges for a three-cycle); `MinDepth` passed as 0 (2); the probe
hop not walked (1); the probe inferred as `depth >= max` (1, on the
control); the depth ceiling raised so the probe hop reaches the picture
(2); the seed's depth not added back (1); `edge_where` compiled and never
handed to the walk (2, one of them the golden file); the renumbering off
by one (3); the edge predicate applied to the walk's output rather than
its recursion (1, in `internal/graph`); the edge predicate dropped (2).
Twenty-two mutations, twenty-two red — plus **two confirmed green and
recorded as such**: the plan's own path-guard mutation before the
`Truncated.Depth` assertion was added (finding 2), and the from-set
grouping (finding 9).

**Two corrections to the round-1 notes above.**

- Finding 9 said the from-set `GROUP BY` is observed by nothing. It is
  observed by the **golden file**: removing the grouping is red on
  `testdata/mmorpg_progression.sql` and green everywhere else. The
  distinction matters, because the rule this project applies is about
  mechanisms *nothing* reads, and a golden file is a reader. Reworded in
  `compiler.walk`'s comment to "only the golden file observes it".
- Finding 2's new `Truncated.Depth` assertion in
  `TestACycleInContentIsDrawnRatherThanHung` is a good assertion but a
  **general** one: it detects "the walk went deeper than it should have",
  which a mutation to the depth ceiling fires too. The path guard's
  *specific* signature is `internal/graph`'s row count — eleven rows for
  a three-node cycle against four. Said so in the test.

**Corrections made during implementation, round 2** (a second review pass
over the six commits above, every reproduction run against the live
database before and after):

1. **The walk's row cap dropped content with every flag saying the
   picture was complete.** `MaxRows: max_nodes * 4` makes `internal/graph`
   emit `LIMIT MaxRows + 1`, and that package documents the extra row as
   *the* mechanism by which a caller detects truncation — **its only
   caller never read it**. A walk row is an edge *traversal*, so four
   times the node cap collapses to far fewer than `max_nodes` nodes, and
   neither the node cap nor the edge cap fires either. Measured on
   `a -> n1` plus a seven-clique `n1…n7` (22 relations), one step
   `direction: any, depth {min:1,max:4}`, `max_edges: 1000`: at
   `max_nodes: 8` the answer held **20** edges, at 9 and at 5000 it held
   **22**, and all three reported `{Nodes:false Edges:false Depth:*}`.
   Two relations vanished, silently, and which two is undefined —
   `internal/graph` says order within a depth is unspecified. This was
   new in Task 8 and is the silent-content-loss failure mode this plan
   calls its worst. **The overflow is now read**, the same way the depth
   probe reads its extra hop: the step's own CTE takes
   `FROM (SELECT * FROM w_out LIMIT $rowCap)`, so the overflow row never
   reaches the picture, and a `walk_truncated` arm — `EXISTS (SELECT 1
   FROM w_out OFFSET $rowCap)` — carries the fact back. After: 20 edges
   with `{Nodes:true Edges:true}`, 22 with the same at 9, 22 with all
   flags false at 5000.
   `TestAWalkRowCapIsReportedRatherThanLosingContentSilently` pins it,
   with the large cap as its control, and is red — `{false false false}`
   — with the probe arm removed.

   **It sets both element flags, and the over-report cannot be removed.**
   A dropped walk row carried a node *and* an edge, and which of the two
   the picture actually came up short of — if either; the cut traversals
   may all have reached things another row also reached — is not
   knowable, because the walk hands back exactly one row past its cap and
   no more. Both flags are set rather than a guess made. `Truncated`'s
   doc says so at the one position where `Nodes` and `Edges` are not
   exact.

2. **`Truncated.Depth` reported a complete picture as partial on any
   dense or cyclic graph.** The probe asked "did the recursion produce a
   row past the bound", not "is anything missing". On a complete graph of
   six seeded at one of them, `depth {min:1,max:4}`: all six nodes and
   all fifteen edges drawn — the entire graph — with
   `{Nodes:false Edges:false Depth:true}`. Deeper simple paths exist in a
   dense graph long after they stop reaching anything new, so this is the
   same over-report round 1's finding 3 refused, moved from chains to
   cliques and to cycles longer than the bound; the suite's only cycle
   fixture is a 3-cycle at max 12, too small to show it. The predicate is
   now the honest one — **is there a node *or an edge* past the bound
   that the walk does not already hold within it** — and it costs
   nothing (below). After: the same query reports
   `{false false false}` with the identical picture.
   `TestACompletePictureOfADenseGraphIsNotDepthTruncated` pins both
   halves: a clique of six with a four-quest tail hung off it flags at
   `max: 4`, where the tail's last quest is genuinely one hop further,
   and does not flag at `max: 5`, where the whole graph is drawn and only
   the clique's deeper paths remain. It is red against the old predicate.

3. **The project-filter guard was breakable a third way: `\$1` matches
   the prefix of `$10`.** Mutating `graph.Renumber` to renumber `$1` too,
   with six extra predicate leaves in the guard's query so the walk
   carries ten binds before it, gave `anchor.project_id = $18`,
   `far.project_id = $18`, `r.project_id = $18` — three filters reading
   an arbitrary argument — and `--- PASS`. Today the same mutation is
   loud elsewhere only because the value at that position happens not to
   be a uuid. `filteredOn` now closes the placeholder on the right,
   `\$1(?:[^0-9]|$)`, as a character class rather than the lookahead Go's
   regexp does not have; the alias was already closed on the left for the
   same reason. Re-run after the fix, the mutation is red on all three
   positions. **This was the twelfth instance of the recurring defect and
   the third time this one guard was broken by review**, so the rest of
   the regexp was read rather than the one hole patched — which is
   finding 4.

4. **The same guard could not see three shapes of table reference.**
   `tableReference` required an identifier after the table name, so
   `FROM relations)` (no alias), `FROM entities, relations r` (a
   comma-joined list, which hid every table after the comma) and
   `FROM "relations" q` (quoted) matched nothing and were asserted by
   nothing. Task 9 adds `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` next. The alias group is now
   optional and the name may be quoted; a reference that yields **no
   alias is an error rather than a non-match**; a comma-joined list is
   refused outright with "write it as a JOIN". The scan is factored into
   `projectFilterProblems`, and
   `TestTheProjectFilterGuardSeesTheShapesItMustSee` runs it over all
   five shapes plus the `$18` one, with three controls: a filtered
   reference, a quoted filtered one, and a `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` written
   the way this compiler writes joins.

   **One known over-strictness is pinned rather than left to be
   discovered**: the scan splits on the word `SELECT`, so a filter
   written *after* a nested `SELECT` in the same block lands in the next
   block and is not seen. Nothing this compiler emits is shaped that way,
   and the failure is loud, which is the side of the trade a guard
   belongs on — but Task 9's lateral joins have to keep the filter ahead
   of the nested `SELECT`, as the control does.

5. **Recorded, not fixed: `filteredOn` cannot see that the text it
   matched is live SQL.** `-- r.project_id = $1` in a comment, or the
   same inside a string literal, satisfies it. Unreachable today — this
   compiler emits neither — and it is the next step along rather than a
   reason to build a tokeniser this package has no other use for.

6. **The probe hop's cost is stated where the hop is asked for.** Round
   1's finding 3 rejected probing a one-hop step because it "would cost a
   second scan of `relations`" and then accepted a whole extra
   breadth-first level for multi-hop steps without measuring it. Measured
   on this project's Postgres, an eight-entity clique walked `any` at
   `depth {min:1,max:4}`, best of twenty-one runs: **36.1 ms as emitted
   against 18.3 ms with `MaxDepth: Depth.Max`** — a factor of 1.97,
   because the extra level of a clique is wider than every level before
   it put together. The mechanism stays; the number is now on `MaxDepth`
   in `compiler.walk`, beside the note that a one-hop step is not probed
   for the same argument run the other way. The honest predicate of
   finding 2 is free beside it: 36.1 ms against 34.8 ms for the bare
   row test it replaced, inside the run-to-run spread.

7. **`Truncated.Depth` is false for a one-hop step, and a test now
   observes it.** `max: 1` from the head of the five-quest chain returns
   `[c2]` with `Depth: false` while c3, c4 and c5 lie further on; `max: 2`
   returns `[c2 c3]` with `Depth: true`. That is documented and
   defensible, but Task 7 shipped `Truncated` with "all-false means not
   measured" and the meaning is now **per step**, with only the prose
   saying which — and `TestTruncatedDepthIsFlagged`'s last clause covered
   a query with *no* traversal, not a one-hop one. The one-hop case is
   now in that test.

8. **A comment in `execute.go` was wrong twice and is rewritten.** It
   said a walk that hit its row cap "can also lose the evidence — the
   walk's own cap keeps the shallowest rows, so the probe's are the first
   to go", and that "such a run reports a node truncation instead". The
   probe reads `w1`, the recursion, not the capped `w1_out` — `walk()`
   says so explicitly and gives that as its reason — so the row cap
   cannot lose the depth evidence; and "reports a node truncation
   instead" is exactly what finding 1 shows did not happen. It now says
   the two flags are independent and neither substitutes for the other.

**Recorded for Task 9 and Task 15.** An empty `{"any":[]}` compiles to
`true`, so an `edge_where` of "no conditions" follows **every** edge
where "follow none" is the arguably-correct reading. Pre-existing from
Task 6's `combine`, where it only ever widened a projection; since Task 8
the same `true` prunes a recursion. Recorded on `combine` itself. The
change belongs with the validation pass that would refuse an empty list
outright — Task 15's — and a silent flip of the identity element between
tasks is worse than either reading: say why before changing it.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running with
`-v` before each: the honest depth predicate reverted to "is there a row
past the bound" (1); the `walk_truncated` probe arm removed (1);
`graph.Renumber` renumbering `$1`, in the guard's shipped query and in a
ten-bind version of it (1, both spellings). Three mutations, three red.

---

### Task 9: Projection — labels, colours, the one-hop related attribute and its ambiguity

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/project.go`
- Create: `internal/views/project_test.go`
- Modify: `internal/views/execute.go`

This is what makes *"coloured by zone"* work: the colour is not a property
of the quest, it is the name of the zone one hop away.

Restricting it to one hop is deliberate — a multi-hop colour source is a
traversal, and traversals belong in `traverse` where they are bounded and
visible in the document. And when the hop yields several entities, **the
first by name is used and the node carries `ambiguous: true`**: silently
picking one and saying nothing would produce a map that is wrong in a way
nobody can see.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

`internal/views/project_test.go`:

- `TestALabelDefaultsToTheEntityName` — no `project` in the document, and
  every node's `Attrs["label"]` is its name.
- `TestAProjectedFieldAppearsInAttrsAndNotInFields` — `color_by:
  "min_level"` puts `22` in `Attrs["color_by"]`, and `Fields` stays nil
  because `include_fields` was not asked for. **This is a read-back
  step**: it asserts the value came out, not that the query ran.
- `TestAOneHopRelatedAttributeReadsTheFarEntity` — the spec's own case:
  `color_by: {related: {via: "takes_place_in", direction: "out", type:
  "zone", attr: "@name"}}` gives Hogger `"Elwynn Forest"` and Defias
  `"Westfall"`, and the quest with no zone gets no attribute rather than
  an empty string, so "unset" and "the empty zone name" stay
  distinguishable.
- `TestAnAmbiguousHopIsMarkedRatherThanSilentlyPicked` — a quest given
  two `takes_place_in` edges gets the alphabetically first zone **and**
  `Ambiguous: true`; the single-zone quest in the same result has
  `Ambiguous: false`, which is the control that stops an always-true flag
  passing.
- `TestIncludeFieldsReturnsTheWholePayloadAndTheDefaultDoesNot` — the
  token-discipline rule, asserted both ways.
- `TestAProjectedFieldOfAnUndeclaredKeyIsRefusedAtResolution` — belongs
  to Task 4's pass and is asserted here because this is the task that
  makes projections reachable.

- [ ] **Step 2: Implement**

The one-hop attribute compiles to a `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` per projection
slot, ordered by `far.name, far.id` and limited to two rows, so the
`ambiguous` flag is **detected** rather than inferred — the same
`cap + 1` trick truncation uses, and for the same reason:

```sql
LEFT JOIN LATERAL (
    SELECT far.name AS value, count(*) OVER () AS matches
    FROM relations rel
    JOIN entities far ON far.id = CASE WHEN $d = 'out' THEN rel.target_id ELSE rel.source_id END
                     AND far.project_id = $1
    WHERE rel.project_id = $1
      AND rel.relation_type_id = $rt
      AND (CASE WHEN $d = 'out' THEN rel.source_id ELSE rel.target_id END) = n.id
      AND far.entity_type_id = $et
    ORDER BY far.name, far.id
    LIMIT 2
) AS color_by ON true
```

with `matches > 1` becoming `Ambiguous`. A `LEFT` join, not an inner one,
so a node with no far entity keeps its row: dropping it would silently
narrow the result, which is a different query.

- [ ] **Step 3: See red**

Change the `LIMIT 2` to `LIMIT 1`. Expected:
`TestAnAmbiguousHopIsMarkedRatherThanSilentlyPicked` fails with
`Ambiguous was false` — the exact defect the flag exists to prevent.
Change the `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` to a plain `JOIN LATERAL`. Expected:
`TestAOneHopRelatedAttributeReadsTheFarEntity` fails because the zoneless
quest disappeared.

- [ ] **Step 4: Commit**

```bash
git add internal/views/project.go internal/views/project_test.go internal/views/execute.go
git commit -m "feat: projection attributes, the one-hop related hop and its ambiguity flag"
```

**Corrections from implementing Task 9** (landed as `9c4c1df`, `caf95f6`
and `35de5da`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped
code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the
baseline confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`,
`TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository). Nine
findings. One of them replaces the block this task gives.

1. **The plan's `LIMIT 2` returns two rows and duplicates the node row,
   and its `matches` is no better for it.** A `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` that
   yields two rows yields two rows *of the node*: the arm emits one per
   far entity, they differ in `attrs` so `UNION` cannot collapse them,
   and what collapses them instead is `capOf`'s
   `DISTINCT ON (id) … ORDER BY id, rank, depth` — which names no
   attribute, so **which of the two zones survives is unspecified**. It
   was the first one on this Postgres in every run, which is the same
   "green three runs out of three" that Task 6's correction 8 had to pin
   as text. Measured rather than reasoned about, on a `VALUES` fixture of
   one node with two far entities:

   ```
    id |  name  |     value     | matches
   ----+--------+---------------+---------
     1 | hogger | Elwynn Forest |       2
     1 | hogger | Westfall      |       2
     2 | defias | Westfall      |       1
   ```

   Note the second column of that measurement: a window function is
   computed **before** `ORDER BY` and `LIMIT`, so `count(*) OVER ()` is
   already the true number of candidates with `LIMIT 1`. What ships is
   `LIMIT 1` with that count, which is not a weaker detection than
   cap + 1 but a stronger one — `matches` is exact rather than capped at
   two — and it costs nothing, because finding the first far entity *by
   name* has to sort the matches anyway, so stopping at two saves no
   work. The plan's step-3 mutation ("`LIMIT 2` → `LIMIT 1`, expect
   `Ambiguous was false`") therefore describes a change that is not the
   defect: applied to the shipped code the equivalent mutation is
   `count(*) OVER ()` → `1`, which is red with exactly that message. The
   plan's own `LIMIT 2` restored is **green** on every behavioural test
   and is recorded here as such — it is the fourth prescribed mutation in
   this plan not to reach the path it names. The node-count assertion the
   ambiguity test now carries (a quest in two zones is still one node) is
   what would have caught it had the collapse not been silent.

2. **`project.fields` and `edges[].label_from` were knobs nothing read,
   and both are wired here rather than left for a later task.** `Edge.Label`
   said in its own doc comment that Task 9 fills it, and no later task
   mentions either. `label_from` compiles to the relation's own field or
   its type's key, resolved against the relation types the entry actually
   draws — the `via` spelling names them, the `from_step` spelling
   inherits the step's, read from the catalogue rather than through
   `relationType` so one reference does not become two `TypeRef`s and
   report one broken dependency twice.

   **`project.fields` fills the payload, not `attrs`.** The two members
   ask for different things — content under the game's own keys, and a
   presentation attribute under the slot's name — and merging them would
   let a field called `color_by` overwrite the colour, which is a game's
   vocabulary breaking a renderer's. `include_fields` still wins, because
   the whole payload is a superset of any list.

3. **A projection's rule for a field key is not a predicate's rule, and
   the difference had to be decided rather than inherited.**
   `fieldScope.field` refuses a key that is not declared on *every* type
   in scope, because a comparison against a type that does not declare it
   silently matches nothing there. A projection has no such failure mode:
   a node whose type does not declare the key carries no attribute, which
   is the same "unset" a node with no value carries, and "colour the
   quests by min_level, the zones have none" is a picture a designer
   legitimately asks for. So `projectionScope.declares` is *declared by
   at least one type in scope*, which still refuses the case worth
   refusing — a key nothing declares, which is a typo, and a typo
   answered with a picture in one flat colour is the silent-empty failure
   §3 calls the most expensive one. Two scopes cannot judge at all and
   say so instead of guessing: a query with a `traverse` step that names
   no `to_type` reaches entities of any type, and a hop with no `type`
   reaches whatever the relation reaches.

4. **Unset and empty stay distinguishable because the attributes travel
   as jsonb.** `jsonb_strip_nulls(jsonb_build_object(…))` means a slot
   that found nothing is *absent* from `attrs` rather than present and
   empty, and a projected number arrives as a number rather than as the
   text of one — a renderer sizing or ordering by a projected field would
   otherwise be comparing strings. The same applies to the payload
   `project.fields` builds. A stored JSON `null` reads as unset, which is
   the reading every other stage of this package gives it.

5. **The one-hop hop takes no conditions, which is this task's answer to
   the question Task 8 left it.** Task 8 recorded that an empty
   `{"any":[]}` compiles to `true`, so an `edge_where` of "no conditions"
   follows every edge where "follow none" is arguably right, and asked
   whether the related-attribute hop inherits the same reading. It does
   not, because the identity element never arises there: `RelHop` is
   `via`, `direction`, `type` and `attr` and has **no predicate member at
   all**. A colour source that needs filtering is a traversal, and
   traversals belong in `traverse` where they are bounded and drawn —
   the same argument that makes the hop one hop. The open question is
   unchanged and still Task 15's: refusing an empty list outright is the
   right place to settle it, and a silent flip of the identity element
   between tasks is worse than either reading.

6. **`Node.Ambiguous` is a property of the node, not of a slot**, and
   that is a decision rather than an omission. A node with two related
   slots, one of them ambiguous, is flagged and does not say which:
   saying it would mean a second attrs-shaped map that every renderer
   would have to read to draw anything, for a distinction a designer
   resolves by looking at the query.

7. **The far entity is excluded when it is invalid, and the exclusion
   follows `include_invalid`.** A picture that excludes rows the
   metamodel flagged as no longer fitting their schema should not be
   coloured by one either — the same rule a step applies to its
   destination. `TestARelatedHopDoesNotColourWithAnInvalidEntity` holds
   both halves, and the control is the same query with `include_invalid`.

8. **The `COALESCE` on the ambiguity term is observed only by the golden
   file, and that is recorded rather than dressed up.** Removing it —
   `p1.matches > 1` in place of `COALESCE(p1.matches, 0) > 1` — leaves
   every behavioural test green: a null boolean scans into a `*bool` of
   nil, which `Run` already reads as false, and `null OR true` is true.
   It stays because the column then means "not ambiguous" rather than
   "unknown" to anything that reads the statement instead of `Run` — a
   later arm that ANDs the term, or a `WHERE` over it, would drop the
   unmatched rows. This is the same distinction Task 8's finding 9 drew:
   a golden file is a reader.

9. **The project-filter guard's known over-strictness was not reached,
   and the reason is written into the code that could have reached it.**
   Task 8 recorded that the guard splits on the word `SELECT`, so a
   filter written after a nested `SELECT` in the same block is invisible
   to it, and named Task 9's lateral joins as the shape that would meet
   it. It does not: the hop's three references — `relations`, `entities`
   and, for an `attr` of `@type`, `entity_types` — each carry their own
   filter in the `JOIN` or `WHERE` that introduces them, and there is no
   nested `SELECT` inside the lateral at all. `@type` on the far entity
   is a **join** to `entity_types` rather than the scalar subquery
   `typeLeaf` uses, which is what keeps it that way, and `relatedHop`'s
   comment says so as a requirement rather than as a style. The guard is
   left as Task 8 built it; it is not patched for a shape nothing emits.

   What did change is **what the guard is run over**:
   `TestEveryTableReferenceIsProjectFiltered`'s query now carries a
   one-hop related attribute with `attr: "@type"`, so the three
   references inside the lateral are counted by the canonical guard and
   not only by this task's own text test. All three are red under their
   own deletion — the far entity's filter, the relation's, and the far
   entity type's — each reported three times, once per node arm.

**Two things decided here that the plan did not name:**

- **The attributes ride in one `jsonb` column and an edge's label rides
  in the same one**, under the single key `label`, rather than each
  taking a column of the row. A column one arm fills is a column every
  other arm has to spell as a typed null, and the two are the same thing
  anyway: a presentation attribute read off the row. `rowColumns` gains
  `attrs` and `ambiguous` **after** `depth`, for the reason Task 8 added
  `depth` last — `rank` stays the eleventh column and the final
  `ORDER BY 1, 11, 2` does not move.
- **A related hop defaults its `direction` and its `attr` in
  `applyDefaults`**, where every other default is filled, and the
  compiler *refuses* an empty one rather than defaulting it a second
  time. That is the rule Task 6's review settled for a step and an edge
  entry, applied to the third position that has a direction. Both
  defaults are pinned by `TestARelatedHopWithoutATypeReadsEveryNeighbour`,
  which writes neither, and both are red when their line is deleted.

**Recorded for Task 15.** Four things its tool description has to say,
because an agent cannot infer them from the document's shape: `attrs` is
keyed by the *slot name* (`label`, `color_by`, …) and never by the field
key a slot names, so a renderer reads `attrs.color_by` whatever the
document put there; `ambiguous` is per node and says only that *some*
related slot had more than one candidate; and a projected key must be
declared by at least one type the query draws, which is a weaker rule
than the one a predicate's field obeys — the two look identical in the
document and refuse different things.

The fourth belongs beside that last rule, because it is the rule's
escape hatch and it is wider than it looks: **a single `traverse` step
with no `to_type` switches the check off for the entire projection**, not
merely for the part of the graph that step reaches. `nodeScopeOf` sets
`open` if *any* step lacks `to_type`, and `projectionScope.declares`
then returns before consulting a schema, so a misspelt key is accepted
for the types the document *did* name, and for `project.fields` as well.
The answer is the flat one-colour picture the whole rule exists to
prevent. An agent composing a broad "everything this quest touches" view
will meet this, and the tool description has to say that an untyped step
buys reach at the cost of every typo check in `project`.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each: `LEFT JOIN LATERAL` reduced to `JOIN LATERAL` (3 — the zoneless
quest disappears, the direction control finds no row, and the text
assertion); `count(*) OVER ()` replaced by `1` (2); `jsonb_strip_nulls`
dropped (3 + all three golden files — an absent attribute becomes a
present null); the hop's far-entity project filter deleted (2, one of
them the canonical guard, three references each); the hop's relation
project filter deleted (2, likewise); the far entity type's project
filter deleted (1, the canonical guard); the ambiguity term hard-wired
to `false` (1); `project.fields` ignored (1); `label_from` ignored (1);
`ORDER BY far.name, far.id` deleted (1, text — which of two zones is
used is otherwise unspecified); `projectionScope.declares` always
accepting (3, the three positions that use it); the hop's direction
default deleted (1); the hop's attr default deleted (1); the hop's
invalid-row exclusion deleted (1). Fourteen mutations, all red — plus
**two confirmed green and recorded as such**: the plan's own `LIMIT 2`
(finding 1), and the `COALESCE` on the ambiguity term (finding 8).

**Corrections from reviewing Task 9** (landed as `5bd59a2` and `57a71e9`,
on top of `9c4c1df`, `caf95f6`, `35de5da` and `4fe1dd0`; the review
confirmed the rest of the task, including both items recorded above as
green).

1. **The ambiguity flag counted edges, not far entities, and
   `direction: "any"` made it lie.** The `count(*) OVER () AS matches`
   counts *rows*. For `out` and `in` the unique index on
   `(relation_type_id, source_id, target_id)` makes one row one far
   entity, so those were always right. For `any` the anchor is
   `(rel.source_id = e.id OR rel.target_id = e.id)`, so **a reciprocal
   pair of the same relation type is two rows reaching one far entity**,
   and the node was flagged ambiguous with a single candidate to choose
   from. Reproduced on the live database by adding `hogger requires
   defias` beside the fixture's `defias requires hogger` and hopping
   `via: requires, direction: any, type: quest`:
   `color_by="The Defias Brotherhood" ambiguous=true`.

   That contradicts the three places that say the flag is about
   *entities* — `Node.Ambiguous`'s doc, `project.go`'s header and this
   plan — and `any` is the natural spelling for a symmetric relation
   type, of which `connects_to` is one the project's own instructions
   name. Two edges to one zone is not a colour a designer has to
   resolve, and a flag that fires where there is nothing to resolve is
   one designers learn to ignore, which costs exactly what a flag that
   never fires costs. So the reading stands and the SQL was wrong.

   **The fix is a `GROUP BY` before the window**, since a window is
   computed after grouping: `GROUP BY far.id, far.name`, and
   `, fet.key` as well when `attr` is `@type`, because `fet.key` is not
   functionally dependent on `far.id` the way `far.*` and
   `far.fields -> $n` are. `TestAReciprocalPairIsOneFarEntityNotTwo` is
   the new test — that shape had none, which is why it shipped — and it
   is red against the old SQL with both ends of the pair reported. The
   two controls stay in the test beside it: two distinct zones still
   report `true` with a stable winner, one zone still reports `false`.
   Only `mmorpg_quests.sql` moved among the golden files.

2. **"No nested `SELECT` in this construct" was a requirement nothing
   enforced, and the reason it was said to matter turned out to be
   wrong.** Finding 9 above records the requirement in a comment on
   `relatedHop`; the review asked for a mechanism instead of a sentence,
   on the argument that a Task 13 lateral nesting a `SELECT` with its
   filter after it would pass both guards **silently** and reach
   production unfiltered.

   **That last part is not what the code does, and it was measured
   rather than argued.** The split puts a filter written after a nested
   `SELECT` in the *next* block, which leaves the reference looking
   unfiltered — so it is **reported**, not missed. Five nesting shapes
   were run through the guard with the new check disabled: the outer
   filter after a nested `SELECT`, a nested `SELECT` ahead of the
   reference, a nested `SELECT` over a scoped table with and without its
   own filter, and an outer alias's filter appearing inside the nested
   block by coincidence. Every incorrect one was caught; the two correct
   ones passed; none was silent. The guard's own table already asserts
   `want: true` for that shape — it is pinned as over-strictness, which
   is the side of the trade a guard belongs on.

   **What was actually missing is the reason, so that is what was
   added.** Over-strictness that reports `relations is read as "FROM
   relations rel" without rel.project_id = $1 in the same block` about a
   query whose filter is visibly right there is a failure the next task
   debugs as a bug in its own SQL. `flatLateralProblems` now asserts
   that the body of every `JOIN LATERAL (` holds exactly one `SELECT`
   before its matching `)`, failing with a message that names the split,
   says the filter will be reported as missing even when present, and
   says the fix is to keep the lateral flat or to teach the guard to
   parse. It runs inside `projectFilterProblems`, so both the canonical
   guard and this task's own text test carry it. Confirmed by mutation:
   `relatedHop` rewritten to nest `rel.id IN (SELECT id FROM relations)`
   ahead of `rel.project_id = $1` fails with the new message, where
   before it failed with the false accusation.

   **And the rest of that regexp was looked at**, which is the step
   skipped in the three previous rounds and how this guard keeps
   breaking one level down. Two properties were unwritten and are now
   pinned as cases. A longer identifier sharing a table's prefix —
   `entities_archive` — matches the `entities` alternative, and since
   what follows is not whitespace the alias group captures nothing, so
   it is reported as unaliased: loud, and wrong for the right reason.
   And `tableReference` is `(?i)` throughout while `filteredOn` is
   case-*sensitive*, so an uppercase alias filtered the same way is seen
   correctly, but an alias and its filter spelt in different cases is
   reported even though Postgres folds both: over-strict again, and now
   a known cost rather than a surprise.

**Recorded, not fixed.**

3. **An untyped `traverse` step disables typo detection for the whole
   projection**, not only for the untyped part. Written up in the
   Task 15 note above, where an agent will meet it, and on
   `projectionScope.open`. Reproduced: with one `to_type`-less step,
   `color_by: "min_levle"` and `fields: ["no_such_key"]` are both
   accepted. The permission itself is right — refusing there would
   refuse a projection over exactly the types the untyped step was
   written to reach — but what it costs is now said out loud.

4. **`jsonb_strip_nulls` recurses**, so a projected *object* value would
   be rewritten and `project.fields` and `include_fields` would answer
   the same question with different values. Unreachable today from every
   direction: the metamodel declares no object field type, array
   elements are left alone, and a null written through `UpsertEntity` is
   dropped on the way in — it takes raw SQL to build a row that shows
   the difference. One note on `payload`, for whoever adds an object
   field type.

5. **An edge drawn by two entries takes the first entry's label**,
   deterministically — `capOf`'s `DISTINCT ON` keeps the lowest rank per
   id and the Go-side dedupe applies the same rule — but `Edge.Label`
   did not say so, and two documents differing only in the declaration
   order of their `edges[]` entries label the same edge differently. One
   note on `Edge.Label`.

6. **`capOf`'s `DISTINCT ON (id) ORDER BY id, rank, depth` names no
   attribute**, so it does not say which row's `attrs` and `ambiguous`
   survive the dedupe. That is harmless only because every projection
   lateral is anchored solely on `e.id` and returns exactly one row, so
   all the candidate rows for one id carry identical attributes and an
   identical flag. That is a property of the *projection*, not of the
   dedupe, and it is now written on `capOf`: a lateral that reads
   anything but the node's id, or that may return more than one row,
   makes this `ORDER BY` have to name the attributes too.

---

### Task 10: The renderer catalogue and its requirements

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/renderers.go`
- Create: `internal/views/renderers_test.go`

**The renderer catalogue is a contract, not pixels.** Colour palettes,
node shapes, typography, density, interaction affordances and empty
states belong to sub-project 5 and to `/impeccable`; nothing in this task
decides what anything looks like. What it decides is what a renderer
*consumes* and what knobs it exposes — and, crucially, **what a query has
to produce for a view to be saveable against it**, so a broken view is
caught when it is written rather than when a designer opens it.

| Renderer | Consumes | Key parameters |
|---|---|---|
| `graph` | nodes, edges | `color_by`, `group_by`, `size_by`, `edge_labels`, `arrows`, `cluster_by` |
| `layered` | nodes, edges (expected mostly acyclic) | `rank_direction` (`TB`/`LR`), `rank_by` (`edges` — longest path — or a numeric field), `layer_labels`, `align` |
| `nested` | nodes, edges of one containment relation type | `contain_via` (required), `max_depth`, `leaf_label` |
| `map` | nodes, coordinates required | `background_asset_id`, `background_scale`, `background_offset`, `coordinate_source` (`manual` \| `fields`), `x_field`, `y_field`, `snap` |
| `table` | nodes only | `columns`, `sort`, `group_by`, `page_size` |
| `timeline` | nodes with a numeric or ordinal axis | `axis_field` (required), `axis_end_field`, `lane_by`, `axis_label` |

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

- `TestTheCatalogueIsClosedAndNamesItselfBack` — an unknown renderer is
  `query_invalid` at `/renderer` listing the six.
- `TestNestedRefusesAQueryWithNoContainmentEdges` — with the positive
  control (a query that *does* declare `contain_via`'s relation type in an
  `edges` entry) in the same test.
- `TestTimelineRefusesAnAxisFieldThatIsNotNumberOrOrderedEnum` — checked
  against the resolved `field_schema`, so it needs a game and belongs
  after Task 4.
- `TestMapInFieldsModeRefusesNonNumericCoordinates`.
- `TestARendererParameterItDoesNotDeclareIsRefused` — `renderer_params`
  is a free jsonb blob on the wire and must not be one in practice, or a
  typo'd `rank_dircetion` is stored, returned and silently ignored
  forever.
- `TestEveryRendererDeclaresItsParametersAndTheDescriptionIsGeneratedFromThem`
  — the tool description (Task 15) is built from this table, so a
  renderer added without parameters fails here rather than shipping an
  undocumented knob.

- [ ] **Step 2: Implement**

```go
// Renderer is one entry of the fixed catalogue.
//
// **This describes a contract, not an appearance.** What a `graph` looks
// like — its palette, its node shapes, its density, its empty state — is
// sub-project 5's and /impeccable's. What lives here is what a renderer
// consumes and what it can be told, so that views.upsert can refuse a
// view whose query cannot feed the renderer it names.
type Renderer struct {
	Name        string
	Description string
	Params      map[string]RendererParam
	// Requires is checked against a *resolved* query at upsert time. It
	// returns a message naming which requirement failed, or "".
	Requires func(r *Resolved, params map[string]any) string
}
```

`CheckRenderer(name string, params map[string]any, r *Resolved) error`
returns `renderer_requirements` when `Requires` speaks and `query_invalid`
when the name or a parameter is wrong — two codes because the recoveries
differ: one is "pick another renderer or change the query", the other is
"fix this argument".

- [ ] **Step 3: See red, commit**

Make `Requires` return `""` unconditionally for `nested` and watch
`TestNestedRefusesAQueryWithNoContainmentEdges` fail.

```bash
git add internal/views/renderers.go internal/views/renderers_test.go
git commit -m "feat: the renderer catalogue and the requirements a saved view is checked against"
```

**Corrections from implementing Task 10** (landed as `ca4e596`, `6d80989`
and `8def069`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped
code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the
baseline confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`,
`TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository — 1220
tests, 0 skipped). Twelve findings. Three of them change what the spec's
own §5.1 says.

1. **The plan's prescribed mutation reaches its path, for the first time
   in four tasks.** `nested`'s `Requires` returning nothing
   unconditionally is red in
   `TestNestedRefusesAQueryWithNoContainmentEdges` with "expected a
   refusal at /renderer_params/contain_via … got none". Recorded because
   the last four were not.

2. **`Requires` returns problems with pointers, not the plan's bare
   string.** A sentence is the one refusal in this sub-project with no
   address, and `map` alone can fail at `x_field`, at `y_field` or at
   both: an agent told "this renderer cannot consume this query" has to
   guess which of seven parameters to rewrite. The signature is
   `func(rc *rendererCheck) []metamodel.FieldError`, the same
   pointer-addressed shape every other refusal in this package carries,
   and the same whole-document pass — every requirement of one call comes
   back at once.

3. **"An `enum` with ordered options" cannot be violated, so that half of
   the spec's requirement is vacuous, and two checks that are *not*
   vacuous replace it.** `metamodel.Schema.Validate` refuses an enum with
   no options (`0004`-era rule, `schema.go`), and options are ordered by
   declaration, so every enum is an ordered enum and "number or ordered
   enum" is "number or enum". What is left unsaid by the spec and does
   fire:
   - **Every type in scope that declares the key must declare it the
     *same* way.** `number` and `enum` are each admitted, so a scope
     holding one of each passes "is it number or enum" while having no
     single axis to draw — the quests are placed and the regions vanish,
     and the picture looks right. This is the hole one step along from the
     check the spec does name, and the fixture already had it:
     `min_level` is a number on `quest` and an enum on `region`.
   - **An enum axis is its option *sequence*, not its option set.**
     `quest.rank` is `[common, rare, epic]` and `region.rank` is
     `[epic, common, rare]` — the same set in a different order, which
     `resolve.go`'s `sameOptions` deliberately treats as equal because a
     predicate over them asks whether a value is one of them and the
     order changes no answer. An axis is the one place the sequence is
     the meaning, so `sameSequence` is a second comparison rather than a
     reuse of the first, and both are commented against each other.
   `TestAnAxisDeclaredTwoWaysIsTwoAxes` holds both, and the `timeline`
   test carries its two positive controls — a number axis and an enum
   axis are exactly what this renderer is for.

4. **`table.columns` cannot mean "attribute references, including
   one-hop related", and the refusal says where the hop belongs.** A
   renderer consumes the envelope; the one hop is a `LEFT JOIN LATERAL`
   the *compiler* emits (Task 9), and no renderer can take it after the
   fact. A related column is drawn by projecting it into a slot and
   naming the slot, which is what the slot vocabulary is for, and the
   message says so rather than leaving an agent to discover a blank
   column. The rest of the column rule follows from the same principle:
   the built-ins a table may draw are the three a `Node` actually carries
   (`@name`, `@key`, `@type`) and not the five a predicate admits —
   `@invalid` and `@created_at` compare against columns of the row and
   are nowhere in the envelope.

5. **A renderer channel names a projection slot, not a field key, and
   `include_fields` cannot answer for a saved view.** `color_by`,
   `group_by`, `size_by`, `cluster_by`, `lane_by` and `leaf_label` take a
   slot name, because the query already decides what a slot reads — a
   second place to choose a field would be a second place to get it
   wrong, and only the query can take the one hop that makes "coloured by
   zone" mean anything. And a column naming a declared field key is legal
   exactly when `project.fields` asked for that key: `include_fields` is
   a **per-run** option (`RunRequest`), so a saved view cannot turn it
   on, and a column that would only exist when a caller asks for it is a
   blank column in the saved picture. The refusal names both halves.

6. **Where the "read by nothing" rule stops, decided rather than left to
   the next reader.** `map` refuses `x_field` in `coordinate_source:
   "manual"` — including the *default* manual, which is the hole one step
   along from checking only the written value — because a stored
   parameter that changes no picture is a lie a designer will believe.
   `table` does **not** refuse a query that draws edges, and that is not
   an inconsistency: one envelope for every renderer is the property that
   lets a saved view swap `graph` for `table` without rewriting its query
   (`Result`'s own doc), so a query member this renderer ignores is one
   the next renderer reads, while a *parameter* this renderer's own mode
   ignores will never be read by anything. Both are pinned —
   `TestMapAsksForTheCoordinatesItsModeReads` and
   `TestATableDoesNotRefuseAQueryThatDrawsEdges`, the second a deliberate
   non-refusal, which is worth a test for the same reason a refusal is.

7. **The two codes, and the order they are answered in.** A `QueryError`
   carries one code, so a call with a mistyped parameter *and* an
   unmeetable requirement cannot report both: shape problems win, because
   an agent fixing a typo has learned nothing from being told in the same
   breath that a different parameter names a slot its query lacks. The
   line between them is what is wrong with the value: wrong in itself —
   an unknown renderer, an unknown parameter name, a non-uuid, a spelling
   that is no slot at all — is `query_invalid`, "fix this argument"; a
   well-formed value this query cannot feed is `renderer_requirements`,
   "pick another renderer or change the query".
   `TestAShapeProblemIsAnsweredBeforeARequirement` pins the order and the
   handover, and this is the **first reader** `CodeRendererRequirements`
   has had since Task 2 declared it.

8. **Three guards over the table, all bidirectional, because the
   catalogue is data.** `TestAParameterKindCannotBeHalfAdded` (a kind a
   parameter declares with no checker would be a value stored and never
   judged; a checker no parameter declares is a kind somebody meant to
   use) — red in both arms.
   `TestEveryDeclaredParameterIsCheckedNotJustStored` drives **every**
   parameter of every renderer through a value its kind cannot accept and
   requires the refusal to name that parameter, with its own vacuity
   check that every kind has a wrong value in the test's table.
   `TestAnEnumParameterDeclaresItsValuesAndNothingElseDoes` keeps `Values`
   and `kindEnum` in step both ways. And in production a kind with no
   checker is an error rather than a silent acceptance, because a test is
   not a runtime guard.

9. **The description guard caught its own reader first.** The
   description is generated from the table and parsed back into
   renderers-and-parameters, compared in both directions. Its first
   version's `\(consumes ([^)]+)\)` stopped at the wrong parenthesis in
   `layered`'s own "nodes, edges (expected mostly acyclic)" and reported
   the whole catalogue as missing — caught by the test, fixed to a greedy
   match, and recorded because a guard that reads generated text is one
   more thing that can be wrong about it. Both directions are red under
   mutation: a parameter dropped from the generator, and a hand-written
   `sankey` paragraph naming a `width_by` knob nothing takes.

10. **The refusal-order test needed five wrong parameters, not three.**
    Problems are collected from a `map`, whose iteration order Go
    randomises, and `sortProblems` is what makes a refusal reproducible.
    With three keys an unsorted implementation comes back sorted by luck
    one run in six, and a guard that survives its mutation one run in six
    is not a guard; with five it is one in a hundred and twenty. Landed
    as its own commit with the reason in the test.

11. **The nil check was extended before it was needed, at the level the
    defect always has.** `CheckRenderer` refusing a nil `*Resolved` — a
    check that cannot see the query is not a check — left `Query` and
    `Cat` nil one line further on, and `Resolved` is exported and
    hand-buildable, exactly as Task 6 recorded for `Compile`. Both were
    panics under the first version's mutation; both are refusals now, and
    `ResolvedEdge.Spec` is nil-guarded for the same reason.

12. **What this task does not build, said out loud.** Nothing calls
    `CheckRenderer` yet: `views.upsert` is Task 11 and is where it is
    wired, and `RendererDescription` is read by Task 15. That is a
    mechanism nothing reads *for one task*, which is the plan's own
    sequencing rather than the failure this project keeps refusing — but
    it is the shape of that failure, so **Task 11's block must call
    `CheckRenderer` on every upsert and Task 15's tool description must
    be generated from `RendererDescription()`**, not restate the
    catalogue in prose. The description already carries the two things
    that would otherwise only live in this plan: open question O4's "no
    cursor on `views.run`" with `views.run_table` named as the escape
    hatch, and Task 7's finding 12 — an edge's endpoints are not
    guaranteed to be in `Nodes`, so `graph` says it tolerates them. Both
    are asserted by the description test.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each (`-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported): `nested`'s `Requires` returning
nothing (1, the plan's own); an unknown parameter accepted instead of
refused (1); the admitted-type check deleted (2, timeline and map); the
same-declaration check deleted (1); the enum sequence check deleted (1);
`drawnRelationTypes` blind to the `from_step` spelling (1, on the positive
control); the required-parameter loop disabled (1); requirements answered
in the same error as shapes (1); `sortProblems` removed (1, red four runs
out of four with five keys); `graph`'s `edge_labels` requirement removed
(1); `layered`'s edge requirement removed (1); `map`'s manual-mode arm
removed (1); a column's `project.fields` check always accepting (1); a
slot's declared check always accepting (1); the nil-`Resolved` refusal
narrowed back to `r == nil` (1, a panic); a parameter dropped from the
description generator (1, both directions of the guard); a hand-written
`sankey` paragraph added to the description (1); `kindPoint`'s checker
renamed to a kind nothing declares (2, both arms of the kind guard);
`@invalid` and `@created_at` admitted as table columns (1). Nineteen
mutations, nineteen red.

**Corrections from Task 10's review round** (landed as `6d0a89a` and
`e3e6775`). The round confirmed the twelve findings above in substance
and sent back ten more. Six of them are one defect: **a rule established
correctly on the parameter it was first written for, and not carried to
the next parameter along.** Every mutation below was applied to the
shipped code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored;
baseline confirmed green and confirmed *running* before each (`-v`,
`TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP`).

13. **`include_fields` was enforced for a table's columns and for
    nothing else** (major). Finding 5 above established the rule — a
    saved view may only name a declared field key that `project.fields`
    asked for, because `include_fields` is a per-run option no saved
    view can turn on — and `column()` was the only place that held it.
    `axis_field`, `axis_end_field`, `x_field`, `y_field` and `rank_by`
    checked the schema and never the projection. Reproduced against the
    shipped code: `CheckRenderer(timeline, axis_field=min_level)` over
    `{"from":[{"type":"quest"}]}` returned `<nil>`, and so did `map` in
    `fields` mode with `x_field`/`y_field`. **A timeline saved that way
    has no axis at all — every node at the origin — and a map in fields
    mode has no coordinates**, which is precisely the failure this task
    exists to prevent, and no later task rescues it: Tasks 11 and 15
    never set `IncludeFields`. The rule now lives in one
    `requireCarried`, called by `column()` and by `requireFieldKey`,
    because the duplication is why it drifted. Both answer
    `renderer_requirements` with `column()`'s message. New test
    `TestAFieldKeyParameterNamesAFieldTheSavedQueryCarries`, five
    parameters and four controls; red when `requireFieldKey` skips the
    carried half.

14. **`requireDeclaredAs` admitted an axis some types in scope do not
    declare, while its own doc said it refused exactly that** (major).
    The comment says it is *"deliberately stricter than
    `projectionScope.declares` … an axis that finds nothing has nowhere
    to put them"*; the code refused only when **no** type declared the
    key. Reproduced: `timeline axis_field=min_level` over `quest+zone`,
    where `zone` declares nothing, returned `<nil>`, as did the `map`
    fields-mode pair. That is finding 3's own sentence — *"the quests
    are placed and the regions vanish, and the picture looks right"* —
    reached by a shorter route than the one finding 3 closed, and
    **declaring a key nowhere is far rarer than declaring it on one of
    the two types drawn**. It now refuses when fewer types declare the
    key than are in scope, naming the ones that do not. New test
    `TestAnAxisSomeTypeInScopeDoesNotDeclareIsRefused`, carrying the
    control that keeps the two rules apart: the *same* key as a
    projection slot over the *same* two types is still accepted, because
    a node with no attribute is drawable and a node with no axis
    position is not.

15. **The span check compared field types, not enum options** (major).
    Finding 3 added `sameSequence` so that two types declaring one key
    with reordered options are two axes; the span check one screen above
    compared only `metamodel.FieldType`, so two *different* enum fields
    with unrelated options were "the same kind of axis" — a span from
    `semi` to `gold`, which has no length. Reproduced on the fixture,
    where `region` declares `min_level` as `[low, high]` and `rank` as
    `[epic, common, rare]`: `<nil>`. The same hole one step along from
    the fix that closed the previous one, which is the shape of this
    whole round. Both ends resolving to `enum` now require the same
    option sequence, and the case lives beside the type case in
    `TestATimelineSpanHasBothEndsOnOneAxis`.

16. **`columns` reported one fault, lost the index, and could flip the
    call's error code** (major). Three defects in one loop, and the
    third is the one that matters: `["@invalid","difficulty"]` answered
    `query_invalid` and `["difficulty","@invalid"]` answered
    `renderer_requirements` — **the same two wrong columns, a different
    code, decided by which the agent happened to type first**, which is
    finding 7's shape-wins invariant breaking at the one place a list
    can break it. `["difficulty","tags"]` reported only `difficulty`,
    against finding 2's whole-document rule. And every pointer was
    `/renderer_params/columns`, the list, when a list is exactly where
    the index is the address. A checker now returns `paramFaults` — all
    of them — each carrying an optional element index, and
    `CheckRenderer` addresses `/renderer_params/columns/3` and routes
    each fault into the shape/requirements split so the existing
    arbitration decides the code. `pointerLess` already orders indices
    numerically, so `/columns/10` comes back after `/columns/2`; the new
    `TestEveryBadColumnComesBackWithItsIndex` pins that with eleven
    columns, both orders of the mixed pair, and the two-of-one-class
    case.

17. **"Read by nothing" was enforced for `x_field` and not for the
    background knobs** (medium). Finding 6 refuses a parameter the
    renderer's own mode ignores, including the defaulted mode. One
    parameter along, `background_scale=2` and `background_offset=[1,2]`
    with no `background_asset_id` both returned `<nil>`: a scale and an
    offset for an image that is not there change no picture. Both are
    refused now. And **`snap` in `fields` mode is decided rather than
    left to the next reader**, which is the same omission finding 6
    closed for `x_field`: it is the grid a *dragged* node lands on,
    dragging is what manual mode reads, and in fields mode a node's
    coordinates come off its declared fields — so it is refused there,
    with the sentence saying why in the code.
    `TestMapRefusesBackgroundKnobsWithNoBackground` carries both, plus
    the control that `snap` alone in manual mode is still fine.

18. **The generated description documented no requirement, though its
    first sentence promised them** (medium). Finding 9's guard is sound
    — the description is genuinely generated and nothing is restated —
    but the header promises *"what a query has to produce for a view to
    be saveable"* and the body said it for no renderer. Nothing printed
    told an agent that `nested` needs edges of `contain_via`, that
    `map`'s `x_field` is refused in manual mode, or that a timeline axis
    must be declared identically across the scope; Task 15 ships this
    text as the tool surface. Worse for drift: a `Requires` could be
    added, changed or deleted with no generated sentence changing and no
    guard noticing — the "sentence that can lie" risk the description
    test exists for, in the half of the contract that matters most.
    `Renderer.RequiresDoc` is now printed under each renderer and
    guarded bidirectionally by
    `TestEveryRequirementIsDocumentedAndEveryDocHasARequirement`: a
    non-nil `Requires` must have one, a doc must have a `Requires`, and
    the doc must appear in the generated text. `table` has neither,
    which is the right answer for a renderer whose requirements are all
    in its parameter kinds.

19. **An untyped step switched off the axis check for types that *are*
    in scope** (medium). The permission is finding 3's, inherited from
    `projectionScope.open` and deliberately wide, but it was wider than
    its justification: under an open scope, `timeline axis_field="tags"`
    returned `<nil>` even though `tags` is `list<text>` on `quest` and
    `quest` is written right there in `from` — the closed-scope call is
    refused with a good message, so "nothing can judge it" was never
    true of that key. The projection's justification (a node carrying no
    attribute is still drawable) does not transfer to an axis, as the
    doc already said. `requireDeclaredAs` now returns early for an open
    scope **after** the per-declaration loop rather than before it: the
    "declared nowhere" and "declared on some" arms stay switched off,
    and a key a named type declares unusably is refused.
    `TestAnUntypedStepSwitchesOffTheFieldChecks` grew that arm, and its
    open-scope document now carries `no_such_field` in `project.fields`
    — which an open scope also accepts, the same permission seen from
    the other side.

20. **Internal kind identifiers were printed into agent-facing prose**
    (low). The description read `- rank_by: rank_by.`, `- sort:
    column.`, `- x_field: number_field.` — Go constants shown to a
    reader who has never seen one, saying nothing about what to send.
    `kindEnum` already printed its own admitted spellings; nothing else
    had a phrase. `kindPhrases` now gives each kind one, and
    `TestAParameterKindCannotBeHalfAdded` grew the same both-arms guard
    it already applies to the checkers: every kind has a phrase, every
    phrase has a kind, and `kindEnum` must have none.

21. **A parameter name could mean two kinds on two renderers,
    unguarded** (low). Mutating `table.group_by` from `kindSlot` to
    `kindText` and leaving its doc alone left the whole package green:
    the description would then print two kinds under one prose line, and
    a misspelt slot name on a table would be stored, returned and read
    by nothing — this file's own first defect, through the one door none
    of the per-renderer guards watch.
    `TestAParameterNameCarriesOneKindAcrossTheCatalogue` closes it, with
    an `allowedToDiffer` exception list that is empty on purpose and a
    vacuity check that fails if no name is shared at all.

22. **Finding 10's arithmetic was wrong and the guard was accidental.**
    It claimed three keys survive an unsorted implementation one run in
    six and five keys one in a hundred and twenty, as though Go permuted
    a map's keys. **It does not.** A small map is iterated from a random
    start slot, so what comes back is a *rotation* of the literal's
    insertion order. Measured on this toolchain, 10,000 runs rebuilding
    the literal each time:

    - the literal as written (starting with `snap`): **5 distinct
      orders**, all rotations, **0/10000 already sorted (0.00%)**;
    - the same five keys written alphabetically: **4,984/10000 already
      sorted (49.84%)**;
    - three keys starting with `snap`, the shape finding 10 called one
      in six: 3 orders, 1,254/10000 sorted (12.54%).

    **The key count is irrelevant.** The guard worked only because the
    literal happened to begin with `snap`, and the next person to tidy
    it alphabetically would have destroyed it silently. The test now
    asserts determinism instead of improbability: the same call twenty
    times over, every run's order identical and equal to the sorted
    expectation, no database per iteration, and reordering the literal
    cannot break it. Red on five consecutive runs with `sortProblems`
    removed, with the literal in both orders. Checked, as the review
    asked: the only other order-sensitive assertion in this task's tests
    is `TestEveryBadColumnComesBackWithItsIndex`, whose order comes from
    a slice and not from a map.

**Also recorded.**

- **The enum-vacuity claim in finding 3 held through the API and not on
  the read side.** `metamodel.Schema.Validate` refuses an optionless
  enum at upsert, which made "every enum is an ordered enum"
  *unreachable* rather than true — nothing revalidates a schema on the
  way back **out** of the database, so a row written straight into the
  schema column loads and is believed, and gives an accepted axis with
  no order and no place to put a node. One arm in `requireDeclaredAs`
  now refuses it, which makes the rule hold rather than merely go
  untested. `TestAnOptionlessEnumIsNoAxisEvenIfTheSchemaColumnHoldsOne`
  mutates the loaded catalogue, which is exactly what such a row
  produces.
- **`Node.Set` and `Node.Role` are in the envelope and have no sigil**,
  so a table drawn over a multi-set query cannot show which set a row
  came from — `columns` admits exactly the sigils `builtins` lists.
  Nothing depends on it today (a predicate filters entities, and a set
  is not a column of one). Recorded on `builtins` in `predicate.go`
  rather than here, because that comment implies the list is complete
  and `@set` is the first thing a reader will look for and not find.
- **No third nil dereference.** Every renderer and every declared
  parameter was driven through a `Resolved` whose `Query` and `Cat` are
  non-nil and empty of everything else, singly and all at once, with and
  without an empty `ResolvedEdge`/`ResolvedStep`: no panic. The
  `Query`/`Cat` pair of finding 11 was the whole of it.

**Mutations proved red in this round**: `requireFieldKey` skipping the
carried half (1); the "declared on every type" arm removed (1); the span
option-sequence check removed (1); `columns` returning at its first fault
(1); `columns` faults addressed at the list instead of the element (1,
two tests); the background-asset arm removed (1); the `snap` fields-mode
arm removed (1); the open-scope return moved back above the declaration
loop (1); the optionless-enum arm removed (1); a `RequiresDoc` deleted
while its `Requires` stayed (1); a `RequiresDoc` added to `table`, which
checks nothing (1); the `requires:` line dropped from the generator (1);
a kind's phrase deleted (1); a phrase added for `kindEnum` (1);
`table.group_by` retyped to `kindText` (1); `sortProblems` removed (2 —
five consecutive runs each, with the literal as written and rewritten
alphabetically). Sixteen mutations, sixteen red.
