### Task 11: Saved views — upsert, get, list, remove, refs and events

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/db/queries/views.sql`
- Create: `internal/views/views.go`
- Create: `internal/views/events.go`
- Create: `internal/views/views_test.go`
- Create: `internal/views/list_test.go`

The CRUD is ordinary sqlc — every statement takes the resolved project id
and filters on it — and it repeats four invariants exactly.

**Compare-and-set under `FOR UPDATE`.** `GetViewByKeyForUpdate` reads the
row inside the transaction with the lock held, so the version a caller is
told about is the one their write will meet; `UpsertView`'s
`ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE … WHERE views.version = $expected` is the guard,
and no rows returned is the conflict. `updated_at` belongs to the trigger
and to no query.

**`view_refs` are rewritten in the same transaction**: delete this view's
refs, insert the resolved ones. Outside the transaction they would drift
from the query they index, which is the one thing they exist not to do.

**A query that has passed only `ParseQuery` is bounded, not judged**, and
this is the task where the difference is spent (recorded in Task 3's
corrections and in `ParseQuery`'s own doc comment). Structurally it is
safe to store — strings, collections and every `any`, `params[].default`
included, are bounded, so nothing arriving here can be an unbounded blob
that every later stage re-walks. Semantically nothing in it has been
resolved: no key names anything, no operator is known to suit its field,
and a parameter default is not yet known to be a scalar of its declared
type. **Store what Task 4's resolve pass returned, never what `ParseQuery`
alone returned** — the resolved form is what `view_refs` are computed
from, and a row written before resolution is a document with no
guarantee that it answers any question.

**The cursor.** The listing pages with `internal/paging`, and it carries
**two** tests, not one:

- `TestACursorFromAnotherGameIsRefusedByTheViewListing` — behavioural.
- `TestTheViewListingFingerprintIsProjectIdFirstAndCarriesItsDomain` —
  compositional, asserting the exact ordered argument list. **The
  behavioural test can pass without the project id** whenever another key
  part discriminates — here the renderer filter does — and that is
  precisely how the original defect survived its first test. The
  compositional one is:

```go
func TestTheViewListingFingerprintIsProjectIdFirstAndCarriesItsDomain(t *testing.T) {
	projectID := uuid.New()
	got := viewListingFingerprint(projectID, ViewFilter{Renderer: "graph"})
	want := paging.Fingerprint(projectID.String(), "views", "graph")
	if got != want {
		t.Fatalf("the fingerprint must be project id first, then the domain discriminator, "+
			"then the filter: got %q want %q", got, want)
	}
	// A different game must not produce the same fingerprint even with an
	// identical filter — which is what the project-id-first rule buys and
	// what a behavioural test cannot isolate.
	if viewListingFingerprint(uuid.New(), ViewFilter{Renderer: "graph"}) == got {
		t.Fatal("two games share a fingerprint")
	}
	// And the domain discriminator: without it, a views cursor and an
	// entities cursor over the same game and an empty filter would be
	// interchangeable.
	if got == paging.Fingerprint(projectID.String(), "graph") {
		t.Fatal("the fingerprint carries no domain discriminator")
	}
}
```

**Events publish after commit.** `view.upserted` and `view.removed`, both
`MinRole: roles.Role("")` and `HumanOnly: false`, each argued in
`events.go` rather than copied: a view is derived content that any viewer
can open and any agent can run, so gating its invalidation above viewer
would withhold the signal from exactly the reader who has no other way to
know, and gating it to humans would withhold it from the agent that is
composing against it. The payload carries **identity only** — id, key,
version — because publication order is not commit order.

And the test the header names:

```go
// TestNoViewEventIsPublishedWhenTheCommitFails is the one placement a
// refusal test cannot catch: a publish written as the last statement
// inside fn differs from the correct one only by the commit that
// follows, and a rolled-back write and a refused write look identical
// from outside. A deferred constraint in this test's own throwaway
// database makes the commit, and only the commit, fail.
// internal/metamodel's test of the same name is the model.
func TestNoViewEventIsPublishedWhenTheCommitFails(t *testing.T) { ... }
```

- [ ] **Step 1: Write `internal/db/queries/views.sql`**

Ten statements, each with the header comment convention the file's
neighbours use: `UpsertView`, `GetViewByKey`, `GetViewByKeyForUpdate`,
`GetViewByID`, `ListViewsPage`, `DeleteView`, `DeleteViewRefs`,
`InsertViewRef`, `ListViewRefs`, `ListViewsBrokenByType`. The listing
keysets on `(name, id)` like `ListEntitiesPage`, and **every one of the
ten filters on `project_id`**, including the two addressed by id — a view
id in a caller's hand is not authority to read it.

- [ ] **Step 2: `make sqlc` and confirm the generated code compiles**

```bash
make sqlc && go build ./... && make sqlc-check
```

Expected: `sqlc diff` silent. If it prints a diff, the generated code on
disk is stale and `make sqlc` was not run.

- [ ] **Step 3–8: the tests, the service, red, read-back, commit**

The read-back step (defect: write-only fields): after
`views.upsert` with a non-empty `renderer_params`, `layout_mode:
"manual"`, `layout_seed: 7` and a `description`, **read the view back
through `Get` and assert all four**, then again through the MCP tool in
Task 15. `renderer_params` is the exact shape `relations.fields` had when
it was write-only for a whole sub-project.

```bash
git add internal/db/queries/views.sql internal/db/dbq internal/views/views.go \
        internal/views/events.go internal/views/views_test.go internal/views/list_test.go
git commit -m "feat: saved views, their dependency refs, their listing and their events"
```

**Corrections from implementing Task 11** (landed as `dd77d52`, `e88394b`,
`461c1d0`, `db06a03`, `d96baaf`, `cf30841`, `c68e146` and `3d88bad`;
every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code, watched fail
the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline confirmed green
and confirmed *running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP`
across the repository — 1701 tests, 0 skipped). Thirteen findings. Four
of them are the same shape: **an invariant this task was told to hold
exactly, held correctly, and pinned by nothing**, because every test
about it asserted what a *successful* call stores and both the right and
the wrong implementation store the same rows.

1. **"The refs are rewritten in the same transaction" was the invariant
   with the most tests and the least coverage.** Three tests assert the
   refs a call writes, rewrites and leaves behind after a refusal, and
   **all three stay green when `writeRefs` is moved out of the
   transaction** to run against the pool once the view has committed —
   measured, not reasoned about. Both placements store the same rows on
   every path that succeeds; what separates them is a failure *between*
   the two writes, which nothing in the suite produced.
   `TestAViewAndItsRefsAreOneChange` manufactures one: a deferred foreign
   key from `view_refs.view_id` to `projects.id` is satisfied by nothing
   and is checked at COMMIT, so written inside the transaction the refs
   take the view down with them and nothing is stored, while written
   after it the view is already committed and only the refs fail —
   leaving a stored view with an empty dependency index, so a type
   deleted afterwards would report that it broke nothing. Red against the
   moved placement with the view found where it should not be.

2. **The compare-and-set's three Go-side checks were each redundant
   against the tests as first written, and two of them are not redundant
   against the *right* tests.** Each was mutated separately.
   - **The locked read's version check** is behaviourally redundant and
     stays: deleting it leaves the whole package green, because the
     guarded `DO UPDATE` refuses on its own and `conflictOnViewKey`'s
     re-read reports the same current version. That is the position
     internal/markdown reached for the identical check, and it is now
     written on the branch rather than left to look load-bearing: what it
     earns is that a caller already known to be wrong is turned away
     before a query document, its refs and a version row are built, sent
     and rolled back.
   - **The locked read's spelling check** was redundant only because no
     test made a caller wrong twice. With a correct version in hand the
     post-write check catches a respelling, and with a stale one the
     failed-guard re-read does; neither can choose *which* fault to
     report when a caller has both, and "current version is 1" over a key
     that would be refused again at version 1 is a loop a caller cannot
     leave by doing what the error said.
     `TestARespellingIsNamedEvenWhenTheVersionIsAlsoStale` is what makes
     it load-bearing, and is red without it.
   - **The post-write spelling check is live here, and that was staged
     rather than argued**, because internal/markdown made the same claim
     for a check that turned out to be unreachable on its side (with
     `expected_version: 0` spelling a create, the loser of that race
     always falls to the failed-guard arm). Here a version is a claim
     about a row rather than a claim to be creating one, so a caller
     holding the version the winner is about to land on passes the empty
     locked read *and* the guard and updates a row it never saw.
     `TestACreationRacingACreatorUnderAnotherSpellingIsToldTheSpelling`
     stages exactly that overlap; deleting the check makes it report a
     nil error over a silently overwritten row.

3. **`RemoveView`'s "how many rows did the delete actually remove" arm
   was a signal nothing read.** Deleting it left the package green and
   shipped a `view.removed` announced by a caller that removed nothing —
   a second event for one removal, on a hub whose subscribers re-read on
   hearing one. Two goroutines racing would not pin it: they serialise as
   often as not, and then the *read* refuses and the arm is never
   reached. `TestARemovalThatLostItsRaceAnnouncesNothing` stages it with
   a rival transaction that deletes the row and holds its lock, so this
   call's own DELETE blocks and then matches nothing.

4. **The project filters on the id-addressed statements are invisible to
   every caller this package offers, and the SQL file's own claim about
   them was wrong in one direction.** `RemoveView` resolves the key
   inside the game before it deletes, and `UpsertView` writes refs for a
   row its own transaction just wrote, so each of those reads masks the
   filter under it: with the filter deleted from `DeleteView` the whole
   package stays green. The first version of the comment then said, of
   `view_refs`, that dropping the filter "can be caught by no test" —
   copied from internal/db/queries/metamodel.sql, where the composite
   foreign key really does make it unobservable. It does not here: **a
   composite key constrains what a row may hold, not which rows a DELETE
   may match**, so `DeleteViewRefs` called with a foreign view id and
   this game's project id would clear another game's index.
   `TestTheViewQueriesAddressingARowByIdAreScopedToTheProject` drives
   `GetViewByID`, `ListViewRefs`, `DeleteViewRefs` and `DeleteView`
   directly, which is the only way to observe a filter every service path
   has already made redundant, and the comment now says what is actually
   true of each. Four filters, four mutations, four red.

5. **`id` in the listing's `ORDER BY` is not observable here, and that is
   recorded rather than dressed up as a test.** internal/metamodel found
   dropping it from `ListEntitiesPage` red the moment a fixture shared
   one name; the same mutation on `ListViewsPage`, against seven views
   sharing one name paged three at a time, leaves the package **green** —
   `views_project_idx` is `(project_id, name, id)`, so the index scan
   that serves this statement already returns tied rows in id order and
   the clause asks for what the plan was doing anyway. So the *index* is
   today's guard and the clause is what keeps the statement correct
   without it; the statement says so.

6. **The fingerprint's domain discriminator is caught only by the
   compositional test, exactly as the plan predicted.** Both tests are
   red with the project id removed; with `"views"` removed the
   behavioural one stays **green** and only
   `TestTheViewListingFingerprintIsProjectIdFirstAndCarriesItsDomain`
   fails. Recorded because it is the plan's own argument confirmed rather
   than assumed, and because it is why a listing gets two cursor tests.

7. **A statement the plan asked for would have been read by nobody for a
   whole task**, which is the shape Task 10's finding 12 warned this task
   about. `ListViewsBrokenByType` is Task 12's lookup, and a generated
   statement with no caller is a mechanism nothing reads: it ships with
   `Service.ViewsDependingOn` and a test, and Task 12 uses it rather than
   introducing it. Two things were decided in writing it. **It must be
   asked before the type is deleted** — `ON DELETE SET NULL` is what
   makes the ref row survive its type, and the same SET NULL empties the
   column this lookup matches on, so asking afterwards finds nothing and
   reports that nothing broke, which is a wrong answer rather than an
   error; the statement says so and Task 12's deletion asks inside its
   own transaction. And **a kind that is neither of the two is refused**
   rather than answered with an empty list, which would read as "nothing
   depends on it".

8. **`renderer_params` needs no size bound of its own**, checked rather
   than assumed. Every other blob this package stores is bounded by
   `ParseQuery`, and the obvious inference is that a free jsonb column
   needs the same. It does not: `CheckRenderer` refuses a parameter name
   no renderer declares, and every declared one is judged by its kind's
   checker — `kindText` bounds its string at `MaxStringLen`,
   `kindColumns` at `MaxColumns` elements each of which must be a string,
   `kindPoint` at two numbers. So the catalogue is the bound, a caller
   cannot store a megabyte under a name nothing takes, and a second size
   rule here would be a second place to get it wrong.

9. **The three background columns are deliberately not in `UpsertView`'s
   SET list.** A background is written by `views.set_background` (Task
   14) and belongs to the picture rather than to the query; listing them
   would make every ordinary edit of a query or a renderer parameter
   silently detach the world map a designer put behind it, because a
   caller that says nothing about a background would be saying "none" —
   the same distinction markdown's `WriteInput.Links` draws with a
   pointer, drawn here by the column simply not being writable from this
   path. Task 14 must not add them to this statement; it needs its own.

10. **Two of the row's own columns needed a decision the plan did not
    name.** `layout_mode` is judged in Go against the same closed list
    `0008_views.sql` CHECKs, because the CHECK alone answers a caller's
    own argument with `violates check constraint "views_layout_mode_check"
    (SQLSTATE 23514)` — seen, under mutation — which reaches an agent as
    `internal_error`. And `LayoutSeed` is a `*int32`: 0 is a seed a
    caller may deliberately choose, so a plain `int32` would silently
    store 1 for it and draw a different diagram under the number the
    designer picked. Both are red under their own mutations.

11. **`renderer_params` is stored as `{}` and never as `null`.**
    `encoding/json` renders a nil map as `null`, and `null::jsonb` is a
    legal jsonb value that is not an object, so every later reader —
    Task 15's tool, a renderer reading its parameters back — would have
    to handle two spellings of "no parameters". The same rule Task 6
    applied to an empty result envelope.

12. **The row's own arguments answer `invalid_input`, addressed by JSON
    pointer.** Two conventions meet in this call: internal/metamodel and
    internal/markdown report a row's arguments as `invalid_input` at a
    bare path (`key`, `name`), and everything in this package addresses
    itself by pointer — `CheckRenderer` already answers at
    `/renderer_params/x_field`. The *type* is the metamodel's, because
    internal/web's existing `invalid_input` arm matches it and because
    the recovery really is "fix this argument"; the *address* is this
    package's, because a call that answered at `name` and at
    `/renderer_params/columns/3` in the same breath would be two
    spellings of one thing. Task 15 must publish both as
    `details.fields[].path` unchanged.

13. **A comment naming a test that does not exist fails the suite**, and
    this task found it the hard way: `internal/markdown`'s
    `TestNoCommentNamesATestThatDoesNotExist` scans the whole module, so
    a `views.sql` comment citing a test name that was renamed before it
    was written broke a package this task never touched. Worth recording
    for the tasks after this one: every "X pins it" in a comment is
    checked.

**Also recorded, decided rather than left to the next reader.**

- **The query is stored as the caller wrote it, and only after resolution
  passed.** The plan's rule — store what the resolve pass returned, never
  what `ParseQuery` alone returned — is about *judgement*, not about
  which bytes land: `0008_views.sql` says the column holds the document
  as authored, and Task 12's "a rename does not rewrite the stored query"
  depends on that. So the bytes are the caller's and the refs are
  resolution's, and nothing is written until resolution and
  `CheckRenderer` have both passed.
  `TestAQueryThatDoesNotResolveIsRefusedAndNothingIsStored` and
  `TestARendererThatCannotDrawTheQueryIsRefusedAtSaveTime` hold the two
  halves, each asserting that nothing is stored as well as that the call
  refused.
- **`RemoveView` takes no expected version.** A view is derived content —
  the query is stored, the picture is not — so removing one somebody had
  just edited costs one re-upsert from a document the caller already
  holds, while requiring a version would make every removal a
  read-then-write a designer has to retry against churn they do not care
  about. The metamodel makes the same choice for its own removals.
- **The event payload carries a version, which is the one field of the
  three that is not an address.** It is the token a caller's next write
  must carry, so a subscriber learns from one event both that the view
  moved and what to merge onto, and it is monotonic where nothing else on
  the row is. It is still not a value to render: publication order is not
  commit order, so two edits in flight can arrive as 3 then 2. Said on
  the payload type.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each (`-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported): `GetViewByID`'s project filter
(1); `GetViewByKey`'s (1); `ListViewsPage`'s (1); `DeleteView`'s (1, in
the direct-statement test only); `ListViewRefs`'s (2); `DeleteViewRefs`'s
(1, likewise); `FOR UPDATE` dropped from `GetViewByKeyForUpdate` (1); the
fingerprint without its project id (2); without its domain discriminator
(1, compositional only); `CheckRenderer` never called (2); the refs never
written (4); the refs written after the commit (1, the new
deferred-constraint test); the publish moved inside the transaction, as
its last statement (1); the old refs not cleared before the new ones are
written (1, a unique-index violation); every ref written into the

the empty-name arm removed (2); `layout_mode` not judged in Go (2, one of

plus **three confirmed green and recorded as such**: the locked read's

**Second review round on Task 11** (landed as `5b37a0b`, `d46a7bf`,
`b63c5d3` and `fa7756e`). The review re-ran all twenty-five mutations
above and reproduced every one, including the three recorded as green,
and raced the compare-and-set properly — eight concurrent upserts at one
version, eight concurrent creates, twelve rounds of writers alternating
between queries with different ref sets, twenty rounds of racing removals
— **without producing a single row whose refs disagree with its stored
query**. The core and the deferred-foreign-key pin stand. Six findings

found four of, arriving in a fourth place each: *a rule stated in a
comment that no test asks the question of.*

1. **`ListViewsBrokenByType` was the one statement of the ten whose

   Pointer:/from/0/type}]`, package `ok` in 30.8s.
   `TestViewsDependingOnATypeAreFoundByIdWithTheirPointers` looked like
   the isolation test — it seeds an identical view in the other game —
   but every assertion in it discriminated on the *type ids* differing,
   which passes either way. The composite foreign key does not defend
   this one, unlike `view_refs`' case: the ref row legitimately holds the
   other game's type id under the other game's project id, and
   `ViewsDependingOn` takes a bare type id and resolves nothing inside
   the game first — so this filter is load-bearing for its own caller
   rather than defence in depth. The test now asks the other game about
   this game's type id on *both* arms, with the positive control in the
   same test, and the statement says which of its filters is load-bearing
   and that they mask each other, the standard the file header sets.

2. **The audit columns were write-only, and the test written to catch
   exactly that omitted them.** `TestAViewIsReadBackWithEveryFieldItWas
   SavedWith`'s own doc comment says why it exists — a column *"was
   write-only for a whole sub-project because every test asserted the
   call succeeded and none read the row back"* — then enumerates four
   fields and never sets `Actor`, the one field of `ViewInput` no test in
   the package set. Replacing both `UpdatedBy…` arguments with `nil` left
   the **whole module** green. Both are now set and read back, and the
   foreign-token refusal both sibling packages assert is here too:
   `TestAViewWrittenByAnotherGamesTokenIsRefused`, which without the new
   mapping arm reported `err = <nil>` — the constraint fires, but
   `SQLSTATE 23503` over a generated constraint name says nothing about a
   token scoped to the wrong game. `metamodel.actorConstraintViolation`
   is exported as `ActorConstraintViolation` and shared rather than
   copied; `ErrActorNotInGame` is aliased here and is deliberately *not*
   in `Sentinels()`, for the reason `metamodel/bulk.go` gives.

3. **The prose caps counted bytes where the metamodel counts runes, so
   the comment promising they are the same cap was false for every
   non-ASCII name.** The two constants match internal/metamodel's
   *"deliberately… so a designer does not meet two different caps for the
   same shape of text in two places of one product"*. The numbers
   matched; the unit did not. Measured, 200 accented characters:

   ```
   metamodel label: err = <nil>
   views name:      err = invalid_input: /name: must be at most 200 bytes, and this one is 400
   ```

   A Spanish view name got 100 characters where a Spanish type label gets
   200 — the asymmetry the metamodel forbids in writing (*"a cap measured
   in bytes makes an accented label shorter than an unaccented one for no
   reason a designer could ever guess, and these columns are a game's own
   prose in a game's own language"*), in a product whose content is a
   game's prose in the designer's own language, and this project's own
   author writes Spanish. It was copied from internal/markdown, where
   counting bytes is right because those values are paths and kinds
   rather than prose. The rune count is now
   `metamodel.LengthProblem`, called rather than restated —
   `checkStorableText` already argues that for the control-character scan
   beside it — and `TestAViewsProseIsCappedInCharactersNotBytes` pins
   both ends: exactly the cap in two-byte characters saves and reads back
   character for character, one past it is refused with the metamodel's
   own message for the same value. Beside it, `MaxStringLen` was printed
   as "characters" by `renderers.go` and as "bytes" by `query.go` over
   the same `len()`; both say bytes now, and the constant records why its
   unit is the opposite of the two prose caps' — it is a machine bound on
   a query document, not a designer-visible cap on prose.

4. **Finding 9's decision — the three background columns deliberately
   absent from the upsert's `SET` list — was asserted by nothing.**
   `TestAnOrdinaryUpsertLeavesABackgroundStanding` writes a background
   directly, edits the view through the only write path there is, and
   asserts all three columns stand; adding `background_scale` to the
   `SET` list makes it red (`BackgroundScale = 1, want 2.5 untouched`).
   Task 14 inherits the test, and the statement now names it.

5. **Recorded, not changed: an update blocked behind a committed removal
   resurrects the view.** The blocked caller's locked read returns no
   rows, so it takes the creation path, and its `ExpectedVersion` —
   asserted about a row that no longer exists — is accepted by an insert
   with no version to guard, storing the view again under a new id and
   undoing the designer's deletion. Staged and observed.
   internal/metamodel's type upsert has byte-identical structure, so this
   is inherited repository-wide behaviour rather than this sub-project's
   drift; it is written beside the no-rows branch so the next reader
   knows it was seen, and filed as a backlog item rather than changed
   under this task.

6. **A claim dropped because it was not literally true.** `ViewInput.Query`
   said the document is stored *"as the caller wrote it, bytes and all"*.
   `jsonb` normalises whitespace, collapses duplicate keys and reorders
   object keys, so it is stored as written *semantically* — which is the
   claim Task 12 depends on, and what the read-back test already asserts
   by comparing decoded values. The comment now says that.

**Carried forward to Task 14, written where Task 14 will read it.**
`CheckRenderer` has exactly one caller because the upsert is the only
write path over a view's query and its renderer parameters together, so
no partial update can leave a stored view undrawable. `views.set_background`
is the second write path: it may write the three background columns,
which no renderer rule reads, and **must not** grow into a setter for

**No finding on the events.** The argument in `events.go` is genuinely

### Task 12: Staleness — a renamed type, a deleted type, and what a stale view answers

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/stale.go`
- Create: `internal/views/stale_test.go`

**Stale views are the normal case, not the exception.** A game's
vocabulary keeps moving for as long as the game is being designed, and a
view written in March references types renamed in June.

Resolution at execution time goes, per ref:

1. **By id.** A renamed type still resolves, because a rename does not
   change its id.
2. **By key**, when the id is null. A type deleted and re-created under
   the same key resolves, which is the common shape of a designer fixing
   a mistake.
3. Neither: the reference is dead.

Diagnostics, each carrying the pointer:

```go
// Diagnostic is one thing a query says that this game no longer has, or
// no longer spells that way.
type Diagnostic struct {
	Code    string `json:"code"`
	Pointer string `json:"pointer"`
	Was     string `json:"was,omitempty"`
	Now     string `json:"now,omitempty"`
}
```

Codes: `entity_type_missing`, `relation_type_missing`,
`entity_type_renamed`, `relation_type_renamed`, `field_missing`,
`field_type_changed`, `enum_option_missing`, `param_unbound`.

**`on_stale` defaults to `fail`**, and that is the decision to defend: a
diagram that silently dropped its level filter looks exactly like a
correct diagram, and a designer will believe it. A wrong picture is worse
than no picture. `best_effort` exists because sometimes seeing most of
the graph is what you need, and it is the designer's explicit choice, and
the dropped parts come back in `Result.Stale` for the UI to band across
the top.

**A rename does not rewrite the query.** When resolution succeeds by id
but the key text no longer matches, Maestro reports `*_renamed` and
leaves the stored query alone — it runs correctly in the meantime. Silently
rewriting the stored key would edit an author's document underneath them
without a version bump, which makes optimistic concurrency lie: the next
`expected_version` check would pass against a document nobody wrote.
Repair is an explicit `views.upsert`.

**Deleting a type that views depend on is allowed**, and the deletion
response lists the views it broke. Refusing with `in_use` — the way the
core spec refuses deleting a type that still has entities — is rejected
here because the cases are not alike: an entity is content and losing it
loses work; a view is derived and can be rewritten in one call. Making a
type undeletable because a six-month-old diagram mentions it would push
designers into deleting views to delete types, which is worse.

- [x] **Step 1: The tests**

- `TestARenamedRelationTypeStillRunsAndReportsItsRename` — rename
  `requires` to `depends_on` through `relation_types.upsert`, run the
  view, assert the **nodes still come back** (the id resolved) *and* a
  `relation_type_renamed` diagnostic at `/traverse/0/via/0` with
  `was: "requires"`, `now: "depends_on"`.
- `TestARenameDoesNotRewriteTheStoredQuery` — read the view back through
  `Get` after the run and assert the stored document still says
  `"requires"` and the version did not move.
- `TestADeletedTypeFailsTheRunByDefault` — `query_stale` with the full
  diagnostic list, and the view still readable through `Get`.
- `TestBestEffortDropsTheStalePartAndSaysWhatItDropped` — the same view
  with `on_stale: "best_effort"` returns the un-stale part, non-empty,
  plus the diagnostic. Non-empty is the control: an implementation that
  dropped everything would satisfy "no error".
- `TestADeletedAndRecreatedTypeResolvesByKey`.
- `TestDeletingATypeListsTheViewsItBroke` — one indexed query over
  `view_refs`, not a scan over jsonb, and the test asserts the *pointer*
  comes back so a designer is told which step broke.
- `TestAFieldDroppedFromASchemaIsAFieldMissingDiagnostic`.

- [x] **Step 2: Implement, see red, commit**

Break step 2 of the resolution order (the key fallback) and watch
`TestADeletedAndRecreatedTypeResolvesByKey` fail. Break the `fail`
default to `best_effort` and watch `TestADeletedTypeFailsTheRunByDefault`
fail — the default is the safety property, so it gets its own red.

```bash
git add internal/views/stale.go internal/views/stale_test.go
git commit -m "feat: stale-reference resolution, diagnostics and the on_stale policy"
```

**Corrections made during implementation** (landed as `6dbc0c4`,
`0c0dd1a`, `9118a20`, `2510bc8`, `3a948bc`, `82590b3`, `5aa6925` and
`e88bc05`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code,
watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline
confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL`
exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository — 1735 tests, 0 skipped).
Eleven findings. Two of them are the same shape this plan keeps finding:
a rule established correctly at one position and not carried to the next
one along.

1. **The product has no rename, so the plan's step 1 cannot be
   performed.** "Rename `requires` to `depends_on` through
   `relation_types.upsert`" describes a call that does not exist:
   `EntityTypeInput` and `RelationTypeInput` carry no id and both upserts
   are addressed **by key**, so upserting under a new key creates a
   second type and leaves the first standing — a different scenario, and
   one in which resolution by id is never exercised at all. There is no
   `UPDATE … SET key` anywhere in `internal/db/queries`. The tests
   perform the rename on the row directly, with the reason written where
   they do it. This is the sixth prescribed step in this plan not to
   reach the path it names, and it is also the argument for building
   staleness *before* a rename operation exists: id-first resolution is
   what makes such an operation addable without breaking every saved
   view in a game.

2. **`@type` is a dependency, and that is the decision Task 4 left to the
   compiler and Task 6 left to here.** `@type eq "class"` holds that
   entity type up exactly as `from[0].type` does — the compiler refuses
   the whole view when the key names nothing — so leaving it out of
   `view_refs` meant *deleting the type reported that it broke nothing*,
   and renaming it broke a view that every other reference in the
   document would have carried through by id. Resolution now resolves an
   `eq`/`neq`/`in` operand through the same three-step lookup as every
   other reference, records the `TypeRef`, and **rewrites the operand in
   the resolved leaf to the type's current key** so the compiler's own
   lookup finds it after a rename. The stored document is untouched: the
   rewrite is in the `*Resolved` a run holds, never in the jsonb column.
   The compiler's own refusal stays for the two cases resolution cannot
   see — a hand-built `*Resolved`, and a key a run binds to a parameter —
   and `TestAMisspelledTypeNameIsRefusedRatherThanDrawnAsNothing` moved
   with the decision, which also gives Task 15's `views.validate` the
   refusal without having to compile. The pattern operators still take
   the key-text road and contribute no reference, as Task 6's review
   recorded.

3. **The three-step resolution had to be carried to three positions that
   resolve a key a *second* time, and by-key resolution at any of them
   loses exactly what a rename moved.** The closures `resolveInto` hands
   itself were the easy half. The other three:
   - **`nodeScopeOf`**, which builds the projection's scope from each
     selector's type. By key it comes back holding *no type at all* after
     a rename, which `projectionScope` reads as "unresolved" and answers
     every key with a shrug — so the picture is identical and only the
     judgement is gone. `TestARenamedTypeDoesNotSwitchOffTheProjections
     TypoCheck` is what observes it, because the first test written for
     this (the picture still drawn, the rename reported) is **green under
     the mutation**: the difference shows only where a key needs
     refusing. Recorded because it is the exact shape of "a test that
     passes for the wrong reason".
   - **An `edges[]` entry's inherited relation types.** Task 9 reads them
     out of the catalogue by key rather than through `relationType`, so
     that one reference does not become two `TypeRef`s. By key a rename
     empties that list, and an empty list is an unresolved scope, which
     accepts every `label_from` without looking at a schema. They are
     read at *the step's own pointer* now.
     `TestARenamedRelationTypeStillJudgesTheEdgeLabelItDraws` needs a
     `label_from` that ought to be refused to see it, so its fixture
     renames the type and drops the field in one edit.
   - `resolveEdgeLabel`'s refusal was also not turned into a diagnostic,
     which the same test caught.

   All three positions pass `note: false`, because the pointers they
   resolve are the pointers the reference closures already reported — a
   diagnostic reported twice is a designer told to repair one thing
   twice, and `TestARenameIsReportedOnceAndNotOncePerPositionThatResolvesIt`
   is red with the flag flipped.

4. **`param_unbound` had to be reported by a whole-document pass, and the
   compiler's refusal stays where it is.** `compiler.withParam` raises
   the first unbound parameter it meets and stops, which under
   `best_effort` would abort a picture that can still be drawn without
   that leaf's set. `staleness.noteUnboundParams` walks the resolved
   leaves and reports every one; the two cannot disagree, because both
   ask the same question of the same bound map. An ad-hoc run still gets
   the compiler's `query_invalid` at the same pointer, unchanged.

5. **Staleness is a property of a stored document, and `on_stale` is
   refused on an ad-hoc run rather than accepted and ignored.** An inline
   query has no recorded past: nothing to resolve by an id a rename left
   alone, and nothing that can have moved. Wiring the diagnostics into
   `Run` as well would have turned every ordinary typo in an ad-hoc query
   into `query_stale`, which is a worse answer *and* a change to
   behaviour four tasks of tests already pin. So `Run` refuses a
   non-empty `OnStale` at `/on_stale` with the reason, and `RunView` —
   new here, and the entry point the whole file exists for — is what
   reads it. `TestOnStaleIsRefusedOnAnAdHocRunRatherThanIgnored` pins the
   refusal and the control.

6. **`best_effort` prunes on the *resolution problems*, not on the
   diagnostics, and the unit it drops is never a condition.** A predicate
   whose leaf failed to resolve comes back as the tree *without that
   leaf* — so running the set anyway silently widens it, which is exactly
   the picture `fail` is the default to prevent. The rule is: drop the
   smallest whole thing that can still be drawn — a selector, a step, an
   `edges[]` entry — and never weaken a condition; then drop what
   depended on it, to a fixed point.

   **The first test written for this could not observe the widening**,
   and that is worth recording: filtering on a field and then dropping
   that field from the schema marks every entity holding a value for it
   *invalid*, so the widened set draws nothing anyway and a broken
   implementation looks exactly like a correct one — green under the
   mutation. Narrowing an *enum's options* invalidates only the rows
   holding the option that went, which leaves the other quests valid,
   drawable and outside the filter. That is the fixture now, and the
   mutation is red with the two quests the document excludes named in the
   failure.

7. **Pruning the projection was a mechanism nothing could read, and it is
   gone.** `resolveProjection` already walks past a slot or a
   `project.fields` key it cannot judge, so the resolved projection
   handed to the pruner already lacks it. The first version pruned it a
   second time by index — with a careful comment about matching a
   document index against the shorter resolved list — and **no mutation
   of that code could be made to change a picture**. Deleted, with the
   reason written where the `/project` pointers arrive.
   `TestBestEffortKeepsTheProjectedFieldsItCanStillRead` asserts the
   outcome rather than the mechanism, so it holds whichever pass does the
   work; its fixture gives one quest a `difficulty`, because the fixture
   declares that field and no entity holds a value for it, and a key
   pruned correctly and a key never written are the same empty payload.

8. **A `Diagnostic.fatal()` predicate was written, read by nothing, and
   removed.** The obvious version is a predicate over the code — the two
   renames survivable, the other six not — and it would be a second
   statement of something resolution already says: a reference that
   resolved reports no problem. `runStored` refuses and prunes on those
   pointers, so the two can never disagree; a code-based predicate could,
   the first time a code is produced beside a reference that still
   resolves. A `ViewsBrokenBy` wrapper over `ViewsDependingOn` went the
   same way, for the same reason: one thing, one name.

9. **The plan's own step-2 mutation does not reach the key fallback, and
   the reason is a constraint.** "Break step 2 of the resolution order"
   applied inside the by-id branch — refusing when the stored id names no
   live type — leaves the whole package **green**, because `view_refs`'
   composite foreign key is `ON DELETE SET NULL`: a stored id that is not
   null always points at a live type of this game, so that arm is
   unreachable and the fallback after it is defence in depth. The
   mutation that *is* equivalent refuses the key lookup when the stored
   reference exists with a null id, which is precisely "a deleted type
   does not resolve by key", and it is red with the recreated type
   reported missing. Recorded because the same shape has now been the
   reason a prescribed mutation missed four times: the mutation has to be
   applied to the branch the scenario actually takes.

10. **A type that is gone is reported once, and the fields it declared do
    not report it again.** A step whose `to_type` has gone reaches
    entities of no known type, so every field its `where` names is
    undeclared *there* — and answering that as `field_missing` beside the
    type's own diagnostic tells a designer to repair a field that never
    moved. `fieldScope` says which of its refusals is staleness (the code
    travels on a `*scopeError`, so nothing has to be matched out of a
    message or inferred from a pointer) and which is a consequence of one
    already reported. The addressed sentence for the consequence is still
    carried in `Fields`, because it is still true and an agent should see
    every position that has to change; what it is not is a second thing
    to repair.

11. **The deletion report is a courtesy and not a lock, and the ordering
    is asserted rather than trusted.** `RemoveTypeReportingViews` composes
    in this package because the dependency runs one way — views knows
    about types and nothing in the metamodel mentions views — and it reads
    the list **before** the removal, since the same `SET NULL` that lets a
    ref row survive its type empties the column the lookup matches on.
    `TestDeletingATypeListsTheViewsItBroke` asks the same question again
    *after* the deletion and requires the empty answer, so reordering the
    two calls is red. A view saved in the gap between them is missing from
    the list and loses nothing by it: its ref row still survives with the
    key text intact, so the next run of that view reports the reference
    dead at its own pointer.

**Two things decided here that the plan did not name.**

- **Under `fail`, the refusal carries both spellings.** `QueryError`
  gains `Stale []Diagnostic` beside `Fields`, and `staleQuery` fills both
  from one list: internal/web publishes `Fields` as
  `details.fields[].path`, which is what an agent acts on, while a UI
  banding a warning over a picture wants the codes and the was/now pair.
  A resolution problem whose pointer no diagnostic covers is carried too,
  so a stale view that is also wrong for another reason does not lose the
  other reason. Task 15 publishes both.
- **`Was` and `Now` are read one way throughout**: Was is what the
  document says, Now is what the game says today, and Now is empty when
  the game says nothing. So a rename carries both keys, a missing type
  carries the key alone, a changed field carries the key and the type it
  is declared as now, and a vanished enum option carries the option
  alone. `TestEveryDiagnosticCodeHasASentenceOfItsOwn` requires every
  code's sentence to name what the document says, and requires no two
  codes to answer with the same sentence.

disabled for a reference whose type was deleted (1); the `on_stale`
default flipped to `best_effort` (2); resolution by id removed (8); a
rename resolved and never reported (6); the projection's scope built by

never reported (1); `best_effort` dropping an unresolvable condition
instead of the set that carries it (1); the broken-view list read after
the deletion (1); `on_stale` accepted and ignored on an ad-hoc run (1); a
rename reported again by the position that only reads it (3); a stale

seed set gone (1); an edges entry left behind by the step it draws (1);
by a `between` set that is gone (1); a step left reading from a set that

propagation run once instead of to a fixed point (1); a field refused

**One hygiene note, because it cost a commit.** A mutation run restored

diagnostic reached a commit. It was caught by the next full run and
fixed in `e88bc05`. The lesson is the obvious one: `git status` and a
full green run belong between the last mutation and the next commit, not
after it.

**Corrections from Task 12's review round** (landed as `0177915`,

1. **A stored ref id was followed without checking it describes *this*
   document, so a torn read drew the wrong types under a green light.**
   `storedID` matched on kind and pointer and never compared
   `view_refs.ref_key` — a column this package wrote and never read — to
   the key the document spells at that pointer. `RunView` reads the
   document and the index in **two separate pool statements, with no
   transaction and no version check**: Task 11 writes the pair in one
   transaction, and the read does not pair them, so an upsert committing
   between the two hands a run document version N beside refs version
   N+1 and every ref whose pointer survived the edit redirects by id.
   Measured on the row state that race produces for an instant — the same
   direct edit the rename tests already use, since the product can
   produce it no other way:

   ```
   as saved:   err=<nil> nodes=3 stale=[]  drawn=[quest/cook quest/defias quest/hogger]
   repointed 1 ref row to zone
   after:      err=<nil> nodes=2 drawn=[zone/westfall zone/elwynn]
               stale=[{entity_type_renamed /from/0/type was:quest now:zone}]
   ```

   The document says `quest`, the run drew **the two zones**, returned
   success, and called the disagreement a rename: the
   wrong-picture-that-looks-right `fail` is the default to prevent,
   arriving with a diagnostic that misdescribes it. One comparison closes
   it, and closes the race as a side effect: the id is followed only when
   the ref's own key is the key the document spells there. After a rename
   the two still agree — **neither the document nor the ref row is
   rewritten**, which is this file's own header decision — so the rename
   path is untouched, and a mismatch means the index is not this
   document's, where falling through to the by-key lookup answers from
   the document alone. `TestARefIdIsTrustedOnlyWhenItsKeyIsTheOneTheDocumentSpells`
   is the pin, red on the shipped code with the two zones named.

   **It is also a constraint on the rename operation that does not exist
   yet**, written where the check is: such an operation may move a type's
   key freely, but it may not tidy `view_refs.ref_key` to the new
   spelling while leaving the stored documents alone — that is exactly
   the disagreement this reads as a torn index, and every renamed view
   would then report its type missing. The two spellings move together or
   neither moves.

   **And correction 1 above needs its claim narrowed.** The two rename
   codes cannot be defended as covering a direct database edit, because
   that edit path is what this finding closes. What they cover is a
   *catalogue* that moved under a document nobody edited: a type whose
   key changed while the view and its index both kept the old spelling.
   That is the state a rename operation will produce when it is built,
   and the state the tests produce today by performing the rename the
   only way the product can.

2. **Staleness stopped at the renderer parameters, and the rename
   diagnostic's own repair instruction was refused.** `CheckRenderer` is
   called from the upsert and never from a run, so a parameter naming a
   relation type or a declared field — `contain_via`, `axis_field`,
   `x_field`, `rank_by` — was a **fourth position that turns a key into a
   declared thing** and the one position none of Task 12's three
   corrections reached. Measured:

   ```
   run after rename: err=<nil> nodes=3
     stale=[{relation_type_renamed /traverse/0/via/0 was:requires now:depends_on}]
   repair as the diagnostic prescribes:
     renderer_requirements: /renderer_params/contain_via: no relation type
     "requires" in this game: declare it with relation_types.upsert…
   ```

   The rename sentence says re-saving with the new spelling is how it
   stops being reported. Doing exactly that came back refused, at a
   pointer the designer had never been shown, with advice that would
   recreate the type they had just renamed away from — the view was
   uneditable until they guessed.

   **Resolved rather than documented as a gap**, because the alternative
   was to make the two rename sentences promise less while leaving a
   designer stuck. Three pieces, each reusing machinery that already
   exists rather than restating a rule:
   - `paramFault` carries the diagnostic code it *is*, exactly as
     `scopeError` does (finding 10 above), set at the arms of
     `requireDeclaredAs` and at the relation-type checker. The run-time
     pass keeps only the faults that carry a code, which is what stops it
     being a second implementation: a shape fault, a slot this query does
     not declare and a key `project.fields` does not carry are all real
     refusals of a *save* and none of them is something the game did.
   - The relation-type checker resolves through `staleness.relationTypeAt`
     like every other reference. On a save the staleness is nil and it is
     the plain catalogue lookup it always was; on a run the id resolves a
     renamed type and reports the spelling at the parameter's own pointer.
   - `view_refs` gains a row per type-naming parameter, at
     `/renderer_params/<name>`. **Without it the fix makes things worse
     rather than better**: a run would resolve the parameter by key, find
     nothing and report the type *missing*, refusing a view whose picture
     the rename did not change. `typeNamingKinds` is the one place that
     says which kinds are references, because the refs a save writes and
     the resolution a run performs have to agree;
     `TestEveryTypeNamingParameterKindIsARecordedReference` asserts the
     membership behaviourally, against the checkers that actually refuse
     an undeclared type, rather than against a second list.

   **A `/renderer_params` pointer is not a pointer into the query
   document**, which every other pointer in this package is. It does not
   need to be: the renderer and its parameters are stored beside the
   query, and `CheckRenderer` has always refused a bad parameter at this
   same address, so the diagnostic lands where the repair is made. After
   the fix the run names both positions, repairing both is accepted, and
   the repaired view reports nothing.

   **The deletion report gains nothing measurable from the new rows, and
   that is recorded rather than claimed otherwise.** `contain_via` is the
   only type-naming parameter, and the nested renderer *structurally
   requires* the query to draw an edges[] entry of that same relation —
   so a view naming it in the parameter already named it in the query and
   already appeared in the report. The rows are there for the id.

3. **A negated `@type` comparison is a hard dependency on purpose, and
   the reason is now written down.** Deleting the type an `@type neq`
   names cannot change what it selects — nothing is of a type this game
   no longer has — so the refusal is provably empty of consequence, where
   `eq` and `in` genuinely narrow:

   ```
   before:            err=<nil> nodes=5
   delete the type:   broke=1
   after:             err=query_stale … nodes=0
   ```

   Recorded rather than exempted, for three reasons written at
   `typeOperand`: the no-op is a property of **today's rows and not of
   the document** — resolution's own step 2 exists because deleting a
   type and re-declaring it under the same key is the common shape of a
   designer fixing a mistake, and the moment that happens the negation
   narrows again with no version bump and nothing said; exempting puts a
   second operator rule in that function, on a second axis (narrowing
   versus widening) beside the one already there (spelling versus thing),
   which is where the drift would start; and the deletion report would
   have to promise "the views whose picture changes" instead of "the
   views that name this type", which no index can answer.
   `TestANegatedTypeComparisonIsADependencyLikeAnyOther` pins it, so the
   asymmetry is a decision on the record.

4. **An ad-hoc run accepts `fail` and refuses only `best_effort`.**
   Refusing the switch outright was right about best effort and wrong
   about the default: `fail` is what an ad-hoc run already *does*, so a
   caller saying so states a true fact, and a REST layer or a UI that
   fills the field in on every request from a form whose default is the
   default was refused for having a default. Anything that is neither is
   still refused rather than read as one of the two.

5. **`best_effort` refuses a problem pruning cannot act on, rather than
   running the document whole.** The pruner handled the three document
   positions and let every other pointer fall through, pruning nothing
   and returning success — so best effort executed a document with a
   resolution problem standing against it, which is the silently widened
   picture arriving by the one road nothing watched. It was unreachable
   through the product's own writes and **unasserted in both
   directions**, which is what made it a hazard rather than a bug; finding
   2 then made it reachable, since a renderer parameter naming a deleted
   type is exactly such a pointer. Every broken pointer is now accounted
   for: the three positions drop, a `/project` pointer counts as acted on
   because resolution already pruned it one pass earlier, and anything
   else refuses. `TestBestEffortRefusesAProblemPruningCannotActOn` draws
   the case that matters — a containment renderer whose containment
   relation is gone draws one flat row of boxes, and drawing that under a
   warning is the wrong picture with a note attached.

6. **The "an empty picture reads as this game has nothing" comment now
   says what it covers.** The guard is about the *query* — every seed set
   pruned away — and not about the result, and the sentence invited the
   wrong reading. Emptiness also arrives by a road pruning cannot see: a
   narrowing schema invalidates the rows that held a value for what it
   dropped, so a seed set that survives whole comes back with nothing —
   measured at zero nodes, no error, warning attached. Refusing empty
   results is not the fix either, since a legitimately empty query is a
   thing a designer asks for.
   `TestBestEffortCanStillAnswerWithAnEmptyPictureItDidNotPrune` pins the
   measurement rather than the wish.

**One assertion added beside the six.** The dependency listing returns
one row **per reference**, not per view: a view naming a type at three
positions comes back three times. That is what the pointers are for — a
designer repairing a view wants the three positions, not the name three
times — but a caller counting rows reports "three views broken", and
nothing said so.
`TestTheDependencyListIsOneRowPerReferenceAndNotPerView` now does.

`make check` — `gofmt`, `go vet`, `golangci-lint` (0 issues), `sqlc diff`
and `go test -race ./...` — exits 0.
