### Task 13: Positions and the layout contract

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/positions.go`
- Create: `internal/views/positions_test.go`

Three service calls: `SetPositions`, `ClearPositions`, `GetPositions`.
Positions are addressed by **entity key plus type key**, never by id, so
an agent that has never read the game can write them, and the resolution
is the project-filtered lookup `EntityByKey` already provides.

**What the server does about layout: nothing.** `layout_mode` and
`layout_seed` are stored, validated and returned as a contract with the
client (this plan's open question O1), and `positions.go`'s doc comment
says **"no server code reads layout_mode beyond validating and returning
it"** — because a comment claiming the server honours a mode it never
sees is the first defect in its purest form.

Bounds, all `invalid_input` at the caller's own path:
`x` and `y` must be finite (`NaN` and both infinities refused at
`positions[<i>].x`, ahead of the CHECK the migration also carries), at
most `MaxPositions = 5000` per call — the node cap, because a call that
positions more nodes than a run can return is positioning something it
has not seen.

- [ ] Tests: `TestAPositionSurvivesAReload` (write, read back through
  `GetPositions`, assert `x`, `y` **and `pinned`** — the write-only
  candidate this task carries); `TestDeletingAnEntityDropsItsPositionAndKeepsTheView`;
  `TestPositionsArePerViewAndNotPerEntity` (two views, same entity, two
  coordinates, neither disturbing the other — this is the core spec's own
  requirement); `TestANonFinitePositionIsRefusedWithItsIndex`;
  `TestRunningAViewNeverRewritesPositions` (run the query twice with
  content added in between and assert the stored rows are byte-identical,
  which is what lets a query be edited without losing an afternoon of map
  work); `TestPositionsOfAnotherGameAreNotReachable` with its positive
  control.

- [ ] See red: drop `pinned` from the `GetPositions` scan and watch
  `TestAPositionSurvivesAReload` fail — the read-back assertion doing its
  job, and the exact defect that survived nine reviews last time.

```bash
git add internal/db/queries/views.sql internal/db/dbq internal/views/positions.go \
        internal/views/positions_test.go
git commit -m "feat: per-view node positions, bounded and read back"
```

**Corrections made during implementation** (landed as `ff0fa16`, `13dad47`
and `901d283`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped
code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the
baseline confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`,
`TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository). Eleven
findings. One of them is a cross-game overwrite the first version of
this task's own statement performed, measured rather than reasoned
about, and caught only after the test written for it stopped counting
rows — it did not reach a commit, and it was one assertion away from
doing so.

1. **The write's isolation takes two mechanisms, and the first one alone
   silently overwrote another game's arrangement.** The obvious reading
   of this file's own rule — every table's isolation is enforced in SQL —
   gives `UpsertViewPosition` no `WHERE` at all: it is an `INSERT`,
   `project_id` is written as a column value, and 0008_views.sql's two
   composite foreign keys, `(view_id, project_id)` into `views` and
   `(entity_id, project_id)` into `entities`, refuse a parent from
   another game with 23503. That is true of the insert path and **false
   of the `ON CONFLICT` path**. The conflict target is
   `(view_id, entity_id)`; `project_id` is not in the `SET` list, so the
   stored row keeps its own project id, every key stays satisfied, and
   the write is an `UPDATE` of another game's row. Measured on the first
   version of this statement: outland calling with azeroth's view id and
   its own project id moved azeroth's node to (1, 1) and returned
   `err = <nil>`.

   **And the test written to catch exactly that passed**, because it
   asserted `len(positions) == 2` for the positive control — the count is
   two whichever game wrote the coordinates. It is the plan's own
   "correct and unasserted" and "a fixture too small to distinguish any
   policy" in one place. What ships: the `DO UPDATE` is guarded on
   `view_positions.project_id = excluded.project_id`, the statement is
   `:execrows` rather than `:exec` so a guard that matches nothing is a
   refusal instead of a silent success, and
   `TestPositionsOfAnotherGameAreNotReachable` drives **both paths** —
   an entity with no stored position for the 23503, one with a stored
   position for the guard — and reads the coordinates back rather than
   the row count. Red under the guard's deletion with
   `azeroth's hogger = {X:1 Y:1}, want (12.5, -40.25)`.

2. **The Go arm that reads that statement's row count is unreachable
   from its own caller, and is recorded as such rather than dressed up.**
   `SetPositions` resolves the view inside this game and every entity
   inside it too, so a row either of them collides with carries this
   game's project id: deleting the `written == 0` branch leaves the whole
   package green, measured. It stays because the alternative is
   discarding a row count that can only be zero when an invariant of this
   package is broken, and a silent no-op is how finding 1 reached a
   commit in the first place. The guard it reads *is* load-bearing, and
   is asserted by driving the statement directly.

3. **The plan's prescribed mutation reaches its path, and the fixture
   had to be built for it.** Dropping `pinned` from the read-back — as
   `Pinned: DefaultPinned` in `GetPositions`, the shape the defect
   actually has once sqlc owns the scan — is red in
   `TestAPositionSurvivesAReload`. It is red **only because one of the
   two nodes is written `pinned: false`**: a fixture whose every node
   takes the default cannot tell a stored flag from a hard-wired one, so
   `placed()` writes one of each and
   `TestTheLayoutModeChangesNothingTheServerAnswers` carries a vacuity
   check that fails if the two ever stop differing.

4. **`PositionInput.Pinned` is a `*bool`, for the reason
   `ViewInput.LayoutSeed` is a `*int32`.** `false` is a value a caller
   means, so a plain `bool`'s zero value would store the opposite of
   0008_views.sql's column default for every caller that said nothing —
   the same defect Task 11's finding 10 removed from the layout seed,
   one column along. Red under two mutations: the pointer ignored, and
   `DefaultPinned` flipped.

5. **An empty list means two different things on the two calls, and
   that is the decision rather than an inconsistency.** `SetPositions`
   refuses one: a call that set no position changed nothing, and
   reporting a change it did not make is the silent no-op this package
   refuses everywhere else. `ClearPositions` reads a **nil** list as
   "every position of this view" and refuses an **empty** one — said
   nothing is not said none, which is the distinction markdown's
   `WriteInput.Links` draws with a pointer, and here it is what stands
   between a caller whose list of dirty nodes came out empty and a wiped
   arrangement. Both arms are red under their own mutation.

6. **The transaction around the writes is observable by nothing the
   product can do, so the test manufactures a failure.** Every refusal
   `SetPositions` can produce — the arguments, then the addresses —
   happens *before* the transaction opens, so no call a caller can make
   leaves a write to roll back and moving the loop onto the pool turns
   nothing red. That is the shape of Task 11's refs finding, closed the
   same way: `TestNothingIsStoredWhenOneWriteOfManyFails` adds a
   `CHECK (x <> 666)` to its own throwaway database so the second write
   fails with the first already written. Red with one stored position.

7. **The duplicate check is keyed on the resolved entity id, not on the
   spelling.** Two entries addressing one node are two coordinates of
   which the array order would silently pick one, so they are refused —
   and the fold that decides whether two keys are one entity is
   Postgres's own (`entities_key_key` is
   `UNIQUE (project_id, entity_type_id, lower(key))`) rather than a
   second rule in Go that could disagree with it for a character
   `strings.ToLower` and `lower()` fold differently.
   `TestTheSameEntityTwiceInOneCallIsRefused` uses `quest/hogger` and
   `QUEST/Hogger`.

8. **The cap is answered before the addresses are resolved, and the test
   proves the order rather than the number.** `MaxPositions` is
   `HardMaxNodes` — one rule, one place, asserted as an equality so the
   two cannot drift — and the oversize call in
   `TestAnEmptyOrOversizePositionsCallIsRefused` names 5001 entities
   **that do not exist**: a `not_found` answer would mean the bound is
   applied after five thousand lookups. Red with the cap removed, and
   the failure is the `not_found` the assertion names.

9. **The read-back's ordering is asserted, and checked for luck.**
   `ORDER BY et.key, e.key` is deleted and the read-back is red on three
   consecutive runs — the fixture writes `hogger` before `defias` and
   expects `defias` first, so the clause is doing visible work rather
   than agreeing with the physical order. Checked three times rather
   than once because this plan has twice recorded an ordering that held
   by accident (Task 6's correction 8, `internal/graph`'s own).

10. **Positions are absent from the run envelope, and Task 16 needs them
    there.** Task 16's step 6 runs a view after `views.set_positions` and
    asserts the four coordinates come back, and step 7 asserts they
    survive twelve more quests — but `Result` has no member for them,
    and neither this task's block nor Task 14's nor Task 15's adds one.
    It is **not** added here, and the reason first recorded for that
    was wrong: it said the envelope's shape is a contract Task 15's tool
    description is generated against, and that a half-designed member
    landing a task early is the mechanism-nothing-reads this plan
    refuses. **Task 12 had already set the opposite precedent one task
    earlier** — `Result` carries the staleness diagnostics, added by Task
    12, optional, and meaningful on a saved view only, which is the same
    shape a positions member has. So this deferral *declines* a precedent
    this package set one task ago rather than applying a rule it follows.
    It stands anyway, on the only ground that survives review: **nothing
    reads a positions member before Task 16**, so landing it here would
    be a member with no reader for three tasks, and Task 15 is where the
    tool description documenting it is written. **Task 15 must add it**,
    on `RunView` only — an ad-hoc query has no saved view and therefore
    no positions, exactly as it has no staleness — and Task 16's steps 6
    and 7 are what read it. Task 15's block now carries it as a checklist
    item rather than as a sentence here.

11. **No event kind is published for a position write, and that is a
    decision Task 15 owes rather than a loss this task records.** Task 15
    registers `view.upserted` and `view.removed` and no third kind, so a
    `view.positions` published here would be a signal nothing subscribes
    to — which is why nothing is published *here*. The first version of
    this finding stopped there, recorded the cost in positions.go and
    called it closed. That disposition was wrong: the events design
    argues at length that an invalidation must reach *viewers*, because a
    browser holding a picture has no other way to learn the picture
    changed — and a drag is that same situation, with the same reader, no
    error anywhere in between, and the hub the argument was built for
    already in the tree. Two designers in one shared session see
    different arrangements indefinitely. So the finding is now **a
    checklist item on Task 15's block**, naming the kind
    (`view.positions`), its payload (the view id and its key and nothing
    else — the rule that a payload never carries a value a client may
    treat as current applies verbatim to coordinates), its gating
    (`view.upserted`'s role and topic) and the fact that `ClearPositions`
    publishes it too. positions.go says the same and points at Task 15.

**Two things decided here that the plan did not name.**

- **A position write does not advance the view's version.** The version
  guards the query document (Task 11); a drag is not an edit of that
  document, and making it one would hand every agent holding a version a
  conflict for a change to something it never wrote.
- **The layout contract is asserted, not promised.**
  `TestTheLayoutModeChangesNothingTheServerAnswers` saves one view under
  each of the three modes over one arrangement and requires the same
  positions back from all three. The plan asks for a doc comment saying
  no server code reads `layout_mode`; a comment claiming the server
  honours a mode it never sees is defect 1 in its purest form, and a
  comment claiming it ignores one it secretly reads is the same defect
  with the sign flipped — only a test tells those two apart. Red when
  `GetPositions` returns nothing under `auto`.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running
before each (`-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported): `pinned` read back as
`DefaultPinned` (2); the caller's `pinned` ignored (1); `DefaultPinned`
flipped to false (2); `ListViewPositions`' project filter (1);
`DeleteViewPositions`' (1); `DeleteViewPosition`'s (1); the `ON CONFLICT`
project guard (1, with the coordinates as the evidence); the finite check
made a no-op (1); the empty-call refusal (1); the call cap (1); the
duplicate refusal (1); the writes moved out of the transaction onto the
pool (1); `GetPositions` branching on the layout mode (1); the read-back
ordering (1, red on three consecutive runs); the clear's empty-list guard
(1); the clear's row count discarded (1); the clear's addresses resolved
by nobody (1). Seventeen mutations, nineteen reds — plus **one confirmed
green and recorded as such**: the `written == 0` arm of the write
(finding 2), which no call this package offers can reach.

`make check` — `gofmt`, `go vet`, `golangci-lint` (0 issues), `sqlc diff`
and `go test -race ./...` — exits 0.

**Second review round** (landed as `ccd9489` and `4b2134a`; the
first of those is outside this sub-project and is committed separately
for that reason). Eight findings. The two that matter most are not in
`internal/views` at all: the cross-game overwrite finding 1 caught in
this task's own statement **exists in two more statements**, and a sweep
of the repository for its shape is what found them.

**The `ON CONFLICT` sweep, and what it covered**, recorded here so nobody
repeats it. The shape hunted for is: a conflict target that does not name
the project, a `SET` list that does not carry `project_id`, and no guard
on the `DO UPDATE`. Every upsert in `internal/db/queries/` was read. Two
carried it:

- **`UpsertRelation`** (`internal/db/queries/metamodel.sql`). Conflict
  target `(relation_type_id, source_id, target_id)`. Reproduced by
  driving the statement: one game called with its own project id and
  another game's three ids, overwrote that game's `fields`, and was
  handed back the whole stored row *carrying the other game's project
  id* — a cross-game write and a cross-game read in one statement, with
  `err = <nil>`.
- **`UpsertDocumentLink`** (`internal/db/queries/documents.sql`).
  Conflict target `(document_id, entity_id)`. Reproduced the same way:
  one game rewrote the `role` on another game's document link and got
  that game's row back.

Cleared by the sweep: Task 11's two upserts (`UpsertView`, whose
`DO UPDATE` is already guarded on the expected version, and
`InsertViewRef`, which has no `ON CONFLICT` at all), the identity upsert,
and the four metamodel key-based upserts — every one of those conflicts
on a target whose leading column *is* `project_id`, so the project is
part of the identity being matched and the conflict cannot cross a game.

Both defects are, today, unreachable from their service callers, which
resolve everything by key inside the project before they get there —
**which is exactly the status of this task's own finding 2, where the
guard was kept anyway and that was right.** The same decision is made
here for the same reason: a statement that is safe only because of how
today's caller happens to address it is a trap for tomorrow's caller.
Each gets the guard (`WHERE <table>.project_id = excluded.project_id`,
which turns a matched-nothing update into `pgx.ErrNoRows` rather than the
silent success a bare `ON CONFLICT` gives), each gets a
**direct-statement test** the way Task 11 pinned its delete filters —
`TestUpsertRelationsConflictPathCannotWriteAnotherGamesEdge` and
`TestUpsertDocumentLinksConflictPathCannotWriteAnotherGamesLink`, both
with a positive control in both directions — and each statement's comment
now says which filter is load-bearing on which path and why the composite
keys do not cover the conflict one. That is the standard this file's
header sets and those two files did not carry.

The six findings inside this sub-project:

1. **Two filters this file calls load-bearing were asserted by nothing,
   and losing either wiped another view's layout.** `DeleteViewPositions`
   and `DeleteViewPosition` both say the `view_id` filter is
   load-bearing. True of the code, false of the tests: neutering it on
   either statement left **the whole package green**. Measured:

   ```
   unmutated:  ClearPositions(route, nil) removed=2 ; route now=0 ; map now=2
   mutated:    ClearPositions(route, nil) removed=4 ; route now=0 ; map now=0
   ```

   Clearing one view destroyed another view's arrangement — which is the
   core spec's per-view requirement that
   `TestPositionsArePerViewAndNotPerEntity` already asserts for the
   *write* path, not carried one step along to the clear, inside the very
   task that recorded that standing defect.
   `TestClearingOneViewLeavesAnotherViewsArrangementStanding` holds a
   second view with its own positions and asserts it is untouched by both
   clears — the whole-view one and the single-entity one — comparing
   coordinates rather than counts. Red under each of the two mutations
   separately (`removed = 3, want 1` and `removed = 2, want 1`), and
   under each the rest of the package stays green, which is the finding
   rather than a footnote.

2. **The clear was uncapped, and its own comment said it was capped.**
   The comment on the delete loop said "the list is bounded by the same
   cap a write is". Nothing bounded it: the bound lived in
   `positionProblems`, which the clear never calls. No cap, no duplicate
   check, no transaction. Measured:

   ```
   before:  ClearPositions with 50000 addresses: removed=1 err=<nil> elapsed=25.127054791s
   after:   ClearPositions with 50000 addresses: removed=0 err=invalid_input: /entities: names 50000 entities … elapsed=2.833µs
   ```

   Fifty thousand lookups plus fifty thousand deletes, scaling linearly
   on one pooled connection, returning success — one call parking a
   connection for half a minute, in the file that got the *write's* cap
   right. `clearProblems` now applies `MaxPositions` at the caller's own
   path, and `TestAnEmptyOrOversizeClearIsRefused` is the twin of the
   write's test: its oversize list names entities that do not exist, so a
   `not_found` answer would prove the cap is applied after five thousand
   lookups. Red with the cap removed, and the failure is that
   `not_found`.

3. **The two calls refused in different orders, against their own doc
   comments.** Both say: the arguments, then the addresses, then the
   write. `ClearPositions` resolved the view first. Measured with a bad
   view and a bad key in one call:

   ```
   Set   -> invalid_input: /positions/0/entity_key: is required
   Clear -> not_found: no view "nosuchview" in this game
   ```

   The argument check is hoisted into `clearProblems` and called before
   `ViewByKey`, and `TestAPositionCallRefusesItsArgumentsInTheSameOrder`
   asserts the two calls **agree** rather than that either is right — a
   rule two calls state and one follows is worth less than no rule. Red
   with the check moved back below the view lookup.

4. **Finding 10's reasoning was wrong and its decision was right**, which
   is why that finding is rewritten above rather than reversed. Task 12
   had already put an optional, saved-view-only member — the staleness
   diagnostics — on `Result` one task earlier, so "the envelope is Task
   15's contract" was not the rule this package followed. The deferral
   stands on the narrower ground that nothing reads a positions member
   before Task 16, and Task 15's block now carries it as a checklist
   item.

5. **Finding 11's disposition changed from a recorded loss to a decision
   Task 15 must make.** Rewritten above; the checklist item on Task 15's
   block names `view.positions`, its payload, its gating and the fact
   that `ClearPositions` publishes it too, and positions.go points there
   instead of recording a cost and stopping.

6. Nothing else. The tree, the eleven corrections of the first round and
   the conflict-path defect and its fix were re-verified by mutation
   against a live database before this round started.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each (`-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, 728 tests run and **zero**
`SKIP` across `internal/metamodel`, `internal/markdown` and
`internal/views`): `UpsertRelation`'s conflict guard absent (1, with the
returned row's foreign project id as the evidence);
`UpsertDocumentLink`'s absent (1, the same); `DeleteViewPositions`'
`view_id` filter (1); `DeleteViewPosition`'s (1); the clear's cap (1);
the clear's refusal order (1). Six mutations, six reds, and under each of
the four code mutations the rest of the package is green.

`make check` exits 0 after both commits.

---

### Task 14: Background assets

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/assets.go`
- Create: `internal/views/assets_test.go`

Upload is **REST-only, from the browser**: pushing megabytes of base64
through an MCP tool call to save a human from opening the UI is the wrong
trade, so the agent references an asset that already exists. The MCP
surface gets `views.list_assets` and `views.set_background` and no upload.

Bounds: 8 MB per asset, and `image/png`, `image/jpeg`, `image/webp` only.
**SVG is refused** — it is a script-execution vector when served inline,
and a raster map is what a designer has anyway.

The mime is **sniffed from the bytes**, never trusted from the caller's
`Content-Type` or the filename, and `width`/`height` are decoded from the
image rather than accepted as arguments — which is why they are the third
write-only candidate this plan names, and why step 7 reads them back
through `views.list_assets`.

- [ ] Tests: `TestAnSVGIsRefusedWhateverItCallsItself` (bytes of an SVG
  sent with `Content-Type: image/png` — the case a filename check misses);
  `TestTheMimeIsSniffedNotTrusted`; `TestAnOversizeAssetIsRefusedBeforeItIsRead`
  (the `http.MaxBytesReader` bound, so the refusal happens before eight
  megabytes are buffered); `TestWidthAndHeightAreDecodedAndReadBack`;
  `TestAnAssetIsServedWithANoSniffHeaderAndItsOwnContentType`;
  `TestDeletingAnAssetNullsTheBackgroundOfEveryViewUsingIt`;
  `TestAnAssetOfAnotherGameCannotBecomeThisViewsBackground` with its
  positive control.

- [ ] The serving route is `GET /g/{slug}/assets/{id}` registered through
  `registerContentRoute` — a GET, so viewers reach it — with
  `Content-Type` from the stored mime, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`,
  and a long `Cache-Control` because an asset's bytes never change (there
  is no update path; a new image is a new asset).

```bash
git add internal/views/assets.go internal/views/assets_test.go internal/db/queries/views.sql internal/db/dbq
git commit -m "feat: background image assets, sniffed, bounded and served"
```

**Corrections made during implementation** (landed as `0c09686`,
`b392711`, `5424567`, `2065889`, `740a858` and `6a86b58`; every mutation
named below was applied to the shipped code, watched fail the test that
claims it, and restored, with the baseline confirmed green and confirmed
*running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP` across the
repository — 1807 tests, 0 skipped, exit 0). Eleven findings. Two of them
change files this task's own block does not name, and one is a duplicate
store the spec itself carries.

1. **The background was two stores for one value, and the renderer
   parameters lost.** `0008_views.sql` carries `background_asset_id`,
   `background_scale` and `background_offset` as columns on `views`;
   `renderers.go` carried the same three names as parameters of `map`,
   with a `Requires` rule of their own — and the spec's §5.1 table lists
   them as parameters while its own DDL lists them as columns, so this
   is inherited from the design rather than introduced by Task 10. Two
   stores for one value is a drift with a date on it, and the columns
   win for a reason a jsonb blob cannot answer: only a column can carry
   `FOREIGN KEY (background_asset_id, project_id)` into `view_assets`,
   which is what refuses another game's image, and only a column can
   carry `ON DELETE SET NULL`, which is what detaches every view's
   background when the image is deleted. A uuid inside `renderer_params`
   gets neither — `kindUUID`'s own comment said as much ("checked for
   shape here and for existence there") — so deleting an asset would
   have left a dangling id in every map view in the game and the plan's
   own `TestDeletingAnAssetNullsTheBackgroundOfEveryViewUsingIt` could
   not have held.

   So the three parameters are **removed from the catalogue**, and
   `kindUUID` and `kindPoint` go with them, because each was declared by
   exactly one of the three and a kind no parameter declares is what
   `TestAParameterKindCannotBeHalfAdded` refuses. Task 10's finding 17
   rule — a scale and an offset with no image place nothing — moved to
   `SetBackground` rather than being restated, and a third rule was
   carried one step further along the same line: a background stored
   under a renderer that draws none is read by nothing, so
   `Renderer.ReadsBackground` is the catalogue's one statement of which
   renderers read one and `TestOnlyARendererThatDrawsABackgroundAcceptsOne`
   drives **every** renderer in the catalogue through the setter, with a
   vacuity check that fails if a run goes the same way for all six.

   **The refusal points at where the value went**, which is the half a
   plain deletion would have lost: `movedParams` gives the three names a
   sentence naming `views.set_background`, consulted only in the
   unknown-parameter arm. It changes the *wording* of a refusal and can
   never change the decision — a name in it is a parameter of no
   renderer, so it is refused by the same arm any misspelling is. That
   ordering is deliberate and is the same one `svgLooking` follows below.

2. **This task cannot be done in the two files it names, and the plan's
   own checklist says so without noticing.** Its file list is
   `internal/views/assets.go` and its test, and its `git add` line names
   neither `internal/web` nor `cmd/maestro` — while the checklist asks
   for `GET /g/{slug}/assets/{id}` registered through
   `registerContentRoute`, and two of its seven tests
   (`TestAnOversizeAssetIsRefusedBeforeItIsRead`,
   `TestAnAssetIsServedWithANoSniffHeaderAndItsOwnContentType`) are HTTP
   tests. Upload is REST-only by this task's own decision, and **no task
   in this plan registers an upload route at all**: Task 15's list is
   nine MCP tools and their mirror, and an upload has no tool. A
   REST-only upload nothing serves is the mechanism-nothing-reads this
   plan refuses, so the surface ships here: `internal/web/api_view_assets.go`
   with four routes, `Options.Views`, and `cmd/maestro/main.go` building
   the service — because a route on a service `main.go` never constructs
   is the same defect one layer up.

   **The path is `/api/games/{game}/view-assets/{id}`, not the plan's
   `/g/{slug}/assets/{id}`.** Every other content route in this product
   is `/api/games/{game}/…`, and the difference is not cosmetic:
   `registerProjectRoute` resolves the game from `{game}` and
   `TestEveryGameScopedRouteGoesThroughRequireProject` walks the pattern
   list for exactly that placeholder — a route spelled `{slug}` would
   resolve no scope and would be **invisible to the guard that says so**.
   `view-assets` rather than `assets` because `/static/` already serves
   the product's own assets and one word for two things is how a reader
   ends up in the wrong file.

3. **The plan's `http.MaxBytesReader` cannot fire, and shipping it would
   have made the refusal worse.** The domain reads through an
   `io.LimitReader` of `MaxAssetBytes+1` in the same read path, so a
   transport limiter set at the same cap is never handed the byte that
   would trip it — measured: a 8 MB + 1 KB upload came back with the
   domain's `/bytes: is larger than 8388608 bytes…`, never the
   transport's. Setting the transport a byte tighter *does* make it
   fire, and what it wins the race with is `http: request body too
   large` in place of a sentence telling a designer to scale the image
   down. The bound stays in one place, where it is applied while
   reading; the handler passes `r.Body` unwrapped and says why. This is
   the seventh prescribed step in this plan not to reach the path it
   names.

4. **What was decided about the decoder as an attack surface**, since
   this is the one place in Maestro where hostile bytes meet a parser.
   - **Nothing decodes pixels.** `png.DecodeConfig` and
     `jpeg.DecodeConfig` read the header and return without allocating
     the pixel buffer; WebP is parsed here. A decompression bomb never
     expands inside this process at all.
   - **The decoder is chosen by the sniffed mime, not by a second
     sniff.** `image.DecodeConfig` would re-sniff through the registry
     of whatever formats some other package happened to blank-import —
     a second judgement that can disagree with the stored one, and the
     stored mime is what the serving route later answers with.
     Dispatching on the mime this file already decided makes "the format
     we say it is" and "the format we parsed it as" one decision.
   - **WebP is parsed rather than depended on.**
     `golang.org/x/image/webp` is a full lossy and lossless decoder for
     a job that needs fourteen bits of a chunk header. `webpConfig`
     reads the first RIFF chunk in all three of its shapes (VP8X, VP8,
     VP8L), bounds-checks every offset against the buffer it already
     holds, never seeks by a length the file declares — a length is the
     one number an attacker picks freely — allocates nothing and loops
     over nothing.
   - **A header the parsers believe is still bounded, and the bound is
     for the client.** The parsers report whatever the file claims, and
     a claim is free: forty bytes of crafted IHDR say 30000×30000. This
     process never expands it; the browser drawing the background does,
     at 1.6 GB of RGBA in a designer's tab. `MaxAssetDimension` (20000 a
     side) and `MaxAssetPixels` (40 MP) refuse both shapes a bomb takes
     — the enormous square, and the one-pixel-tall strip a per-side
     bound alone lets through. `hugePNG` in the tests builds exactly
     that forty-byte file, CRC and all.
   - **The size bound is applied while reading**, one byte past the cap,
     and the byte count is asserted rather than the refusal alone — see
     finding 5.
   - **SVG is refused by an allowlist, not by recognising it.** Three
     magic numbers; anything else is refused, so a format nobody has
     thought of is refused for the same reason. That is the lesson
     `internal/markdown`'s `safeDestination` records after a denylist
     let `javascript:` through an autolink. `svgLooking` exists only to
     choose a better sentence for the file every designer will actually
     try, and cannot reach the decision — proved from both sides:
     disabling it leaves the refusal standing with the general sentence,
     and making it answer true for everything is red on the control that
     is not SVG.
   - **The filename is never interpreted.** Not as a format hint, not as
     a path, and not echoed into any response header — the serving route
     sends a `Content-Type` and no `Content-Disposition`, so there is no
     filename-injection surface to bound rather than a bounded one.

5. **"Refused" and "refused before it was read" are different claims,
   and so are "too big" and "not stored truncated".** The first is
   asserted by a `countingReader` that records how many bytes the upload
   pulled: `io.ReadAll(body)` in place of the limited read is red with
   "the upload pulled 67108864 bytes off the reader". The second needed
   its own test and its own fixture. Reading exactly `MaxAssetBytes`
   instead of one more cannot tell a file of that size from the front of
   a larger one, and the first version of that assertion was **red for
   the wrong reason**: padded with a PNG, `png.DecodeConfig` walks
   chunks past the header and trips over the padding, so the standard
   library killed the mutation rather than the test. The fixture is a
   JPEG now — `jpeg.DecodeConfig` returns at the frame header and never
   looks at the padding — and the mutation is red with `got <nil>`: the
   truncated body was **stored**, which is what the +1 exists to
   prevent.

6. **Deleting an asset takes two mechanisms, and they are deliberately
   split so the test can read which does what.** The null on
   `background_asset_id` is `0008_views.sql`'s
   `ON DELETE SET NULL (background_asset_id)` and nothing in Go touches
   that column — removing the action from the migration is red with
   23503, which is the only way to see that the constraint is what
   detaches the image. What Go does, in the same transaction and
   *before* the delete (the column is how the views are found), is reset
   `background_scale` and `background_offset` to their defaults: a
   constraint cannot, and leaving them would create the exact state
   `SetBackground` refuses to store — two knobs placing an image that is
   gone. Both halves are asserted, with a control view backed by a
   different asset that must be untouched.

7. **`ActorConstraintViolation` named the two `updated_by_` columns and
   stopped being complete two sub-projects ago.** `view_assets` has no
   update path — an asset's bytes never change — so its only audit
   columns are `created_by_user_id` and `created_by_token_id`, and an
   upload with another game's token came back as a raw SQLSTATE 23503
   over a generated constraint name: the same defect that function
   exists to remove, one column prefix along. Both prefixes now live in
   one `actorColumns` slice in `internal/metamodel`, shared rather than
   copied, which is the standard Task 11 set when it exported the
   function. Recorded and **not** fixed: `internal/markdown`'s documents carry
   `created_by_` columns too and have no caller of this mapping at all, so a
   document written with a foreign token still reports `internal_error`
   — defensible (the actor is never caller-supplied, which is the
   argument `metamodel/errors.go` makes for `ErrActorNotInGame` not
   being a wire code) but unasserted on that side.

8. **Two mutations confirmed green and recorded as such.**
   - **The `nosniff` header on the serving route is redundant with
     `securityHeaders`, and each masks the other.** Removing either one
     alone leaves `TestAnAssetIsServedWithANoSniffHeaderAndItsOwnContentType`
     green; removing both is red. The route sets it anyway, because this
     is the one response in the product whose safety depends on it — a
     browser sniffing its own type out of bytes a designer uploaded is
     the whole of the risk the closed mime list bounds — and a global
     middleware narrowed later must not take it away from here silently.
     The masking is written down rather than left to be discovered,
     which is the standard Task 8's finding on the walk's paired filters
     set.
   - **`map`'s `Doc` naming `views.set_background` is masked by its
     `RequiresDoc`**, which names it too, so deleting one leaves the
     description test green. The test asserts the *fact* — the generated
     description tells an agent where the background went — rather than
     a sentence, which is the right assertion and is why the mutation
     survives.

9. **A RIFF container that is not a WebP was sniffed as one.** Dropping
   the `WEBP` half of the magic number left the whole suite green: a WAV
   file was accepted as `image/webp` and then refused a step later by
   the chunk parser, telling a designer their WebP was corrupt when they
   had uploaded a sound. Correct behaviour, unasserted — the standing
   "correct and unasserted" shape — so the SVG test grew a WAV case that
   requires the refusal to say the bytes are no image at all. Red under
   the mutation now.

10. **The serving route's `Cache-Control` overrides
    `requireCaller`'s `no-store`, deliberately.** `no-store` exists so a
    response computed for one identity is not replayed to another;
    `private, max-age=31536000, immutable` says exactly that to every
    cache but this browser, `requireCaller`'s
    `Vary: Cookie, Authorization` is left alone and is what keys the
    browser's own copy, and an asset's bytes never change because there
    is no update path. It is the same override `events.go` makes for its
    own reason, and it is written where it is made rather than left for
    a reviewer to notice.

11. **Recorded for Task 15, and it is more than the plan's two tools.**
    `views.list_assets` and `views.set_background` are `ListAssets` and
    `SetBackground` here and have no MCP reader yet — the plan's own
    sequencing, the shape Task 10's finding 12 named. Two things go with
    them. The **read-back sweep's third clause** (`views.list_assets`
    reporting `width`, `height` and `mime`) is already asserted through
    the service and needs asserting through the tool, because the tool
    is a second projection of those columns. And `views.set_background`'s
    arguments are `asset_id` (nullable — nil is how a background is
    cleared, and it is the only way), `scale` and `offset`, the last
    two as `{"x":…,"y":…}` and **not** the `[x, y]` array the spec's
    §5.1 table implies: the column is an object, the array spelling
    belonged to the `kindPoint` parameter that finding 1 deleted.

**Second review round** (landed as `84755cf`, `0e329f2`, `e505dbe`,
`c5fbf1d` and `c03aef8`; the markdown and metamodel work is `84755cf`
and is deliberately its own commit, since it changes two packages this
task's block does not name). Eight findings, every one confirmed by
reproduction or by mutation before it was touched, and one note
recorded without a change. The security core the round was aimed at
held: thirty-four crafted inputs, no panic on any malformed WebP chunk,
both dimension bounds firing, all three deletion mechanisms pinned by
constraints, and the one polyglot that stores inert because the
security headers wrap the whole chain outermost.

12. **The rule finding 1 established reached one of the two writers of
    the column it governs.** `Renderer.ReadsBackground` was called "the
    catalogue's one statement of which renderers read a background" and
    was consulted by `SetBackground` and by nothing else; `UpsertView`
    writes the same row and never asked. Reproduced: save with `map`,
    set a background, upsert the same key with `graph`, and the row
    comes back `renderer=graph background_asset_id=eb00a918-…
    scale=3 offset={"x": 10, "y": -4}` — the exact state the setter
    refuses, reached through the front door. The refusal made it worse
    by naming `views.upsert` as the way to change the renderer, which
    is the call that produced it. **Seventeenth instance of the standing
    pattern, inside the correction that established the rule.**

    `UpsertView` refuses it now, after the version check so a caller
    that is also stale hears the version first, and the setter's message
    names the two calls in the order that works ("change the renderer to
    `map` first, then set this background") rather than pointing at the
    hole. A refusal rather than a silent clear, for the reason every
    write in this package refuses rather than repairs: a designer's
    placed world map is not an agent's to discard while editing a query,
    and the repair is one named call — which the test drives, both
    halves, because a refusal whose stated recovery does not work is
    worse than no message.

    `TestAnOrdinaryUpsertLeavesABackgroundStanding` changed with it. It
    wrote its background directly onto a `graph` view, so it was pinning
    "an edit leaves a background standing" over a row that can no longer
    exist; it is a `map` view now, editing map's own `snap` parameter,
    which is the case that was actually interesting.

13. **Every bound was per asset and nothing bounded the aggregate.**
    Eight megabytes an image, forty megapixels a canvas, and no cap on
    how many: measured, twelve uploads under one filename all landed,
    and so would twelve thousand. Any editor — a designer, or an agent's
    token, and one token is one game — could push unbounded bytes into
    Postgres eight megabytes at a time. The file header argued that
    `MaxAssetBytes` protects memory; nothing protected disk.

    `MaxAssetsPerGame` is a hundred, checked in Go in the transaction
    that inserts. **A count and not an aggregate byte total, and the
    reason is the bytea**: `bytes` is toasted and compressed, so summing
    `octet_length` over it detoasts every stored image on every upload —
    the more honest-looking bound is the one that reads the whole game's
    pixels to decide whether to accept eight megabytes. A count is
    answered from `view_assets_project_idx` without touching an image,
    and it composes with `MaxAssetBytes` into a worst case that can be
    *stated* rather than measured: 800 MB a game, a number an operator
    can multiply. **The race is recorded rather than closed**: two
    uploads racing each other both count `MaxAssetsPerGame-1` and both
    land, so the stored total can overshoot by the number of concurrent
    uploaders. This is a quota and not a security boundary — what it
    exists to stop is unbounded growth, which a handful of browsers
    cannot cause — and locking the game to make it exact would serialise
    every upload against a number nobody reads.

    Second half: the listing had **no limit at all**, in a package whose
    query file contained exactly one `LIMIT`, while
    `view_assets_project_idx`'s own comment said its order existed "so a
    page can be sought to rather than read whole and sorted".
    `ListViewAssetsPage` is a keyset on `(created_at, id)` matching its
    `ORDER BY`, through `internal/paging` like every other listing here;
    the id tiebreak is load-bearing rather than defensive, because a
    browser uploading a folder writes several rows inside one clock
    tick. The REST route reads `cursor` and `limit` and answers
    `next_cursor`, under the names `api_docs.go` and `api_metamodel.go`
    already use. The cursor's fingerprint carries the project and
    `view_assets`, and both halves are asserted: another game's cursor
    and the *view* listing's cursor are each refused at `/cursor`.

14. **Four surviving mutations, all the shape finding 9 found and fixed
    once.** Each applied to shipped code, run against the whole package:
    the PNG magic number cut from eight bytes to four — green; the JPEG
    one from three to two — green; the WebP VP8 start-code check
    disabled — green; `svgLooking`'s xml-declaration arm dropped —
    green. Red for contrast, unchanged: both dimension bounds, the empty
    body, `svgLooking`'s other arm, the VP8L signature, the VP8X
    increment. **The VP8 one is not cosmetic**: with that check gone, a
    RIFF/WEBP container whose first chunk is `VP8 ` followed by ten
    arbitrary bytes yields in-bounds dimensions and the garbage is
    *stored* as an image.

    Finding 9's single WAV case is a table now —
    `TestEveryByteOfEveryMagicNumberIsLoadBearing` — one row per magic
    number one byte short, plus the VP8 chunk with a wrong start code,
    plus an XML declaration whose `<svg` is past anything a prefix check
    sees. Every row shares the control that nothing was stored, which is
    what the VP8 row actually needs, and the positive controls (all five
    well-formed shapes) are in the same test so no row can be passing
    because uploads are broken. All four mutations are red now.

15. **The shared actor list was one column short, and the gap finding 7
    recorded is closed.** Sweeping every composite key into `api_tokens`
    across the migrations finds a fifth and sixth column:
    `author_user_id` / `author_token_id`, under a byte-identical
    constraint shape. The reviewer named the table `document_comments`;
    there is no such table — it is `document_versions`
    (`0007_documents.sql:177-183`), a version being authored once and
    never edited, which is why it spells its actor `author_` rather than
    `updated_by_`. The column is right and the finding stands.
    **Extending a shared helper rather than copying it was the correct
    move and still stopped one prefix short**, which is the pattern
    recurring inside the fix again — the third separate discovery of the
    same list being incomplete.

    So the list is not defended by a list any more.
    `TestEveryCompositeTokenKeyIsNamedInActorColumns` asks `pg_constraint`
    for every foreign key whose target is `api_tokens` — which is an
    actor column by construction, nothing else references that table —
    and requires the mapping to recognise the name Postgres generated
    for each. Nine keys today, with a vacuity guard, a negative control
    and a wrong-SQLSTATE control. A migration adding a fourth prefix
    fails in the run that creates it.

    The markdown gap is closed rather than left. The defence recorded
    for it — the actor is never caller-supplied, so `internal_error` is
    the honest wire code — is an argument about the *wire* and not about
    the log line, which was a generated constraint name; the sentinel
    stays off the wire and the three write paths call the mapping now.
    `TestADocumentWrittenWithAnotherGamesTokenIsRefusedAsSuch` drives a
    write and a soft delete with a foreign token, each with its positive
    control, and both mappings are red under mutation. The two arms on
    `InsertDocumentVersion` are **unreachable today and kept**, with the
    reason written where they are: a version is never inserted outside
    the transaction that wrote its document, and `documents`' own key is
    checked first.

16. **The route-level no-sniff header was unassertable where it stood.**
    Finding 8 recorded the masking honestly and stopped there — but the
    line's own comment calls it load-bearing, and deleting it left the
    whole web suite green, which is exactly what the comment says must
    not happen. `TestTheServingRoutesNoSniffHeaderIsItsOwn` calls
    `handleServeViewAsset` directly with no middleware in front of it,
    which is the only assertion in that package able to tell the two
    sources apart; it is red when the route's line is removed. The other
    two headers the handler owns are asserted beside it, so a reader
    arriving there sees the whole of what the route promises.

17. **A plan reference was on the wire.** `map`'s `Doc` said the
    coordinates were "the ones designers dragged (Task 13's positions)",
    and the generated description really carried it — 6447 characters
    including that string, read by agents that have no plan and no task
    numbering. It came from Task 10 and survived this task's own rewrite
    of the sentence around it. It names `views.set_positions` now, which
    is a thing a caller can act on, and the sweep is a test rather than
    a reading: `TestTheRendererDescriptionNamesNothingOnlyThisRepositoryKnows`
    refuses a task number, a spec section, a numbered finding or a
    source filename anywhere in the generated text, with a length guard
    so it cannot pass over nothing.

18. **One default, three stores, now one.** `ClearBackgroundKnobsForAsset`
    hardcoded `1` and `{"x":0,"y":0}`, the Go constants say them again,
    and `0008_views.sql` declares them a third time. Postgres has the
    one-store spelling: `SET background_scale = DEFAULT,
    background_offset = DEFAULT` writes exactly what the DDL declares,
    so changing a default in the migration cannot leave the reset
    writing the old one. The Go constants stay — `SetBackground` has to
    have something to *write* when a caller says nothing — but they are
    no longer a second answer to what "reset" means.

19. **What the background write does to the audit columns, decided:
    nothing, and here is why.** Both background writes fire the
    timestamp trigger, so a view reads as modified while
    `updated_by_user_id` / `updated_by_token_id` still point at whoever
    last edited the query. The alternative is worse: `updated_by` on
    `views` answers "who wrote what this view *says*" — the query
    document and the renderer parameters, the pair a reader compares
    against a version they hold — and repointing it at someone who
    changed no part of that document would make one row disagree with
    itself. Placement carries no attribution anywhere in this
    sub-project: `view_positions` has no audit columns at all, and a
    dragged node is the same kind of act. "Who moved the map" is a
    column on the placement, in the change that wants it for positions
    too. Written in the statement and in `SetBackground`.

20. **Two things recorded rather than changed.**
    - **The dimension bounds are per canvas, and an animated file has
      more than one frame.** APNG's `acTL` and WebP's ANIM/ANMF both sit
      past where the header decode stops, so nothing here ever sees a
      frame count: `MaxAssetPixels` bounds one canvas and a browser's
      cost is that canvas times however many frames it holds decoded. No
      bound today, judged. `MaxAssetBytes` already caps the compressed
      whole and every frame's data is inside it — animation buys the
      same bytes replayed rather than more of them — and the only
      *cheap* check is the WebP one, a flag bit `webpConfig` already has
      in hand; refusing animated WebP while APNG walks through would be
      a bound that reads as protection and is not one, which is worse
      than the gap stated plainly. The change, if a tab is ever measured
      falling over, is both formats at once and a test per format.
    - **A stale sentence in the file header, found while reading it.**
      The decoder section still said "internal/web wraps the request
      body in http.MaxBytesReader over the same constant" — the wrapper
      finding 3 measured, found unable to fire, and removed. The comment
      described code that is not there, in the one paragraph a reader
      goes to for exactly this question. It now says what is true and
      points at `api_view_assets.go`'s header for the argument.

    Not a finding, recorded because it was seen:
    `TestProjectInviteListingAttributesCreatorAndFlagsRevoked`
    (`internal/web`) failed once in a full-suite run and passed on
    every rerun, including at `-count=3` and in two subsequent full
    runs. It touches nothing this round changed. Flaky, unowned, filed
    here rather than lost.
