### Task 15: The `views.*` MCP tools, the REST mirror and the SSE kinds

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/web/mcp_views.go`
- Create: `internal/web/api_views.go`
- Create: `internal/web/mcp_views_test.go`
- Create: `internal/web/api_views_test.go`
- Modify: `internal/web/mcp.go` (`MCPDeps.Views`, registration)
- Modify: `internal/web/mcp_errors.go` and `internal/web/api_metamodel.go` (four new arms)
- Modify: `internal/web/server.go` (the routes)
- Modify: `internal/web/publish.go` or `internal/views/events.go` (the `view.*` kinds)
- Modify: `cmd/maestro/main.go` (build the service)

Nine tools, exactly the spec's §5.5:

| Tool | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `views.list` | saved views in the game; slim rows (key, name, renderer, stale flag) |
| `views.get` | one view including its query and renderer params |
| `views.upsert` | create or replace by `(project, key)`, with `expected_version` |
| `views.remove` | delete a view, its positions and its refs |
| `views.run` | execute a saved view, **or an inline query**, and return the envelope |
| `views.validate` | compile and validate without saving and without executing |
| `views.set_positions` | write `[{entity_type, entity_key, x, y, pinned}]` |
| `views.clear_positions` | drop positions for a view, or for listed entities |
| `views.set_background` | point a view at an existing asset, with scale and offset |

Two of them carry an argument worth defending in the description.
**`views.run` accepts an inline `query` with no saved view**: "which
quests can a Mage reach?" asked once, in conversation, should not leave a
saved artefact behind in the game. **`views.validate` exists so composing
a view is a loop**: an agent writing a five-step traversal against an
unfamiliar game will get it wrong twice, and validating without executing
is fast, free of timeouts, and returns the same structured errors, so the
loop closes in seconds instead of after a five-second graph walk.

**A misspelled type is refused in one half of `@type` and silently empty
in the other, and the description has to say so.** `@type eq`, `neq` and
`in` compile against the row's type id, so a key this game does not
declare is a refusal with a pointer (Task 6's correction 4). The pattern
operators cannot be answered by an id and compile against the type's key
through a scalar subquery, so `@type matches "qeust*"` draws nothing and
says nothing — the same failure mode the equality arms were changed to
refuse. That is defensible for a pattern, which is a search rather than a
name, but an agent that learned "a wrong type key is refused" from the
equality arms will read the empty picture as an answer, so `views.run`
and `views.validate` must state the split.

**A timed-out run carries advice that `mcpErrorFor` currently throws
away, and this task is what stops it.** Task 7's `*views.TimeoutError`
unwraps to the `pgconn.PgError` carrying 57014, so `metamodel.IsRetryable`
admits it and the wire code stays `retryable` — one code for one recovery,
as intended. But `mcpErrorFor`'s retryable arm deliberately drops the
error's own message and substitutes a generic contention sentence, which
is right for a lock wait and would discard the one thing a timed-out view
adds: the elapsed budget and the three bounds to lower. So `mcpErrorFor`
needs an arm for `*views.TimeoutError` **before** its `IsRetryable` arm,
answering `retryable` with that error's own message, and
`writeDomainError` needs the same. A test asserts the message reaches the
caller, because the alternative is a sentence written in Task 7, asserted
by a unit test, and read by nobody.

**`views.run`'s description must say what `truncated.depth` measures.**
Task 8 measures it — the sentence this block used to carry, that it is
not measured until Task 8 lands, was removed by that task's own commit —
but the flag is exact about the *traversal* rather than about the picture,
and only a description can say so on the wire: it is set when a walk had
a hop left to make when its depth bound stopped it, whether or not the
step's `to_type` or `where` would have drawn what lay there, and a step
of exactly one hop is a neighbour query that never sets it. The same
paragraph carries `truncated.nodes`/`edges`' neighbour fact: an edge's
endpoints are not guaranteed to appear in `nodes` — the two caps are
independent, and an `edges: [{between: …}]` entry can legitimately draw a
relation between sets the document did not draw as nodes.

- [x] **Registration goes through `addScopedTool`**, like every other
  tool, so `TestEveryMCPToolGoesThroughAddScopedTool` passes; **routes go
  through `registerContentRoute`** with their method named, so
  `TestEveryContentRouteIsRegisteredAsContent` and
  `TestEveryContentWriteRouteRefusesAViewer` pass — and the second of
  those is what makes this plan's open question O3 (running is viewer,
  saving is editor) true rather than intended.

- [x] **The four new wire codes**, added to `mcpErrorFor` and
  `writeDomainError` **in the same commit as the sentinels they map**:
  `query_invalid` → 400 with `fieldDetails`, `renderer_requirements` →
  422, `limit_exceeded` → 400, `query_stale` → 409 with the diagnostics
  under `details.stale`. `statusForCode` gains the three that are not
  422 by default. The convention test:

```go
// TestEveryViewsSentinelHasAWireCode iterates views.Sentinels() rather
// than repeating a list, so a fifth sentinel added later cannot be
// forgotten on either surface.
func TestEveryViewsSentinelHasAWireCode(t *testing.T) {
	for _, sentinel := range views.Sentinels() {
		result := mcpErrorFor(context.Background(), "views.run", Caller{}, sentinel)
		code := codeOfResult(t, result)
		if code == errCodeInternal {
			t.Errorf("%v maps to internal_error: a caller-fixable failure must never do that", sentinel)
		}
	}
}
```

  and its REST twin, alongside the existing
  `TestWriteDomainErrorIsTheRESTTwinOfMCPErrorFor`.

- [x] **`/events` gains `view.upserted` and `view.removed`** per game, so
  a designer watching a view is told it changed. Whether the UI re-runs
  automatically or shows a refresh affordance is sub-project 5's; this
  task ships the kinds and asserts they arrive, with
  `TestAViewEventReachesAViewerAndATokenAlike`.

- [x] **Decide `view.positions`, and decide it here.** Task 13 publishes
  no event for a position write, because the two kinds above are the two
  the transport registers and a third would have reached nothing. Its
  first review round recorded that as a loss in a comment; this round
  changed the disposition, because the argument the events design makes
  for an invalidation is that a browser holding a picture has no other
  way to learn the picture changed — and a drag is that situation
  exactly, with the same reader, no error anywhere in between, and the
  hub the argument was built for already in the tree. Two designers in
  one session otherwise see different arrangements indefinitely. So this
  task decides it rather than inheriting a paragraph:
  - the kind is `view.positions`, per game, beside the two above;
  - the payload is **the view id and its key, and nothing else** — the
    standing rule that a payload never carries a value a client may
    treat as current applies verbatim, and coordinates are exactly such
    a value: the reader re-reads through `views.get`/`views.run`, which
    is also what keeps a payload from leaking an arrangement to a
    subscriber whose scope the read would have refused;
  - the gating is `view.upserted`'s, the same minimum role and the same
    per-game topic, because a caller who may not see the view may not
    learn its nodes moved either;
  - and it is published from `ClearPositions` as well as `SetPositions`,
    since a wiped arrangement is the invalidation that matters most.
  If Task 15 decides against it, the decision and its reason go in this
  block — what may not happen again is the question being answered by
  nobody.

- [x] **The positions member on `Result`.** Task 13's finding 10 defers
  it here and Task 16's steps 6 and 7 read it: on `RunView` only, since
  an ad-hoc query has no saved view and therefore no positions, exactly
  as it has no staleness. The deferral's stated reason was that the
  envelope is this task's contract; the honest reason is narrower, and
  Task 13's corrected block says so.

- [x] **The read-back sweep this task owes.** Through the MCP tools, not
  the service: upsert a view with `renderer_params`, `description`,
  `layout_mode`, `layout_seed`, `background_scale` and
  `background_offset`, then `views.get` and assert **all six** came back.
  Then `views.set_positions` and `views.get` and assert `pinned`. Then
  `views.list_assets` and assert `width`, `height` and `mime`.

- [x] **The isolation sweep.** Every one of the nine tools called with a
  foreign game's token, each asserting `scope_violation` — and
  `views.run` twice, once with a saved key and once with an inline query,
  because they take different paths to the same execution.

```bash
git add internal/web/mcp_views.go internal/web/api_views.go internal/web/mcp_views_test.go \
        internal/web/api_views_test.go internal/web/mcp.go internal/web/mcp_errors.go \
        internal/web/api_metamodel.go internal/web/server.go cmd/maestro/main.go
git commit -m "feat: the views.* MCP tools, their REST mirror and the view.* events"
```

**Corrections made during implementation** (landed as `b14ada7`,
`e79e375`, `7274f32`, `8d39c12`, `c2e47f6`, `f8d09aa` and `0e7717c`;
every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code, watched fail
the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline confirmed green
and confirmed *running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, zero `SKIP`
across the repository — 1896 tests, 0 skipped, exit 0). Fourteen
findings, and the four deferred decisions this block was accumulating are
answered first.

**The four deferred decisions, decided.**

1. **`view.positions` ships.** Task 13's second round argued for it and
   left the call here; the argument holds and nothing was found against
   it. The kind is `view.positions`, per game, beside the other two. The
   payload is the view's **id and key and nothing else** — no
   coordinates, because publication order is not commit order and two
   drags in flight would otherwise leave a client rendering the earlier
   one for good, and **no version either**, which is not an oversight of
   the pair beside it: a position write deliberately does not advance a
   view's version, so a version here would be a number that did not move
   for a change that did, which is worse than absent because a subscriber
   merging on it would believe it had learned something. The gating is
   `view.upserted`'s, through the same two constants. It is published
   from `SetPositions` and from **both** arms of `ClearPositions` — the
   whole-view clear and the per-entity one — because a test that drove
   only the write would leave both clears free to go quiet, and a wiped
   arrangement is the invalidation that matters most. The whole-view
   clear publishes even when it removed nothing, and that is argued where
   it is done: the count is the *caller's* information, and a browser
   told to re-read and finding no change has lost one read where a
   browser not told has lost the picture.

2. **`Result.Positions` ships, on `RunView` only**, filled after the run
   rather than before it — a read that only ever feeds a refused answer
   is a read nothing needs. It is read outside the run's own read-only
   transaction, deliberately: a drag committing between the two is a
   picture one drag old, which is exactly what `view.positions` exists to
   tell the reader about. Nothing is compared against the nodes, so a
   position for a node this run did not draw comes back too — it is the
   arrangement the next run of a widened query will use, and dropping it
   would make an edit to a query look like a lost afternoon of map work.

   **`omitempty` collapses two things and the wire does not.** An ad-hoc
   run and a saved view nobody has dragged both marshal to no
   `positions` member from the domain struct, which is fine for a Go
   caller holding two entry points and not fine for an agent that called
   one tool either way. `runOutput` carries the distinction instead:
   `positions` is an array, empty or full, on every run of a saved key,
   and is absent on an inline query.
   `TestAnInlineRunCarriesNoPositionsMemberAndASavedOneAlwaysDoes` is red
   under both directions of the mutation.

3. **`views.set_background`'s offset is an object**, `{"x":…,"y":…}`, as
   Task 14 recorded, and `asset_id`, `scale` and `offset` are all
   pointers for three different reasons stated on the type: nil
   `asset_id` is the only spelling of "clear the background", and nil
   `scale`/`offset` mean *the defaults* rather than "leave what is
   there", because a new image must not inherit the previous one's
   arithmetic and `{0,0}` is a legal offset to ask for.

4. **The empty combinator is refused, and it was already refused.** Tasks
   8 and 9 left the identity element of `{"any":[]}` to this task, on the
   argument that a silent flip between tasks is worse than either
   reading. The settlement is the one they pointed at — refuse it — and
   it turned out `checkPredicate`'s own `children` has always done so,
   with the argument the open question wanted. What was missing was the
   record and the coverage: only `{"all":[]}` at a selector's `where` was
   pinned, which is not the position the question was about.
   `TestAnEmptyCombinatorIsRefusedAtEveryPositionThatTakesOne` drives
   both spellings at every predicate position including `edge_where`,
   where the `true` would have meant "follow every edge", with two
   controls. `combine`'s `true` stays as defence in depth for a
   hand-built `*Resolved`, and its comment now says which of the two it
   is rather than describing an open question that is closed.

**Three things the plan asked for that are not what shipped.**

5. **The plan's table names nine tools and ten ship.** `views.list_assets`
   is the tenth, it is not optional, and the plan's own read-back
   checklist asks for it ("`views.list_assets` and assert `width`,
   `height` and `mime`") while its table omits it. Without it an agent
   has no way to obtain an asset id, so `views.set_background` takes an
   argument no caller on that surface can get — the
   mechanism-nothing-can-reach this plan refuses. Task 14's finding 11
   said the same from the other side. Uploading stays REST-only and
   browser-only; discovering what has been uploaded does not.

6. **`views.list`'s stale flag needed a domain pass the plan does not
   name, and the obvious cheap version would have lied.** The flag cannot
   come from `view_refs` alone: a *field* dropped from a schema is not a
   reference, so a refs-derived flag reports a view whose `where` no
   longer resolves as healthy. `Service.StaleViews` runs the resolve pass
   over each row of the page against **one** catalogue read, which is the
   same two questions `runStored` asks. Two things were decided in
   writing it. It **does not read `view_refs`**, and the boolean is exact
   anyway: the index can only turn "this key names nothing" into "this
   key was renamed", both of which are things the game did, and it can
   neither rescue a reference with no live key nor break one whose key
   resolves — so a per-view refs read would buy a distinction a boolean
   does not carry. And **run arguments are not staleness**: a view
   declaring a parameter with no default is refused by a run that omits
   the value and nothing about the game moved, so binding parameters here
   would light up every parameterised view in a game forever.
   `TestTheStaleFlagAgreesWithWhatARunReports` compares the flag against
   what `RunView` actually reports rather than against a second list of
   expectations, over a renamed type, a dropped field and a view nothing
   touched.

7. **`views.validate` needed a domain entry point too**, and it compiles
   as well as resolving. Task 6's correction 4 said so and Task 12's
   correction 2 narrowed it, and the narrowing is not enough: two
   refusals still live in the compiler alone, a depth resolution has no
   opinion about and an `@type` operand a run *binds to a parameter*,
   which resolution cannot rewrite because it does not know the value.
   `Service.Validate` is `Run` minus the execution, and it binds the
   caller's parameters onto a copy of the resolved query the way
   `execute` does — validating against the declared *defaults* accepts a
   document `views.run` then refuses, which is the loop closing on the
   wrong answer.
   `TestValidateRefusesTheSameDocumentARunRefuses` holds the pair with a
   control that both accept the repaired document.

**Seven findings from writing it.**

8. **`views.TimeoutError`'s wrapped error is exported, and the reason is
   a test rather than a use.** The type's defining property is that it
   unwraps to something `metamodel.IsRetryable` admits — which is
   precisely what makes the arm ordering load-bearing, since an arm below
   the retryable one would catch nothing. A surface that cannot build the
   real shape can only assert the ordering with a value satisfying one
   arm, which passes under either order. Measured: a hand-built
   `TimeoutError` wrapping nothing is green with the arms swapped.
   `TestATimedOutViewKeepsItsAdviceOnBothSurfaces` builds one wrapping a
   57014 `pgconn.PgError`, asserts `metamodel.IsRetryable` admits it as
   its own control, and requires the elapsed budget and all three bound
   names to reach both surfaces identically.

9. **The convention test that claims to see every tool could not see ten
   of them.** `TestEveryMCPToolGoesThroughAddScopedTool` builds its
   server "with a metamodel service, because that is the build with the
   most tools on it", and a comment beside it says the docs eleven must
   be inside the comparison for exactly this reason — and the sentence
   went false in silence the moment a fourth domain existed. The
   `views.*` ten were outside the comparison entirely, so one of them
   registered with `mcp.AddTool` directly would have been invisible to
   the one test that exists to catch that, while every sentence in the
   file claimed otherwise. **The standing pattern, arriving in the guard
   itself.** Fixed by adding the views service to that literal, with the
   instruction that every new domain belongs there in the commit that
   adds it; red under a `views.bypass` tool registered directly.

10. **`views.run` refuses a call naming both a key and a query.** A
    precedence rule would draw a picture the caller did not ask for and
    say nothing, on a read whose whole value is that it answers the
    question posed. Naming neither is refused too. Red under the
    key-wins mutation, with both single-argument controls in the test.

11. **Two mutations survived the first pass, both the standing
    "correct and unasserted" shape, and both are now red.** The stale
    flag was hard-wired to `false` and the whole suite stayed green,
    because the only listing test asserted `stale == false` on a healthy
    view — a control whose value is the same either way;
    `TestTheListingFlagsAStaleViewAndNotAHealthyOne` now holds a stale
    view and a healthy one in one listing. And `renderer_params` handed
    back as a nil map stayed green, because every fixture saved a view
    *with* parameters and unmarshalling allocates the map on the way in;
    `TestAViewWithNoRendererParametersReadsBackAsAnObjectAndNotNull`
    saves one with none and asserts `"renderer_params":{}` on the wire.
    That second mutation is still green against the initialiser itself,
    which is recorded where it is written: the domain stores `{}` rather
    than a JSON null, so the nil is reachable only from a row written
    outside `internal/views`, and the test asserts the *outcome* so it
    holds whichever of the two keeps the promise.

12. **`Service.StaleViews` asks the renderer half of the question and
    nothing observes it, and it stays.** Mutating the
    `rendererStaleness` call away leaves the package green, which is Task
    12's finding 2 one step along: `contain_via` is the only type-naming
    parameter and `nested` structurally requires the query to draw edges
    of that same relation, while every field-naming parameter must name a
    key `project.fields` carries — so a renderer parameter the game moved
    under is today always a query the game moved under as well. It is
    kept because dropping it would make the flag correct by a coincidence
    of the current catalogue, and because this function's own claim — the
    flag and the run agree — holds by construction only if the two ask
    the same two questions. Written where the call is.

13. **The operator table needed a generator, and the tool descriptions
    needed a test that reads what an agent reads.** `predicate.go` had
    said since Task 2 that "the tool description is generated from it
    (Task 15)", and generating the renderer half while restating the
    operator half would have been the exact drift Task 10 built its own
    guard against. `views.OperatorDescription()` prints the table and the
    built-in vocabulary from the same two structures the compiler
    enforces against, in a fixed order (`fieldTypeOrder`, because Go
    randomises map iteration and a description that differs between
    process starts is one a client cannot diff), and
    `TestTheOperatorDescriptionIsGeneratedFromTheTable` parses it back
    and compares in both directions. Beside it,
    `TestTheViewsToolDescriptionsAreGeneratedRatherThanRestated` reads
    the descriptions **as registered** — a new `ToolDescriptionsForTest`
    export, because asserting that a generating function exists says
    nothing about whether its output reached the wire — and requires the
    renderer catalogue and the operator table verbatim, once each. That
    test caught `views.run` shipping without the operator table at all,
    which is the tool an agent writes an inline query through.

14. **Task 14's "name nothing only this repository knows" is now swept on
    this surface too.** That rule was established for the generated
    renderer text and the tools' own prose is written one layer up, where
    the same mistake is equally available; the sweep runs over every
    `views.*` description for task numbers, spec sections, numbered
    findings and source filenames, with a vacuity guard. Nothing was
    found, which is the point: the rule now holds for the text that is
    written by hand rather than only for the text that is generated.

    Beside it the same test pins, as facts rather than as prose, the
    eleven sentences this block's inheritance demanded reach the wire —
    the `@type` split on both tools that take a query, what
    `truncated.depth` measures and that a one-hop step never sets it, an
    edge's endpoints not being guaranteed in `nodes`, `attrs` keyed by
    the projection slot, the parameter type limitation, the honest cost
    of a comparison, the untyped step switching off the projection's typo
    detection, and the permanence of a key on the two tools that take one.

**Two things decided here that the plan did not name.**

- **`views.run` is a POST on REST and is therefore gated as a write,
  which is a real cost and is written down rather than dodged.** A query
  document does not fit in a query string, so a POST is the only honest
  spelling; `registerContentRoute` decides the gate from the method, so a
  viewer can run a view over MCP (a token is editor-equivalent by
  construction) and cannot over REST. The alternatives are worse:
  registering it through `registerProjectRoute` to dodge the editor gate
  would put a game-content route outside the one convention test that
  watches this surface, and a GET carrying a document in its URL would
  meet a server's header bounds on the first interesting query. Recorded
  in `api_views.go`'s header as a decision for the change that builds the
  browser client, and `TestAViewerRunsAViewAndCannotSaveOne` asserts the
  half a convention test cannot: that a viewer still *reads*.

- **The run envelope's node shape is deliberately not in the output
  schema.** A node's `attrs` is keyed by the projection slots the
  document declared and its `fields` by the game's own field keys, so the
  shape of one node is a property of the query that drew it — a schema
  pinning it would be wrong for every document but one. What is pinned is
  what every run shares: `stats`, `truncated`, and the present/absent
  distinction on `positions`.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each (`-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported): the `SetPositions` publish
dropped (1); the same publish moved above its error check (1, the
deferred-constraint test); the whole-view clear's publish dropped (1);
the per-entity clear's publish dropped (1); `RunView` discarding the
positions it read (1); `positionsOf` reading every view's positions (1);
the `*views.TimeoutError` arm moved below the retryable one, on both
surfaces (2); `fieldDetails` blind to `*views.QueryError` (2);
`query_stale` answered without its diagnostics (1);
`renderer_requirements` answered as `query_invalid` (2, both the
convention test and the twin); `query_stale` given `statusForCode`'s 422
default (3); `Validate` stopping after resolution (2); `Validate`
compiling against the declared defaults rather than the call's bindings
(1); `Validate` never checking the renderer (1); `Validate` answering an
empty result (1); the stale flag ignoring the resolution problems (2);
the stale flag reporting unbound run parameters (1); a field type dropped
from the printed operator order (2); an operator row printed short (1); a
built-in printed with the wrong type (1); the empty-combinator refusal
removed (1); `views.run` preferring the key when both were sent (1); the
positions member sent for an inline run (1); the listing never asking
whether a view is stale (1); `renderer_params` handed back as a nil map
(1); `layout_seed` read back as the column default (1); the background
offset never decoded (1); the clear building a slice for an absent
pointer (3); the asset URL built for the wrong game (1); a views tool
registered with `mcp.AddTool` directly (1). Thirty-one mutations,
forty-one reds — plus **two confirmed green and recorded as such**: the
`renderer_params` initialiser (finding 11) and `StaleViews`' renderer
half (finding 12).

`make check` — `gofmt`, `go vet`, `golangci-lint` (0 issues), `sqlc diff`
and `go test -race ./...` — exits 0.

**Second review round** (landed as `a7a06e7`, `18afbaa`, `68cadf3`,
`d72ac73`, `15c6959` and `c5050a0`). The deliverable passed: every tool
description was read end to end as an agent would read it and no false
sentence was found, the generated halves appear exactly once each, all
eleven routes were driven from a foreign game and refused, and a
one-millisecond budget through the saved path proved the timeout type
survives unwrapped. Six findings, all closed, plus two of this round's
own. Every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code, watched
fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline confirmed
green and confirmed *running* (`TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported, `go test
-v ./... -count=1` exit 0 — **1913 tests, 1913 passed, 0 skipped** — both
before the first change and after the last).

15. **The rule correction 1 established did not reach the background
    setter, and now does.** `SetBackground` published nothing, inside the
    very correction that argued a browser holding a picture has no other
    way to learn the picture changed — while `views.sql`'s own header
    states the equivalence outright ("a dragged node is the same kind of
    act as a placed background") and a background is *more* of the
    picture than a drag is: it is the map renderer's ground. Measured
    before the fix: after `set_background`, no event; after
    `set_positions`, `view.positions`. **A fourth kind ships**,
    `view.background`, gated through the same two constants, payload the
    view's id and key and **no version** — the argument for the position
    payload applies verbatim, since this statement deliberately advances
    no version, so a version here would be a number that did not move for
    a change that did. Both arms publish, placement and clear, because
    removing the ground changes the picture as much as placing one.
    `viewPositionsEvent` became `viewPlacementEvent`: one payload for two
    kinds, because both are the same claim — this view's placement moved,
    re-read it. `TestABackgroundWritePublishesItsInvalidation` and its
    refusal control in the domain, and the fourth and fifth steps of
    `TestAViewEventReachesAViewerAndATokenAlike` over the endpoint a
    browser subscribes to. Red under the publish dropped, on both.

16. **The guard written to keep plan references off the wire was a
    substring list and leaked seven of them.** `"Task 1"` caught 1 and
    10–19 by accident and `"Task 6"` caught 6; tasks 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8 and
    9 leaked, and every section but §5. Reproduced exactly as the review
    did — prefixing `views.list`'s description with `"See Task 9 and
    spec §3.2. "` and running the guard:

    ```
    --- PASS: TestTheViewsToolDescriptionsAreGeneratedRatherThanRestated (0.24s)
    ```

    Task 14's round found this exact leak in production text
    (`"(Task 13's positions)"` served to agents); the guard written to
    stop it recurring would not have caught it. **A guard against a kind
    of mistake has to describe the kind**, so it is now seven patterns —
    a task followed by any number, a section sign followed by a digit, a
    numbered finding, a source filename, the sub-project word, an
    open-question reference, and a bare "the plan"/"the spec" — each
    checked against the leak it is written for, so a regexp that matches
    nothing cannot sit there looking like coverage, which is the failure
    mode of the list it replaces one level up. The vacuity guard stays.
    Same mutation after the fix:

    ```
    mcp_views_test.go:680: views.list's description names "Task 9" — a task number
    mcp_views_test.go:680: views.list's description names "§3" — a spec section
    --- FAIL: TestTheViewsToolDescriptionsAreGeneratedRatherThanRestated (0.22s)
    ```

    The shipped descriptions are clean under the wider patterns, which is
    the point: nothing had to be rewritten, only asserted.

17. **Six documented arguments were silently droppable.** All six
    dropped at once left the whole `internal/web` suite green
    (`ok github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/web 47.081s`), with a live
    control — dropping the description from the upsert *did* fail a test,
    so the harness was working. Each is a promise a description makes,
    and this layer is the only place it can be broken: the domain tests
    every one of these behaviours, and what nothing asserted is that the
    argument an agent sends *arrives*.

    | dropped | site | now red under |
    |---|---|---|
    | the stale policy from the run's request | `mcp_views.go:580` | `TestARunCarriesItsStalePolicyAndReportsWhatItPruned` |
    | the stale member from the run's output | `mcp_views.go:455-457` | the same test |
    | the include-fields flag | `mcp_views.go:580` | `TestARunCarriesItsIncludeFieldsFlag` |
    | the renderer from validate | `mcp_views.go:625` | `TestValidateJudgesTheRendererAndTheParametersTheCallSent` |
    | the parameters from validate | `mcp_views.go:625` | the same test |
    | the actor from the upsert | `mcp_views.go:536` | `TestAnUpsertRecordsTheCallerWhoMadeIt` |

    Validate's own text says naming a renderer "is the only way a
    renderer parameter can be judged" — the tool's reason to exist — and
    nothing on the wire asserted the argument arrived; correction 7
    proved `Validate` binds the *call's* parameters in the domain, and

    flips on the parameters with the renderer held fixed, which is what
    makes it a test of the binding rather than of the catalogue, and the

18. **The REST background route was never driven for success, and the
    test whose comment claims to drive it did not.**
    `TestTheViewRoutesAreTheSameContractAsTheTools`' doc comment has
    always said the walk covers "save, read back, list, run, validate,

    deleting the route's own key extraction left the suite green with

    one thing this route can be uniquely wrong about — the tool takes the
    key in its body, this route takes it from the path — so the walk now

19. **The listing's renderer filter and cursor are now driven on both
    surfaces, and the stated-project check is no longer decorative.**
    Reducing the listing input to its limit alone left the suite green on

    renderer or a cursor — the domain covers the pair well, so this was a
    transport gap, but "a cursor belongs to the game and the filter it
    was issued for" is a wire claim and had no wire test. Both surfaces
    now filter, page and replay a cursor against a different filter and
    are refused, each with the unfiltered listing as its control.
    Removing the stated-project check from the run route was also green:
    it is not a security hole, since the scope always wins, but the
    refusal the field exists to make — a client that has lost track of
    which game it is editing being told so rather than told its write

    drives all six, each with the same body minus the field as its

20. **`views.validate` carries the renderer catalogue.** It was the only
    query tool without one, and it is the tool that exists to iterate
    renderer parameters: the upsert carried the catalogue, run and
    validate carried the operator table, and validate — whose own text
    says naming a renderer is the only way a parameter can be judged —
    named no renderer and no parameter. The generation test required the

**Two more from this round's own mutation sweep**, over the three cores
the review did not reach. `views.get` ignoring the caller's key and
`views.remove` never calling the domain are both red already, so those
two are covered; the other three mutations were not.

21. **`views.list_assets`' cursor and limit were droppable.** The

### Task 16: End to end — the spec's definition of done

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/web/views_e2e_test.go`

The spec's §9, executed as one test against a running server, over MCP,
exactly as an agent would:

1. Seed a game: `class`, `quest`, `zone`; `available_to`, `requires`,
   `takes_place_in`; nine quests across two zones and three level bands.
2. Call `views.validate` with a query that names `avilable_to`. Assert
   `query_invalid` at `/traverse/0/via/0` naming the key back.
3. Call `views.validate` again with `min_level` compared by `contains`.
   Assert `query_invalid` at `/traverse/0/where/op` naming the declared
   type and the operators it does answer. **Two validations, the loop the
   tool exists for.**
4. Call `views.upsert` once, with the spec's §3.1 query, renderer
   `graph`, `color_by` the zone one hop away. Assert version 1.
5. Call `views.run` and assert the quests between level 20 and 30 come
   back, each carrying its zone name in `attrs.color_by`, and that a
   quest at level 12 does not.
6. Call `views.set_positions` for four of them, `views.run` again, and
   assert the four coordinates come back **and** that the other nodes came
   back without positions rather than at the origin.
7. Add twelve more quests through `entities.bulk_upsert`, run again, and
   assert the four positions are unchanged and the twelve new nodes
   arrived unpinned. This is the spec's "the positions survive the agent
   adding twelve more quests".
8. Rename `available_to` to `usable_by` through `relation_types.upsert`.
   Run again and assert `query_stale` by default, with a
   `relation_type_renamed` diagnostic **at `/traverse/0/via/0`** — the
   pointer is the point: the view reports which step broke instead of
   quietly drawing something false.
9. Run with `on_stale: "best_effort"` and assert a non-empty result plus
   the same diagnostic.
10. `views.upsert` the repaired query with `expected_version: 1`, assert
    version 2, and run clean.
11. Delete the `zone` entity type (after deleting its entities) and assert
    the deletion **succeeds** and its response lists this view among the
    ones it broke, with the pointer into the projection.
12. The isolation sweep: every tool with a second game's token.
13. Record `stats.duration_ms` for step 5 in the commit message. That
    number is the evidence this plan's open question O2 (indexed jsonb
    fields) is to be re-argued with, and recording it is the difference
    between deciding that with data and deciding it again by argument.

- [x] **Then run the product over its real transport**, which is this
  step done as something that can be re-run. The instruction was `make
  dev` and a browser; what ships is
  `TestASavedViewArrivesOverTheRealTransport` and
  `TestEveryViewsToolIsCallableOverTheRealTransport` — a real MCP client
  over HTTP with a real token, the envelope read out of
  `StructuredContent`, and every one of the ten tools driven so that the
  SDK validates both of its schemas. A session nobody can repeat would
  have found the two defects once; these find them again. It is exactly
  the step that found them, and no other test could have.

```bash
git add internal/web/views_e2e_test.go internal/web/mcp_metamodel.go \
        internal/web/mcp_views.go internal/views/service.go internal/views/stale_test.go
git commit -m "test: the views definition of done, end to end over MCP"
git commit -m "fix: the query document and the run envelope match the schemas the transport enforces"
```

**Corrections made during implementation** (landed as `6231676` and
`c3b6a41`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code,
watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline
confirmed green and confirmed *running*, `-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL`
exported, zero `SKIP` across the repository). Eight findings. Two of them
are defects that made three tools **uncallable over the real transport**
while every test in the repository was green, and they were found the way
this task's own checklist says such things are found.

**Step 8 as written cannot be performed, and this is what was done
instead.** "Rename `available_to` to `usable_by` through
`relation_types.upsert`" describes a call the product does not have:
`EntityTypeInput` and `RelationTypeInput` carry no id, both upserts are
addressed **by key** and idempotent by it, and there is no
`UPDATE … SET key` anywhere in `internal/db/queries` — so writing a
different key creates a *second* type and leaves the first standing.
Task 12 recorded this and reached the renamed state by editing the row;
the same is done here, and **the walk says so in the test rather than in
a helper**, because the honesty of the walk is what is being tested. Two
things go with that decision:

- **The deletion branch is driven as well, and it is the one an agent
  can really cause.** Step 10 deletes the `zone` entity type through
  `types.remove` after deleting its entities, and step 11 runs the view
  under both spellings of `on_stale`. So the walk covers what an agent
  can do today *and* the state a rename operation will produce when it is
  built — a different diagnostic (`entity_type_missing`) at a different
  position (`/project/color_by/related/type`) from the rename's
  (`relation_type_renamed` at `/traverse/0/via/0`), and the pointer is
  asserted in both.
- **The plan's step 8 is also wrong about what a rename answers, and the
  test asserts the shipped behaviour rather than the plan's.** It asks
  for `query_stale` by default. A renamed type still resolves *by id*, so
  the picture is correct and the run succeeds carrying the diagnostic;
  the spec says so in as many words ("it runs correctly in the meantime —
  the id resolved"), and Task 12's finding 8 deleted a code-based
  `fatal()` predicate for exactly this reason. `query_stale` is for a
  reference that no longer resolves at all, which is what step 11's
  deletion produces. Asserting the plan's version would have meant
  changing the product to refuse a right answer. The walk asserts, in
  order: the diagnostic with its `was`/`now` pair at the step's own
  pointer, that the picture is the same one it drew before the rename
  node for node, that the **stored document still spells the old key**,
  and that the view's version did not move.

1. **Three tools could not be called over the real transport at all,
   and nothing in the repository could have noticed.** `views.upsert`,
   `views.validate` and `views.run` take their query document as a
   `json.RawMessage`, and the SDK infers a tool's input schema from the
   Go type: a `[]byte` infers as `{"type": ["null", "array"]}`. Every
   call sending the document as the object it is was refused before any
   handler ran —

   ```
   validating "arguments": validating root: validating /properties/query:
   type: map[edges:… v:1] has type "object", want one of "null, array"
   ```

   — so the three tools an agent composes a view with were unusable from
   a real client, while the whole package was green. It is green because
   every test in it calls the `MCP*` functions directly, which is where
   the isolation invariant is pinned and where **nothing crosses a
   schema**. `queryDocumentInput` now infers each of the three schemas
   from the Go type with one substitution at the type that has the
   problem, rather than hand-writing three schemas a new member would
   silently fall out of.

2. **`views.run`'s declared output schema required two members the
   envelope has never carried.** It named `node_count` and `edge_count`
   as required and omitted `nodes` and `edges`, which are what
   `views.Stats` marshals. The SDK validates the handler's answer against
   that schema before it reaches the wire, so **every run over the real
   transport failed**:

   ```
   validating tool output: validating root: validating /properties/stats:
   required: missing properties: ["node_count" "edge_count"]
   ```

   The two strings appear nowhere else in the module, which is the whole
   story: a schema nothing reads is a mechanism nothing reads, and it
   said something false for a whole task.

3. **The guard is a wire sweep, because one wire test would only have
   covered one tool.** `TestEveryViewsToolIsCallableOverTheRealTransport`
   drives all ten through the mounted MCP endpoint with arguments meant
   to succeed, requires no `IsError` and requires `StructuredContent` on
   each — so both schemas of every tool are exercised by the SDK itself.
   It is registry-driven with the same completeness check Task 15's
   isolation sweep uses: a tool added without a call here fails rather
   than going unexercised. Both defects above are red in it, singly.
   `TestASavedViewArrivesOverTheRealTransport` stands beside it as the
   readable half — the spec's own picture, over HTTP, with the colours
   read out of `StructuredContent` — and is the plan's "run the product
   by hand" step, done as a repeatable test rather than as a session
   nobody can re-run.

4. **Step 11 asks for something no surface could answer, so the surface
   was built.** "Assert the deletion succeeds and its response lists this
   view among the ones it broke" is the spec's §6.5, and
   `views.RemoveTypeReportingViews` has existed since Task 12 with **no
   caller anywhere in `internal/web`**: `types.remove` and
   `relation_types.remove` called `deps.Metamodel.Remove*` directly and
   answered a bare `{"removed": true}`. A promise in the spec, a
   mechanism in the domain, and nothing joining them — this plan's own
   "a mechanism nothing reads is a lie", one layer up. Both tools now go
   through `removeTypeReportingViews` and answer `TypeRemovedOutput`,
   which carries `broke_views`: one row **per reference**, with the view
   key, the type key as the document spells it, and the JSON pointer.
   The REST mirror inherits it, since both handlers write the same value.
   The `deps.Views == nil` branch is a build with no views service, where
   a removal can break no view.

5. **Wiring that report dropped the metamodel's own removal events, and
   the fix is in `views.New`.** `RemoveTypeReportingViews` removes the
   type through *this* package's metamodel handle, which `New` built with
   a **nil hub** on the argument that this package calls only read
   accessors on it. That argument was true when it was written and
   stopped being true the moment Task 12 composed a write there — and
   once the transport goes through this path, it is the path *every* type
   deletion in the product takes, so `type.removed` and
   `relation_type.removed` would have stopped being published at all. A
   designer watching a game would never learn a type had gone. The handle
   takes the same hub now; the event is the metamodel's own, published by
   the metamodel's own code for a change it made, and what a nil hub
   bought was silence rather than a boundary.
   `TestRemovingATypeThroughTheViewsReportStillAnnouncesIt` drives both
   kinds and is red for both with the nil restored.

6. **The fixture is shaped so that every step has a negative half, and
   two of the negatives are the ones a smaller fixture cannot have.**
   Nine quests over three level bands and two zones, plus two classes:
   - `charge-of-the-mounted` is level 26 — inside the band — and
     available to the **warrior**. A fixture whose every quest belongs to
     the seeded class cannot tell a working traversal from a step that
     ignored `available_to` and filtered on level alone.
   - `jangolode-mine requires kobold-candles`, a prerequisite pointing at
     a level-15 quest **outside** the band, is the control for the second
     `edges[]` entry — which is the entry the spec's own example exists
     to demonstrate. Mutating its `between` arm from `AND` to `OR` draws
     that edge, and the walk names it.
   - Two zones rather than one, so `attrs.color_by` is not a constant,
     and the seed class node carries **no** `color_by` at all rather than
     an empty one.
   - Four dragged nodes and a fifth undragged one, so "the positions came
     back" cannot pass by returning every node placed, and the seed class
     comes back **without** a position rather than at the origin.

7. **Step 12's isolation sweep is not repeated here, and what is asserted
   instead is what the existing sweep cannot say.** Task 15's
   `TestEveryViewsToolRefusesAnotherGamesToken` enumerates the registry
   and fails when a tool it does not drive is added, which is a stronger
   guarantee than a hand-written list in this file would be, and copying
   it would leave two lists to keep in step. What it cannot assert is
   that a *real arrangement* survives the attempt, so this walk drives
   the six calls that would change something with the other game's token
   and then re-reads its own picture: the four coordinates unmoved, the
   view still at the version the repair left.

8. **Two tool names in the plan do not exist, recorded because an agent
   reading the plan would look for them.** Step 7 says
   `entities.bulk_upsert`; the tool is `entities.upsert`, which takes a
   batch — "one entity is a batch of one, which is why there is no
   separate single-row tool to keep in step with this one". And step 12's
   "every tool" is ten and not the nine the task's own table lists, which
   Task 15's finding 5 already recorded.

**Step 13, the number the plan asks to be recorded.** Step 5's run over
the seeded game — a selector, one one-hop traversal with a two-leaf
numeric band, two `edges[]` entries and a one-hop related colour, over
nine quests, two zones and two classes — reports
**`stats.duration_ms` between 4 and 10 ms** across runs (5 nodes, 5
edges; 10, 7, 5, 8, 4 ms observed). That is the evidence open question O2
(indexed jsonb fields) is to be re-argued with, and what it says today is
that the guarded cast over `fields` is nowhere near being the cost of a
picture at this size: a game two orders of magnitude larger is what would
change the answer, and this number is the baseline to compare it to.

**Mutations proved red in this round**, baseline confirmed running before
each (`-v`, `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported): the `query` member's inferred
input schema restored on `views.upsert` (1, and the two other tools with
it — the wire sweep names each); `views.run`'s stats schema restored to
`node_count`/`edge_count` (2, both wire tests, with the SDK's own
validation message as the evidence); `removeTypeReportingViews` reporting
an empty list (1, "the deletion reported []"); `views.New` giving its
metamodel handle a nil hub again (2, both kinds); the transport dropping
the caller's `pinned` flag (2 — this walk and Task 15's own read-back);
a traverse step's `where` compiled as nothing (1, with all four
out-of-band quests named); a `between` arm's `AND` changed to `OR` (2 —
this walk names the edge that leaves the band, and `internal/views`'
golden files see it too, which is a reader and is recorded as such).
Nine mutations, twelve reds.

**The suite, at the end of the sub-project.** `go test ./... -count=1 -v`
with `TEST_DATABASE_URL` exported: **1929 tests, 1929 passed, 0 skipped,
0 failed, exit 0**. The three packages this sub-project built or grew
hold 949 of them — `internal/views` 387, `internal/web` 541 (the whole
surface, not only the views half) and `internal/graph` 21. `make check` —
`gofmt`, `go vet`, `golangci-lint` (0 issues), `sqlc diff` and
`go test -race ./...` — exits 0.

## Self-review notes

Checked against `2026-09-02-views-and-query-language-design.md`, section
by section:

- **§1 What this spec decides** — D2 implemented, D1 and D3 absent: no
  built-in view anywhere (the catalogue in Task 10 is renderers, not
  views), and no agent-authored renderer code (`renderer` is a name from a
  closed set, checked at upsert). The spec's own test — *"the quests a
  Mage can reach between level 20 and 30, coloured by zone"* as one saved
  view an agent composes in one call — is Task 16, steps 4 and 5.
- **§2 The query language** — the JSON document and its `v` (Task 2), the
  five stages `from`/`traverse`/`nodes`/`edges`/`project` (Tasks 2, 6, 8,
  9), `limits` (Tasks 3 and 7), `params` (Tasks 2 and 4), predicates and
  the exact operator table including `list<text>` as the whole list
  family (Task 3), attribute references with the one-hop `related` and
  its `ambiguous` flag (Task 9), and the single envelope (Task 6).
- **§3 Worked examples** — all three compile in Task 6's golden test, and
  §3.1 is Task 16 end to end. §3.3's `edge_where` on a soft-reference
  `text` field is Task 4 (resolution against the *relation type's*
  schema) and Task 8 (the edge predicate inside the walk).
- **§4 Compiling to SQL** — §4.1's resolve-before-compile is Task 4 and
  is the reason Task 6 can assert that no caller value reaches the
  statement text; §4.2's three load-bearing lines are Task 5's three reds;
  the `jsonb_typeof` guard and the `invalid` exclusion are Task 6; §4.3's
  bounds table is Tasks 3 and 7; §4.4's honest reading of the indexes is
  the migration comment in Task 1 plus this plan's open question O2.
- **§5 Renderers, layout and storage** — the catalogue and its
  requirements are Task 10; §5.2's three layout modes are stored and
  returned as a client contract (Task 13, and open question O1 says why
  the server computes nothing); §5.3's positions with both failure modes
  are Task 13; §5.4's four tables **with the composite keys the section
  itself says the DDL lacks** are Task 1; §5.5's nine tools, response-size
  discipline, isolation and error codes are Task 15 — with
  `query_timeout` deliberately not shipped and argued.
- **§6 Validation and staleness** — the id-then-key resolution order,
  both strictnesses, the eight diagnostic codes, the rename that does not
  rewrite, and the type deletion that is allowed and lists what it broke:
  Task 12, exercised end to end in Task 16 steps 8–11.
- **§7 Open questions** — O1, O2, O3, O4, O5, O7, O8 and O9 are all
  settled in "Open questions settled", each with an argument; O6 was
  already closed by the spec.
- **§8 What the existing schema needs** — the four tables and the
  `relations (relation_type_id, target_id)` index are Task 1, and the
  index carries the `EXPLAIN` evidence the metamodel plan's correction 6
  demanded before adding it. The two recorded frictions (relations have
  no key and no version; `entities_fields_idx` serves containment only)
  are named in "What in the shipped code makes this harder" and shape
  Tasks 1, 6 and 13.
- **§9 Definition of done** — Task 16, step by step, plus the hand check.

Deliberately **not** here: what any of it looks like (sub-project 5 and
`/impeccable`), cycle detection and unreachable-content reports (the
analysis sub-project), the skill bundle that teaches an agent when to
compose a view rather than list entities, and aggregation.

Three things this plan changes **outside** the views domain, named so a
reviewer looks at them first:

1. **`internal/graph` is new** (Task 5) and has one caller today. Its
   package comment says so rather than claiming the second.
2. **`internal/graph`'s API grows in Task 8** (`EdgePredicate`), which is
   a change to a package Task 5 shipped; the commit touches both.
3. **`internal/web` gains four wire codes** (Task 15) on both surfaces in
   one commit, with a convention test that iterates the sentinel list.

---

## What this sub-project learned

Sixteen tasks, roughly forty review rounds between them, and something
over two hundred and fifty mutations applied to shipped code and watched
go red. This section is for whoever writes sub-project 5. It is not a
summary of what was built; it is what kept going wrong, what actually
caught it, and what is worth copying.

### The defect that recurred, and where it hides

**A rule established correctly and not carried one step along.** It was
named after the metamodel sub-project and it arrived here at least
twenty times more. The instances are not variations on a theme — they are
literally the same shape:

- `walkStrings` bounded every exported field and missed a field promoted
  from an *embedded* type (Task 2).
- The frag guard watched one file, then watched function bodies and not
  package-level declarations, then could not see a parenthesised
  conversion or a local alias (Task 6).
- The statement guard closed the call site and left the assignment one
  line above it open, and watched three pgx entry points out of six
  (Task 7).
- The project-filter guard matched an alias as a *substring* of another
  alias, then matched `$1` as a *prefix* of `$18`, then could not see
  three shapes of table reference (Tasks 8 and 9).
- `include_fields` was enforced for a table's columns and for none of
  the five other parameters that name a field key (Task 10).
- `Renderer.ReadsBackground` was consulted by the setter and not by the
  upsert, which writes the same row (Task 14).
- The shared actor-column list was extended rather than copied — and
  still stopped one prefix short, three separate times (Tasks 11, 14).
- `TestEveryMCPToolGoesThroughAddScopedTool` built its server from "the
  build with the most tools on it" and went silently false the moment a
  fourth domain existed (Task 15).
- And in this task: `views.New` gave its metamodel handle a nil hub on an
  argument that was true when written and stopped being true when Task 12
  composed a write through it.

**The pattern within the pattern is that it lands inside the correction
that establishes the rule.** Task 10's review found six of them in one
round; Task 14's finding 12 is the seventeenth instance and it is inside
the fix for the sixteenth. The practical instruction is narrow and it
works: when you fix an instance, do not patch the hole — **read the rest
of the mechanism**, and grep for the other call sites of the rule you
just stated. Task 9's review is the one round that did this deliberately
("the rest of that regexp was looked at, which is the step skipped in the
three previous rounds"), and it found two more properties nobody had
written down.

### Tests that pass for the wrong reason

The second recurring defect, and the more dangerous one, because a green
suite is the evidence everything else rests on.

- **A fixture too small to distinguish two policies.** Task 13's
  cross-game overwrite: the test written to catch it asserted
  `len(positions) == 2`, and the count is two whichever game wrote the
  coordinates. Task 11's `ListViewsBrokenByType`: the isolation test
  seeded a second game and then discriminated on the *type ids*
  differing, which passes either way. Task 15: the only listing test
  asserted `stale == false` on a healthy view — a control whose value is
  the same under every implementation.
- **Two guards masking each other.** `TestARunFromAnotherGameSeesNothing`
  cannot fail on a lost project filter, because the type-id filter beside
  it isolates the same rows. `ListViewsBrokenByType`'s two project
  filters each imply the other, so either alone is invisible and both
  together are a real cross-game leak. The `nosniff` header on the asset
  route is redundant with the global middleware, and each masks the
  other.
- **An ordering that held by luck.** Task 6's `ORDER BY` survived three
  runs out of three on a `UNION` that happened to come back in arm order.
  Task 10's refusal-order guard "worked" only because its map literal
  happened to begin with the alphabetically-last key — and finding 22's
  measurement (Go rotates a small map's iteration, it does not permute
  it) showed the *reasoning* for that guard had been wrong the whole
  time.
- **Correct and unasserted.** Whole classes of behaviour were right and
  pinned by nothing: `renderer_params` written but never read back,
  `pinned` stored but scanned as a default, six documented tool arguments
  that could all be dropped at once with the suite green, `views.remove`'s
  entire answer being one boolean nothing checked.

The discipline that finds all four: **for every assertion, ask what would
have to break for it to fail** — and then break it. Not "does this test
cover the feature" but "name the mutation this test is red under". If you
cannot name one, the test is decoration.

### What actually caught things

Ranked by what they found here, not by how they look:

1. **Mutation, applied to shipped code and watched.** Nothing else came
   close. Roughly one mutation in six survived the first time it was
   tried, and each survivor was a real hole. Reasoning about coverage
   found none of them.
2. **Running the product over its real transport.** This task found two
   defects that made three tools *uncallable* by any real client — an
   inferred input schema describing a query document as an array of
   bytes, and an output schema requiring two members the envelope never
   carried — with the entire repository green, because every test called
   the Go functions directly and nothing crossed a schema. Task 14's
   round found a plan reference served in production text the same way.
   **Whatever layer your tests stop at, something below it is wrong.**
3. **Measuring instead of arguing.** `SET LOCAL default_transaction_read_only`
   does not make its own transaction read-only; `rows.Err()` after a scan
   that never iterated returns nil; `set_config` takes a bind parameter;
   a `LIMIT 2` lateral duplicates the node row; the depth probe costs a
   factor of 1.97 on a clique and nothing on a chain; a byte cap makes a
   Spanish name half the length of an English one. Every one of those was
   believed, written down, and false.
4. **Text assertions on emitted SQL.** Several invariants have no
   behavioural signature at all in today's build — every project filter
   the compiler emits is redundant against the type-id filters beside it
   — so the guards that read the statement are the only defence for the
   shapes a later task adds. Golden files are a reader too, and that was
   worth recording twice (Tasks 8 and 9) rather than dismissing them.
5. **Bidirectional guards over data tables.** Every renderer parameter
   has a checker and every checker has a parameter; every diagnostic code
   has a sentence and no two share one; every declared kind has a phrase
   and no phrase lacks a kind; every composite key into `api_tokens` is
   named in the actor list — that last one asks `pg_constraint` rather
   than comparing against a second list, which is why it will still be
   right after the next migration.

### What a later sub-project should copy

- **Generate agent-facing text from the structure it describes, and parse
  it back.** The renderer catalogue, the operator table and the tool
  descriptions are generated and asserted in both directions. Restating
  either in prose is drift with a date on it, and Task 15 caught
  `views.run` shipping without the operator table at all this way.
- **Sweep the wire.** One test per surface that drives *every* registered
  tool through the real transport, enumerated from the registry so a new
  tool cannot skip it. Two of this sub-project's worst defects lived
  exactly in the gap it closes.
- **Write down what a mechanism does *not* do, in the code.** "No server
  code reads `layout_mode`", "an edge's endpoints are not guaranteed to
  be in `nodes`", "this filter is redundant today and defends the shapes
  a later task adds", "these two guards mask each other". A comment that
  claims a guarantee the code does not provide is the first defect in its
  purest form; the inverse — a silence around a real limit — is the same
  defect with the sign flipped, and only a test tells them apart.
- **Refuse rather than answer emptily.** The most expensive failure this
  language has is a picture that is really a typo. `@type` refuses a key
  the game does not declare; a projection key nothing declares is
  refused; an axis a type in scope does not declare is refused. Where a
  refusal was not possible — the pattern operators on `@type`, an untyped
  step switching off the projection's typo check — the tool description
  says so, because an agent cannot infer one rule from the other half.
- **Every plan's prescribed mutations should be treated as hypotheses.**
  Seven of them in this plan did not reach the path they named, and each
  time the equivalent mutation had to be found by reading the branch the
  scenario actually takes. Two of them were wrong in a way that mattered:
  the plan's own step 8 asked for a refusal the product deliberately does
  not make, and the plan's read-only mutation described two guards where
  the code had one.
- **Keep `git status` and a full green run between the last mutation and
  the next commit.** Task 12 shipped a deleted diagnostic to a commit
  because one scratch-copy restore did not take. It cost a commit and it
  is the cheapest rule here.

### One thing left open

There is still **no rename operation**. Both type upserts are addressed
by key, so the state every `*_renamed` diagnostic exists for can only be
produced by editing a row — which four tasks' tests now do, each saying
so. Staleness was deliberately built first, and id-first resolution is
what makes such an operation addable without breaking every saved view in
a game. Two constraints on whoever builds it, both discovered here: it
may move a type's key freely, but it **may not** tidy `view_refs.ref_key`
to the new spelling while the stored documents keep the old one — that is
exactly the disagreement a torn index reads as, and every renamed view
would then report its type missing. The two spellings move together or
neither moves.
